Similarity surfaces, connections, and the measurable Riemann mapping theorem
Arnaud Chéritat, Guillaume Tahar

Abstract
. This article studies a particular process that approximates solutions of the Beltrami equation (straightening of ellipse fields, a.k.a. measurable Riemann mapping theorem) on ℂ {\mathbb{C}} . It passes through the introduction of a sequence of similarity surfaces constructed by gluing polygons, and we explain the relation between their conformal uniformization and the Schwarz-Christoffel formula. Numerical aspects, in particular the efficiency of the process, are not studied, but we draw interesting theoretical consequences. First, we give an independent proof of the analytic dependence, on the data (the Beltrami form), of the solution of the Beltrami equation (Ahlfors-Bers theorem). For this we prove, without using the Ahlfors-Bers theorem, the holomorphic dependence, with respect to the polygons, of the Christoffel symbol appearing in the Schwarz-Christoffel formula. Second, these Christoffel symbols define a sequence of parallel transports on the range, and in the case of a data that is C 2 C^{2} with compact support, we prove that it converges to the parallel transport associated to a particular affine connection, which we identify.



Introduction

More than fifty years later, Chapter II of
[
Ahl66
]
remains an excellent introduction to the theory of quasiconformal mappings and we highly recommend it.
1
1

1

Or the second edition
[
Ahl06
]
, which was issued in 2006.

This book also includes in Chapter V a proof of the measurable Riemann mapping theorem (see
here for a statement). This proof makes use of integral operators with singular kernels (Ahlfors-Beurling operator), a good dose of
L^{p}
spaces for
p>2
, letting
p\longrightarrow 2
, and a subtle inequality due to Calderón and Zygmund.
There has been prior and posterior proofs with different approaches in special cases or in the general case, depending on the data
\mu
in the statement (the article
[
Glu08
]
gives a short historical overview and references):

-

Gauss (1825) for an
{\mathbb{R}}
-analytic
\mu
, by complexifying
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
into
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
and using a clever trick that does not extend to the
C^{\infty}
class;

-

Korn (1914) and Lichtenstein (1916) for Hölder-continuous
\mu
; Lichtenstein’s method already involves integral operators. Korn uses a fixed point method involving solving the Laplacian;

-

Lavrentiev (1935) for a continuous
\mu
, by constructing approximations to the solution using conformal geometric methods (we sum-up the method in
)
[
Lav35
,
Lav20
]
;

-

Morrey (1936) for a general, measurable,
\mu
, using a density argument to reduce to solving the case where
\mu
is analytic;

to cite only a few.
According to
[
Boj58
]
(translated in
[
Boj09
]
), the idea to introduce the Ahlfors-Beurling operator (it did not bear that name at that time) for this problem is due to Vekua
[
Vek55
]
.
Concerning the holomorphic dependence on the data
\mu
, all the proofs that the authors of the present article know use the Ahlfors-Beurling operator. These works include:

-

Ahlfors and Bers, via
L^{p}
spaces

-

Buff-Douady, via
L^{2}
spaces only and the Fourier transformation

-

Glutsyuk, via a limit of a version for the torus, which is proved using Fourier series.

in the present article is an addition to this list, with the difference that we do not use the Ahlfors-Beurling operator.
Our proof uses distributions and
L^{2}
spaces for the definition of quasiconformal maps, a classical compactness argument via an
L^{2}
estimate, a new ingredient that is similarity surfaces, and the Poincaré-Koebe theorem.
2
2

2

… Of which there are several proofs that do not use Beltrami forms. See for instance Chapter 10 of
[
Ahl10
]
, or
[
Hub06
]
.
. It bears resemblance with the approach of Lavrentiev and in fact the article of Lavrentiev avoids the use of the Poincaré-Koebe theorem so it is not impossible that the proof we present may be adapted too to avoid this use, but this seems not obvious.

Structure and content of the article

is an introduction to the subject of similarity surfaces with a focus on ones that are conformally equivalent to punctured Riemann spheres and obtained by gluing finitely many convex polygons, possibly unbounded (
Section

2
).
In
Section

3
we draw a link with a generalization of the Schwarz-Christoffel formula: it expresses straightening charts. We believe this fact was known before but we do not have references for this.
In the formula a particular rational map appears, which is the Christoffel symbol (expression in a chart) of a particular conformal connection.

Section

4
is devoted to a proof of holomorphic dependence of the rational map appearing as a Christoffel symbol, when the polygons that are glued are modified by letting the vertices vary holomorphically.
A key point is to completely avoid using the measurable Riemann mapping theorem in this proof. Such an insistance induces complications that are dealt with in
Section

4.4
.

states the measurable Riemann mapping theorem (solution of the Beltrami equation associated to a Beltrami form
\mu
) and details our proof. A crucial point is the holomorphic dependence, which relies on the holomorphic dependence of the Christoffel symbol proved in
.
We first describe an (already known) density method with some amount of generality.
Then we apply the results of the previous section to finalize the proof.

The density method does not involve the Ahlfors-Beurling operator nor
L^{p}
spaces beyond
L^{1}
,
L^{2}
.
It involves the notion of distribution and the Sobolev space
W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}
, which serve in particular for the definition of quasiconformal maps.
It also involves compactness statements for quasiconformal maps: one about normal families, which is proved in
[
Ahl66
]
by conformal geometry techniques; one about
L^{2}
bounds, which is proved in
[
Ahl66
]
by proving differentiability almost everywhere and bounding the differential with the Jacobian.

The presentation of our proof is not completely self contained: part of the book of Ahlfors,
[
Ahl66
,
Ahl06
]
, is considered as a basic reference.
In particular several lemmas of this book are used here without proof, the interested reader will have to check such proofs there.
3
3

3

None of these proofs uses the Ahlfors-Beurling integral operator.

Details about others aspects, proofs, or prerequisite knowledge are given in appendices here.

While
presents an original approach to known results,
is prospective and contains new results.
In
, the solution
f
of the Beltrami equation in the measurable Riemann mapping theorem is obtained as a limit of approximations
f_{n}
.
Each approximation is associated to a particular abstract similarity surface
\cal S_{n}
, with
(2n^{2}+1)^{2}+1
singularities.
Uniformizing the underlying Riemann surfaces to the Riemann sphere gives rise to a meromorphic conformal connection on
{\mathbb{C}}
, whose associated Christoffel symbol
\zeta_{n}
is a rational map with
(2n^{2}+1)^{2}
poles.
A natural question arises: is there a limit to these similarity surfaces?
In the particular case where the Beltrami form
\mu
is
C^{2}
with compact support, we state and prove limit theorems concerning the sequence of connections
\zeta_{n}
.
In particular, it tends in some sense to a conformal connection, but whose Christoffel symbol
\zeta
, which we characterize, is not anymore a holomorphic function.
In some sense, we gave an answer to the above question: the limit of the similarity surfaces
\cal S_{n}
is the plane endowed with the connection
\zeta
.

2.
Polygons

Gluing bounded convex polygons

Assume we are given a finite collection
(P_{j})_{j\in J}
of bounded convex polygons. Each polygon must have finitely many sides (aka. edges) and their vertices are the end of these sides. As subsets of
{\mathbb{C}}
, we will choose that polygons include their sides, i.e. they are closed for the topology of
{\mathbb{C}}
.
We allow flat angles (
\pi
radians) at a vertex, so keep in mind that the data of the polygon as a subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
does not necessarily characterize the set of its vertices.
Each edge is naturally oriented by following the polygon’s boundary anticlockwise.
See
Figures

1
and
2
.

Consider the collection of all the sides
e
with their polygons
P_{j}
and denote them
e{\,:\,}j
. Assume we are given a pairing
8
8

8

A partition into sets of cardinality
2
.
of them.
From this, one can define a compact oriented topological manifold
\cal S
of dimension
2
, as follows: for each pair, choose an ordering of the pair as
e_{1}{\,:\,}j_{1},e_{2}{\,:\,}j_{2}
; note that the case
j_{1}=j_{2}
is allowed by the pairing, but not the case
e_{1}{\,:\,}j_{1}=e_{2}{\,:\,}j_{2}
; there is a unique complex affine map
s
sending
e_{1}
to
e_{2}
and reversing their orientations.
Let
\cal P
be the disjoint union of all polygons, whose elements will be denoted
z{\,:\,}j
to distinguish two elements of differently indexed polygons with the same affix, which may happen.
Let
\cal S
be
\cal P
quotiented by
z{\,:\,}j_{1}\sim s(z){\,:\,}j_{2}
for all the paired edges as above. Call
\pi:\cal P\to\cal S
the quotient map.
9
9

9

We use the same notation for Archimedes’ constant, context should prevent confusion.

Interior points of polygons are not identified with any other points. An interior point
z:j
of an edge is identified with exactly one other point
s(z):j^{\prime}
, which sits in the interior of the matching edge.
Depending on the situation, a vertex can be matched with any finite number of other vertices.

Local model at vertices

Let us look at what the quotient looks like at a vertex
\pi(v_{0}:j_{0})
.
Consider all the
v:j
such that
\pi(v:j)=\pi(v_{0}:j_{0})
: these are the vertices of the
P_{j}
that project to the same point. (Note that it is quite possible that a
P_{j}
has several vertices with the same image by
\pi
.)
Near these vertices the polygon
P_{j}
looks like a sector with some opening angle and the point
v
belongs to two edges, one termed “before” and the other one ”after” so that the sequence before
\ \rightarrow\
sector
\ \rightarrow\
after follows the anticlockwise order.
Consider the data
(v,e,j)
, that we will call
flag
, where
e
is the edge that is termed “before”.
Denote
e^{\prime}
the edge termed “after”: it is glued via some affine map
s
to some edge of some other polygon
P_{j^{\prime}}
and we call
successor
of
(v,e,j)
the flag
(s(v),s(e^{\prime}),j^{\prime})
.
Following the consecutive gluings, we get from flag to flag and eventually back to the initial flag since there are finitely many polygons.
By explicit transformations, we can map homeomorphically each sector to sectors of the same opening with apex of affix
0
and so that the gluings, except maybe one, become the identity. This shows that the quotient is indeed a manifold at the vertices.

Removing vertices and getting a similarity surface

Let
\cal V\subset\cal S
denote the set of all vertices (after passing to the quotient).
On
\cal S^{\prime}=\cal S-\cal V
one can define an atlas of a similarity surface as follows: inside each polygon
\pi(\operatorname{Int}P_{j})
, take
\pi^{-1}
as a chart.
Near an edge
e=\pi(e_{1}{\,:\,}j_{1})=\pi(e_{2}{\,:\,}j_{2})
with endpoints removed (call this
\operatorname{Int}e
), we consider two cases.
If
P_{1}\neq P_{2}
then take the neighbourhood
V=\pi(\operatorname{Int}P_{1})\cup\operatorname{Int}e\cup\pi(\operatorname{Int}P_{2})
and the chart
\phi:V\to s(\operatorname{Int}P_{1})\cup\operatorname{Int}e_{2}\cup\operatorname{Int}P_{2}
mapping
\pi(z:P_{1})
to
s(z)
and
\pi(z:P_{2})
to
z
(this coincides in
\operatorname{Int}e
).
If
P_{1}=P_{2}
we do the same but using only non-overlapping neighbourhoods of
\operatorname{Int}e_{1}
and
\operatorname{Int}e_{2}
in
P_{1}
instead of the full polygon.
In both cases, the sets
s(\operatorname{Int}P_{1})
and
\operatorname{Int}P_{2}
cannot overlap because we took convex polygons and
s
reverses the orientation of edges.

Let us prove that we are in the first case by completing the Riemann surface
\cal S^{\prime}

Let

be a vertex.
Consider the circularly ordered sequence of flags
(v,e,j)
associated to
p
as described earlier in this section, and their associated angular sectors.
Let
\theta>0
denote the sum of the angle of the sectors.
When we pass from a flag
(v,e,j)
to its successor
(s(v),s(e^{\prime}),j^{\prime})
, there is an affine map
s(z)=az+b
performing the transition. Let
\lambda
denote the
inverse
of the product of the dilation factors
|a|
. This product is
not

.
The monodromy
11
11

11

Defined at the end of
.
of the similarity surface for an anticlockwise loop winding once around the vertex is equal to

z\mapsto{\lambda}{e^{i\theta}}z.

Remark
.

It is easy to get confused and believe that the monodromy would be the inverse of this function.
If we scale an rotate the different sectors to attach them successively, we get a big sector (living in the universal cover of
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
if
\theta>2\pi
) of which there remains only one pair of sides to glue together by some similitude.
Call them “before” and after so that what we successively meet in the anticlockwise order is “before”, sector, “after”.
Then the monodromy is the similitude that sends “before” to “after”, not the other way round.

Figure

4
illustrates an example and also illustrates the construction of a Riemann chart that we now explain.
Map each sector to a horizontal band, infinite on the left, by a branch of
z\mapsto\log(z-v)
. The gluing map from a band to its successor is then a translation.
Let us move them around in
{\mathbb{C}}
and stack them according to these gluings.
We obtain a band
B
of height
\theta
, on which the last gluing goes from the top line to the bottom line and is the translation by the complex vector
u=\log(\lambda)+i\theta
.
Now, for a well-chosen complex number
\alpha
, the similarity
z\mapsto\alpha z
send this vector to
2\pi i
and the band to a band that is not necessarily horizontal any more, yet is still infinite on the left, and whose two sides are glued by the translation by
2\pi i
. A uniformizing map for this quotient is just the exponential map
\exp
from
\alpha B
to a neighborhood
U
of
0
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
.
(If we would have sent
u
to
-2\pi i
instead of
2\pi i
, the band would be infinite towards the right, and its exponential would be a neighbourhood of infinity.)
Adding
\{0\}
to
U
we get an analytic chart near the vertex
p
. We have turned
\cal S
into a compact Riemann surface.

Unbounded polygons

c
f
a
a

Figure 5.
Example of an unbounded polygon with the two unbounded sides glued together by a similitude whose center
c
is different from the focus
f
of the unbounded sector.

We will need to include unbounded polygons
P
into the game. As above, we start by including only convex ones.
They are assumed closed as subsets of
\hat{{\mathbb{C}}}
, i.e. they include the vertex
\infty
.
We call
finite
vertex a vertex that is in
{\mathbb{C}}
, i.e. different from
\infty
.
We only consider unbounded polygons that have exactly two edges reaching infinity, we call them
unbounded
edges, and require that these edges are half-lines, i.e. that each reach to a finite vertex, possibly the same. Our requirement rules out in particular the case where an edge is a whole straight line, but still allows the two unbounded edges to have a union that is a whole straight line (iff. they end at the same finite vertex and make there an angle of
\pi
).

This assumption implies that there are at least two edges, so rules out the case
P={\mathbb{C}}
, which has no edge, and also rules out a half plane with no finite vertex.
It also rules out the case where the complement of
P
would have more than one components.
Such a polygon would be bounded by two parallel lines and there would either be at least an edge from infinity to itself or four edges reaching infinity. However, we can recover all these cases by pasting appropriate polygons satisfying our assumptions (see also the last paragraph of the present section).

With these conditions each unbounded polygon has only one infinite vertex and each unbounded edge is a half line. We now consider the previous constructions, but allowing the presence of unbounded polygons that satisfy the conditions. We have to assume that the pairing between edges satisfies that unbounded edges are paired with unbounded edges.
Moreover, the orientation reversing
{\mathbb{C}}
-affine map sending an unbounded
e_{1}:j_{1}
to an unbounded
e_{2}:j_{2}
exists but is not anymore unique.
So the data that must be given prior the construction has to include the choice of this map for each such pair (for a visual way to do this, one can mark unbounded edges with supplementary non-vertex points, that must match under the gluings, as in
Section

4.4
).

Then the construction can be carried out and we still get a topological surface
\cal S
, but the set of vertices
\cal V\subset\cal S
now includes a new kind of element:
infinite
vertices.
These are the vertices which come from the vertex at infinity of an unbounded polygon.

Again something new may happen: conformally,
infinite vertices are not anymore necessarily punctures
. An example is given in
Section

4.4
.
To the infinite vertex of an unbounded convex polygon satisfying our restrictions, one can associate the angle between the two edges reaching to infinity.
It is a non-negative number.
In the example of
Section

4.4
this angle is
0
.

To a infinite vertex
v
of the quotient let us again associate the sum of the angles at infinity of the unbounded polygons whose infinite vertex projects to
v
. Then a sufficient condition for an infinite vertex to be a puncture is that this sum is positive.
The uniformizing map is obtained in a similar way as for finite vertices, with a few complications. We will provide indications but leave details to the reader.

An infinite (closed) sector is of the form
a+[R,+\infty)\cdot e^{i[\theta_{1},\theta_{2}]}
for some
R\geq 0
and some
a\in{\mathbb{C}}
that we will call its
focus
. An half infinite strip is the image of the set
[0,1]\times[0,+\infty)\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}
by a complex affine map.
Here is a list of complications. The angle of an unbounded polygon could be
0
. The total angle at an infinite vertex could be
0
.
The focuses of the infinite sectors do not necessarily coincide with the finite vertices of the unbounded edges.
The focuses of the infinite sectors do not necessarily match under the gluing maps of these edges.
Taking a logarithm based on a focus would not conjugate the gluing (a similarity) to a map as simple as a translation (and if the two finite vertices of the two unbounded edges do not coincide, we of course cannot base the log on both).

Instead we take subsets of the sectors and strips that are neighbourhoods of infinity and glue them in a universal cover
U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
of
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
into one big set sitting in finitely many sheets of the cover (one sheet is enough near
\infty
if
\theta<2\pi
). A neighbourhood of infinity in this set is a sector or a strip. Its two sides must be glued together according to some
{\mathbb{C}}
-affine map (the vertices are now excluded from of the picture, except the one at infinity) whose factor is the monodromy factor (or its inverse, depending on the which side is the domain and which side is the range).
If we order the two sides of the infinite sector/strip in the clockwise order (this is the opposite of the ordering we chose for finite vertices) then the monodromy sends the first one to the second one (as in the finite case).
The monodromy factor must be of the form

\lambda e^{-i\theta}

for some
\lambda>0
. Now there are several cases

(1)

\theta>0
.

(a)

Either the affine map
s
has a unique fixed point.
Then we can take a logarithm based on this fixed point.
The image of the two sides will be almost horizontal curves
13
13

13

Because we work close to infinity. If bothered by that, one can make these curves actual horizontals by shifting the original edges, which can, in a neighbourhood of infinity, be achieved by cutting and pasting pieces of the sectors.
that extend infinitely to the right and that we must glue by a translation of vector
v=\log\lambda-i\theta
.
Multiplication by
2\pi i/v
followed by
\exp
realizes this gluing.

(b)

Or the affine map
s
is the identity.
Then the total angle is a multiple of
2\pi
, and the two curves to be glued together have the same projection by
\phi:U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
.
The quotient is a
k
-fold cover of a punctured neighborhood of infinity.
A uniformization is given by a determination of
z\mapsto z^{-1/k}
.

(c)

Or the affine map
s
is a translation by a non-zero vector
a
.
In this case
\lambda=1
, and
\theta
must be a multiple of
2\pi
:

\theta=k2\pi.

By composing everything with
z\mapsto a^{-1}z
we can assume that
a=1
.
By cutting and pasting we can assume that the edges to glue have their infinite direction of argument
0
.
We provide explicitly a formula
whose inverse
realizes the gluing:

\phi(z)=z^{-k}+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\log z

near infinity, where one takes the branch of
\log z
whose imaginary part lies in
[0,2\pi]
. We omit the details.

(2)

\theta=0
.

(a)

Either the gluing map is a translation, which can be taken as
z\mapsto z+2\pi i
by an appropriate
{\mathbb{C}}
-affine change of coordinate. Then a uniformization is provided by
z\mapsto\exp(z)
.

(b)

Or the gluing map is of the form
z\mapsto az+b
for some
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
with
a>0
and
a\neq 0
. Then the vertex at infinity is not a puncture but a hole.

Signed angle and monodromy factor

To unify the formulas for the monodromy we define the
signed angle

\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}
of a vertex as
\sigma=\theta
if the vertex is finite and
\sigma=-\theta
if the vertex is infinite, where
\theta\geq 0
is the total angle defined in the previous paragraphs. Then the factor of the monodromy of a small loop winding anticlockwise around the singularity is in both cases of the form

\lambda e^{i\sigma}

for some
\lambda>0
. Note that the monodromy factor determines
\lambda
completely but only determines
\sigma
modulo
2\pi i
.

Even fancier polygons

In fact we can use polygons that are non-convex, or not simply connected, and without restrictions on the type of unbounded edges or their number, still finite.
Indeed such polygons can be cut further, possibly introducing auxiliary finite vertices (in which case, obviously, the resulting similarity surface structure will have an erasable singularity at these vertices), so as to respect the previous restrictions and yield the same objects
\cal S
and
\cal S^{\prime}
once we give back to the latter the auxiliary vertices.
Then one thing will have to be kept in mind: that if a polygon
P\subset{\mathbb{C}}
has
n
unbounded complementary components in
{\mathbb{C}}
, then it must be considered as having
n
distinct infinite vertices (before the gluing; after, this number may get reduced).

One could also use polygons that overlap themselves, i.e. are spread over
{\mathbb{C}}
in an non-injective way, but this section is already long enough.

3.
The Schwarz-Christoffel formula

3.1.
Christoffel symbol

Consider any similarity surface
\cal S^{\prime}
. Recall that it is in particular a Riemann surface. Assume that we are given a similarity chart
s:U\to{\mathbb{C}}
and a Riemann chart
r:U\to{\mathbb{C}}
on the same
14
14

14

We can always restrict a chart to an open subset of its domain and add it to the atlas, this gives the same structure on
\cal S^{\prime}
.
open subset
U\subset\cal S^{\prime}
.
Consider the map
\phi=s\circ r^{-1}
: it expresses a similarity chart in a Riemann chart.
Note that
\phi
is holomorphic, by definition of a Riemann surface atlas.
Now if we are given two similarity charts
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
on
U
and if
U
is connected then by definition of a similarity atlas, there must exist
a\in{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
and
b\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that
s_{2}=a\,s_{1}+b
.
(In fact the definition ensures the existence of
a
and
b
locally, and analytic continuation ensures they are constant on the connected set
U
.)
It follows that

\phi_{2}=a\,\phi_{1}+b

hence

\phi_{2}^{\prime}=a\,\phi_{1}^{\prime}

thus

\frac{\phi^{\prime\prime}_{1}}{\phi^{\prime}_{1}}=\frac{\phi^{\prime\prime}_{2}}{\phi^{\prime}_{2}}.

For any holomorphic function on an open subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
we now use the notation

N\phi=N(\phi):=\frac{\phi^{\prime\prime}}{\phi^{\prime}}.

Assume we are given a subset
U
of
\cal S^{\prime}
.
Note that for every point
M\in U
there are similarity charts defined in a neighbourhood of
M
but there may fail to exist a similarity chart that would be defined on the entirety of
U
, even if there is a Riemann chart
r
defined on
U
.

If we are given a Riemann chart
r:U\subset\cal S^{\prime}\to{\mathbb{C}}
, we can use it to endow
r(U)
with the similarity atlas
\{\phi=s\circ r^{-1}\}
where
s
varies in the similarity atlas of
\cal S^{\prime}
.
Note that if we denote
V
the domain of
s
then the domain of
\phi
is
r(U\cap V)
.

We will thus momentarily study open subsets
O
of
{\mathbb{C}}
with a similarity surface atlas whose charts
\phi
are holomorphic.
By the above computation, all similarity charts have the same quantity
N(\phi)
at a given
z\in O
, so even if there may fail to be a single chart
\phi
on
O
, there is a well-defined holomorphic function
\zeta:O\to{\mathbb{C}}
such that for all similarity charts
s:V\subset O\to{\mathbb{C}}
, then

N\phi=\zeta

on
V
.
It is easy to recover
\phi
from
\zeta
, as the equation
\phi^{\prime\prime}/\phi^{\prime}=\zeta
is equivalent to
\log\phi^{\prime}=\int\zeta
(locally), so

\phi=\int\exp\left(\int\zeta\right)

holds locally.
The integration constants make
\phi
known locally only up to post-composition by
{\mathbb{C}}
-affine maps, which is coherent with the fact that we have a similarity atlas:

\phi=b+\int\exp\left(c+\int\zeta\right)=b+a\int\exp\left(\int\zeta\right)
with
a=e^{c}
.
Note also that, for a loop
\gamma
contained in a single chart
O
the monodromy factor of a loop will be equal to the exponential of the integral of
\zeta
along this loop:

\lambda=\exp\left(\int_{\gamma}\zeta(z)dz\right).

Let us come back to abstract similarity surfaces
\cal S^{\prime}
. Up to now we only expressed similarity charts in
one
Riemann chart, but what happens if we change the Riemann chart?
Assume
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are both defined on
U\subset\cal S^{\prime}
and let
\psi=r_{1}\circ r_{2}^{-1}
.
Then the expressions
\phi^{(i)}=s\circ r_{i}^{-1}
of a similarity chart
s
via the maps
r_{i}
are related by

\phi^{(2)}=\phi^{(1)}\circ\psi
.
It is easy, for holomorphic functions, to compute
N(\phi\circ\psi)
:

(1)

N(\phi\circ\psi)=\psi^{\prime}\times N(\phi)\circ\psi+N(\psi).

For our situation let us write this as

(2)

\zeta^{(2)}=\psi^{\prime}\times\zeta^{(1)}\circ\psi+\frac{\psi^{\prime\prime}}{\psi^{\prime}}.

where
\zeta^{(i)}
is the function of the previous paragraph, for the similarity atlas
\phi^{(i)}
on the subset
r_{i}(U)
of
{\mathbb{C}}
.

Remark
.

Equation

1
is reminiscent of the formula of a change of variable for a
1
-form.
In fact
N\phi
does not express a
1
-form on the Riemann surface
\cal S^{\prime}
but a
Christoffel symbol
in complex dimension one.
Christoffel symbols are the coefficients that appear in the infinitesimal expression of affine connections in charts, see
.

Equation

1
is also reminiscent of a similar formula for the
Schwarzian derivative

S\phi
. In fact if we look at surfaces with atlases whose change of charts are homographies instead of affine maps, the operator
S
will play the role of the operator
N
.

If we have an isolated point in
{\mathbb{C}}-U
, then by
eq.

2
the polar part of
\zeta
behaves under a change of variable exactly like the polar part of a holomorphic
1
-form. In particular the residue “
\operatorname{res}
” is an invariant. This is coherent with the fact that the monodromy of the similarity surface around this singularity is independent of any choice of Riemann surface charts and is a conjugacy class of affine map whose linear factor is equal to
\exp(2\pi i\operatorname{res})
.

3.2.
Polygon gluings and residue of the Christoffel symbol near puncture type singularities

We place ourselves here in the situation of
Section

2
where a topological surface
\cal S
and a similarity surface
\cal S^{\prime}=\cal S-\cal V
were constructed by gluing bounded or unbounded polygons, where
\cal V
is the set of vertices, and we make the assumption that all infinite vertices have an angle sum
\theta
that is non-zero.
We saw that in this case that the atlas associated to
\cal S^{\prime}
extends to a Riemann surface atlas of
\cal S
.
Let us study more closely the Christoffel symbol near the vertices.

For any finite vertex we constructed a Riemann chart whose image is a neighborhood of
0
and in which the similarity charts are branches of
z\mapsto z^{\alpha}
for the complex number
\alpha=(\log\lambda+i\theta)/2\pi i
where
\theta
is the sum of the angles of the polygons at this vertex and
\lambda
is such that the monodromy factor at the vertex is
\lambda e^{i\theta}
.
For any branch of
z\mapsto z^{\alpha}
, a direct computation gives

Nz^{\alpha}=\frac{\alpha-1}{z}.

It follows (see
eq.

2
and the consequence on the polar part explained after it) that in any other Riemann chart, the Christoffel symbol
\zeta
will have a simple pole at the vertex, of residue

(3)

\operatorname{res}=\alpha-1=\frac{\log(\lambda)+i\theta}{2\pi i}-1.

In particular

{\operatorname{Re}\,}\operatorname{res}>-1.

A similar construction was done for infinite vertices for which
\theta>0
.
The conclusion was similar: the monodromy has factor
\lambda e^{-i\theta}
for some
\lambda>0
and there is a Riemann chart mapping a neighbourhood of this vertex to a neighbourhood of
0
and for which similarity charts are given by branches of
z\mapsto z^{\alpha}
with
\alpha=(\log\lambda-i\theta)/2\pi i
or, if the monodromy is a non-identity translation (hence
\lambda=1
and
\theta=k2\pi
), by branches of
z\mapsto z^{-k}+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\log z
.
The Christoffel symbol has in both cases a
simple pole
at the vertex, of residue

(4)

\operatorname{res}=\alpha-1=\frac{\log(\lambda)-i\theta}{2\pi i}-1

in this chart and thus in any other Riemann chart.
In particular

{\operatorname{Re}\,}\operatorname{res}<-1.

Remark
.

The notion of signed angle
\sigma
in
Section

2
allows to unify
Equations

3
and
4
: in both cases

\operatorname{res}=\frac{\log(\lambda)+i\sigma}{2\pi i}-1.

The monodromy factor
\lambda e^{i\sigma}
has a unified expression in terms of
\operatorname{res}
:

\lambda e^{i\sigma}=\exp(2\pi i\operatorname{res}).

3.3.
Polygons gluing to a sphere and the Schwarz-Christoffel formula

We now make the supplementary assumption that the topological surface
\cal S
is homeomorphic to a sphere.
(We still place ourselves in the situation of
Section

3.2
, which we recall:
\cal S
is obtained by gluing together finitely many bounded or unbounded polygons; all infinite vertices are moreover required to have an angle sum
\theta
that is non-zero, so that all singularities are punctures and the Riemann surface atlas of
\cal S^{\prime}
extends to
\cal S
.)
Then by the
Poincaré Koebe
theorem,
\cal S
is isomorphic as a Riemann surface to the Riemann sphere
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
. Let
F:\cal S\to\hat{\mathbb{C}}
be such an isomorphism.
Let
\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{m}\}\subset\hat{\mathbb{C}}
be the image by
F
of the vertices.
The point
\infty\in\hat{\mathbb{C}}
may or may not be one of them.
For each vertex
v_{k}
, let
\theta_{k}
be the total angle,
\sigma_{k}=\pm\theta_{k}
the signed angle, and
\lambda_{k}
the dilation ratio of this vertex.
Note that
F
gives us a
global chart
of the Riemann surface
\cal S-F^{-1}(\infty)
. Removing vertices,
F
is a global Riemann chart for
\cal S^{\prime}-F^{-1}(\infty)
.
The associated Christoffel symbol
\zeta
is a holomorphic function from
{\mathbb{C}}-\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{m}\}
to
{\mathbb{C}}
.

Let
\operatorname{res_{k}}
denote the residue of
\zeta
at the vertex
z_{k}
.

Theorem 1
.

We have

\zeta(z)=\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k=1}}{{z_{k}\neq\infty}}}^{m}\frac{\operatorname{res}_{k}}{z-z_{k}}.

Moreover

\sum_{k=1}^{m}\operatorname{res}_{k}=-2.

Proof.

By the above discussion,
\zeta
has at most a simple pole at each vertex
z_{k}\neq\infty
and we know the corresponding residue
\operatorname{res}_{k}
.
To analyse what happens at infinity we use the change of variable
w=\psi(z)=1/z
, call
\tilde{\zeta}
the expression of the Christoffel symbol in the coordinate
w
and use
Equation

2
:

\zeta(z)=-z^{-2}\tilde{\zeta}(z^{-1})-2z^{-1}.

If
\infty
is not a vertex then
\zeta(z)=-2z^{-1}+\cal O(z^{-2})
as
z\to\infty
. If
\infty
is a vertex
z_{k}
then
\tilde{\zeta}(w)=\frac{\operatorname{res}_{k}}{w}+\cal O(1)
as
w\to 0
, from which:

\zeta(z)=(-\operatorname{res}_{k}-2)z^{-1}+\cal O(z^{-2})

as
z\to\infty
. In all cases:
\zeta
tends
0
to at
\infty
.

Let
f(z)
denote the sum of
\operatorname{res}_{k}/(z-z_{k})
as in the statement of the theorem.
The difference
\zeta-f
is a holomorphic function on
{\mathbb{C}}
minus finitely many points, whose singularities are erasable and which tends to
0
at infinity.
It follows that
\zeta-f=0
.
This proves the first claim.
Moreover, from the above it follows that
\zeta(z)-f(z)=\left(-2-\sum\operatorname{res}_{k}\right)z^{-1}+\cal O(z^{-2})
as
z\to\infty
, whence the second claim.
∎

Remark
.

That the sum of residues is
-2
(in the case of
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
) and not
0
is another difference between the Christoffel symbol and
1
-forms.
It can be proved more generally for all Christoffel symbol on
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
with finitely many singularities (polar or essential) by using
Equation

2
as above and expressing
\int\zeta(z)dz
on a big circle in two ways, using the residues on each side.
Another (equivalent) way is to use that the difference of two Christoffel symbols is a
1
-form and to use that the sum of residues of a
1
-form is always
0
to reduce the computation to a particular symbol (for instance
\zeta=0
).
Last, let us remark that this formula generalizes to genus
g
compact Riemann surfaces: the sum of the residues is then equal to
2g-2
.

From
Theorem

1
, the similarity charts
\phi
satisfy locally that

\phi=a+b\int\prod_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k=1}}{{z_{k}\neq\infty}}}^{m}\left(z-z_{k}\right)^{\operatorname{res}_{k}}.

Remark
.

This is a generalization of the
Schwarz-Christoffel formula
. See
[
Ché08
]
for more details.

Example

b

. Left: the edges with the same label are glued together. Right: image under a uniformization.

we illustrate the following example: one polygon is an equilateral triangle and the other is a isocele rectangle triangle.
The three sides of the first are glued to the three sides of the second as on the figure on the left, so that the quotient is homeomorphic to the sphere.
It has three vertices
A
,
B
and
C
, three edges and of course two polygons.
We get the following, where
\theta
is the total angle and
\lambda
the monodromy factor (using
\tau=2\pi
on the left to clarify the computations):

\displaystyle A:

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle=\frac{\tau}{6}+\frac{\tau}{8}=\frac{7}{24}\tau

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}

\displaystyle\operatorname{res}

\displaystyle=\frac{7}{24}-1+\frac{\log\sqrt{2}}{2\pi i}

\displaystyle B:

\displaystyle=\frac{\tau}{6}+\frac{\tau}{8}=\frac{7}{24}\tau

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle=1/\sqrt{2}

\displaystyle\operatorname{res}

\displaystyle=\frac{7}{24}-1-\frac{\log\sqrt{2}}{2\pi i}

\displaystyle C:

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle=\frac{\tau}{6}+\frac{\tau}{4}=\frac{5}{12}\tau

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\operatorname{res}

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{12}-1

In the global chart
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
where
C
is at
\infty
,
A
at
-1
and
B
at
1
we get

\zeta=\frac{\operatorname{res}_{A}}{z+1}+\frac{\operatorname{res}_{B}}{z-1}.

On
Figure

6
we see on the left the two triangles with two different hatchings (think of them as a kind of coordinate systems); on the right the corresponding Riemann surface
\cal S^{0}
has been mapped to
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
as above and we drew the image of the hatchings using the methods of
[

]
, Section 4.3.2 to follow the geodesics associated to
\zeta
.

3.5.
Any Christoffel symbol

Given an open subset
U
of
{\mathbb{C}}
and a holomorphic function
\zeta:U\to{\mathbb{C}}
, we can define an atlas of similarity surface whose Christoffel symbol is
\zeta
, by solving locally
N\phi=\zeta
.
Assume that
\zeta
has an isolated singularity
z_{0}\in{\mathbb{C}}
which is a simple pole of residue
\operatorname{res}
.

Lemma 1
.

Under the conditions above, if
\operatorname{res}\notin\{-1,-2,\ldots\}
then there is a holomorphic change of coordinate
z\mapsto w
sending
z_{0}\to 0
such that similarity charts are expressed as branches of
w\mapsto w^{\operatorname{res}+1}
, up to affine maps, and hence the Christoffel symbol takes the expression

\tilde{\zeta}(w)=\frac{\operatorname{res}}{w}.

Proof.

By a translation we can assume that
z_{0}=0
.
We have the power series expansion as
z\to 0
:

\zeta(z)=\frac{\operatorname{res}}{z}+a_{0}+a_{1}z+a_{2}z^{2}+\ldots

from which one antiderivative satisfies

\int\zeta=\operatorname{res}\times\log z+o(1)

hence

\exp\left(\int\zeta\right)=z^{\operatorname{res}}\exp(o(1))=\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}b_{n}z^{\operatorname{res}+n}

for some sequence
b_{n}\in{\mathbb{C}}
with
b_{0}=1
.
Since
z^{\operatorname{res}+n}
has several values, we need to specify a meaning:
we temporarily work in the universal cover of
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, let
\log z
be a global branch and define
z^{\operatorname{res}+n}=\exp((\operatorname{res}+n)\log z)=\exp(\operatorname{res}\times\log z)z^{n}
.
This generalized power series expansion has complex exponents but positive radius of convergence and we can integrate term by term to get

\int\exp\left(\int\zeta\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}b_{n}\frac{z^{\operatorname{res}+n+1}}{\operatorname{res}+n+1}=z^{\operatorname{res}+1}\exp(R(z))

for some holomorphic function
R
of
z
(i.e. without singularity at
0
).
Then

w=z\exp\frac{R(z)}{\operatorname{res}+1}

does the job.
∎

Note that we did not claim local uniqueness of the coordinate
w
that satisfies the conclusion of the lemma. We do not wish to study the uniqueness problem in this article.

Remark
.

Let us state how to deal with the remaining cases (we give them without proofs because we will not use these normal forms).
In the case where
\operatorname{res}=-1
we can also get
\tilde{\zeta}(w)=-1/w
, which has branches of
w\mapsto\log w
as similarity charts up to affine maps.
In the case
\operatorname{res}=-k\in\{-2,-3,\ldots\}
, we can reduce to either
\tilde{\zeta}(w)=-k/w
or to
\tilde{\zeta}(w)=-k/w+w^{k-2}
, and these two cases are mutually exclusive.
The first one has
w\mapsto 1/w^{1-k}
as similarity charts but the similarity charts of the second one has a complicated expression.
We may prefer an alternative form, for example one which gives branches of
w\mapsto\frac{-1}{w^{k-1}}+\log w
as similarity charts up to affine maps, in which case
\displaystyle\tilde{\zeta}(w)=\frac{-k}{w}+\frac{w^{k-2}}{1+\frac{w^{k-1}}{(k-1)}}
.

An isolated singularity of a Christoffel symbol with a simple pole with

{\operatorname{Re}\,}(\operatorname{res})>-1

is called
bounded
.
It is called

unbounded
if

{\operatorname{Re}\,}(\operatorname{res})<-1.

We do not give a particular name to the case
{\operatorname{Re}\,}(\operatorname{res})=-1
.

According to the lemma above, near any
bounded
isolated singularity of a similarity surface, the latter is isomorphic to a bounded sector of apex
0
and opening
\theta=2\pi({\operatorname{Re}\,}(\operatorname{res})+1)
(if
\theta>2\pi
, the sector sits in the universal cover of
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
) endowed with the canonical atlas of
{\mathbb{C}}
, and whose two sides are glued by the similarity
z\mapsto e^{2\pi i\operatorname{res}}z
from
\arg z=\theta_{0}
to
\arg z=\theta_{0}+\theta
.

Near any
unbounded
singularity of
non-integer
residue, this is the same but the sector is a neighbourhood of infinity and has opening
\theta=-2\pi({\operatorname{Re}\,}(\operatorname{res})+1)
, and the similarity is still
z\mapsto e^{2\pi i\operatorname{res}}z
and maps
\arg z=\theta_{0}
to
\arg z=\theta_{0}-\theta
.

We leave to the reader the task to determine local models for the remaining cases if they wish. Such models will not be used here.

A convenient change of variables for the unbounded type singularities

Assume that
0
is a simple pole of
\zeta
of unbounded type (
{\operatorname{Re}\,}\operatorname{res}<-1
) and consider the Laurent series expansion

\zeta(z)=\frac{\operatorname{res}}{z}+a_{0}+a_{1}z+a_{2}z^{2}+\ldots.

Close to
0
the term
\frac{\operatorname{res}}{z}
is dominant, so it is natural to try and compare the affine charts
\phi
of
\zeta
to the affine charts
z\mapsto z^{1+\operatorname{res}}
of the “ideal” Christoffel symbol
\frac{\operatorname{res}}{z}
.
For this we make the change of variable

\tilde{z}=z^{1+\operatorname{res}}

and express
\zeta
in the
u
coordinate.
Note that the correspondence
z\longleftrightarrow\tilde{u}
is a bijection (and usually only well defined) from the universal cover
U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
of
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
to
U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, via

\log\tilde{z}=(1+\operatorname{res})\times\log z

where
\log:U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}\to{\mathbb{C}}
is a well-defined bijection.

Tending to
0

If
\operatorname{res}\in{\mathbb{R}}
then
|z|\longrightarrow 0

\iff

|\tilde{z}|\longrightarrow+\infty
.
But if
\operatorname{res}\notin{\mathbb{R}}
then the set
|z|<r
, where
r>0
, correspond to a set of
\tilde{z}\in U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
whose boundary spirals inward to
0
as
({\operatorname{Im}\,}\operatorname{res})\times(\arg\tilde{z})\to-\infty
and outward to
\infty
as
({\operatorname{Im}\,}\operatorname{res})\times(\arg\tilde{z})\to+\infty
: indeed the set of values of
\log\tilde{z}
is the half plane

(5)

HU(r):=\big\{{\tilde{z}\in U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}}\,\big|\,{{\operatorname{Re}\,}\frac{\log\tilde{z}}{1+\operatorname{res}}<\log r}\big\},

it is delimited by a straight line that is not vertical and this half plane contains a neighbourhood of
+\infty
in
{\mathbb{R}}
, because
{\operatorname{Re}\,}(1+\operatorname{res})<0
.
In any cases, we have
|z|\longrightarrow 0
iff
\tilde{z}
enters and stays in every
HU(r)
.

\tilde{z}
-sectors

Since
\tilde{z}\in U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, there is a well defined function
\arg\tilde{z}
taking values in
{\mathbb{R}}
.
The sector in
\tilde{z}
coordinates defined by
\arg\tilde{z}\in(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
corresponds in
\log u
coordinate to the strip
{\operatorname{Im}\,}\log\tilde{z}\in(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
.
In this strip, the subset for which the corresponding
z
belongs to
B(0,r)
is the intersection with
\log HU(r)
.
In
\tilde{z}
coordinate, this intersection is a subset of the sector with a spiralling neighbourhood of
0
removed, see
Figure

7
.
We call this set

U(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},r)=\big\{{\tilde{z}\in U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}}\,\big|\,{\alpha_{0}<\arg u<\alpha_{1},\ |z|<r}\big\}\subset U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}

where
\arg\tilde{z}={\operatorname{Im}\,}\log\tilde{z}
is well-defined on
U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
and
\tilde{z}\mapsto z
also.
For any given
U=U(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},r)
we have

\log|z|={\operatorname{Re}\,}\left(\frac{1}{1+\operatorname{res}}\right)\log|\tilde{z}|+\cal O(1)

where
\cal O(1)
is quantity bounded over all
U
with a bound that depends continuously on
(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},r)
for
\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}
and
0<r
.
In particular, under the constraint
\tilde{z}\in U(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},r)
, we have
|z|\longrightarrow 0\iff|\tilde{z}|\longrightarrow+\infty
.
Finally, note that
U
contains the sector defined by
\arg\tilde{z}\in(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
and
|\tilde{z}|>R
where
R=R(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},r,\operatorname{res})
can be computed easily.

0
0
B(0,r)
\times(1+\operatorname{res})
\exp
\exp
z^{(1+\operatorname{res})}
U(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},r)
\log HU(r)

Figure 7.
Example of domain
U(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},r)=\big\{{\tilde{z}}\,\big|\,{\arg\tilde{z}\in[-\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}]\text{ and }|z|<r}\big\}
in the case
{\operatorname{Re}\,}\operatorname{res}<-1
. Dots correspond to
\arg z\equiv\pi\bmod 2\pi
, dashes to
\arg\tilde{z}=\alpha_{0}
or
\alpha_{1}
.

Lemma 2
.

Assume that
{\operatorname{Re}\,}\operatorname{res}<-1
.
There is some
r>0
that depends on
\zeta
and such that the following holds.
Let
\phi
be any solution
15
15

15

They exists since
B(0,r)-\{0\}
lifts to a simply connected subset of
U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
on the part of
U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
on which
|z|<r
, of
\phi^{\prime\prime}(z)/\phi^{\prime}(z)=\zeta(z)
. The quantity
\phi
can be interchangeably considered as depending on
z
or
\log z
or
\tilde{z}
, etc.
Then there exists
a\in{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
such that:

(1)

there exists a holomorphic function
z\mapsto r_{1}(z)
on
B(0,r)
such that

\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial\log\tilde{z}}=a\times\tilde{z}\times(1+r_{1}(z))

and
r_{1}(0)=0
;

(2)

for all
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}
with
\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}
, there exists
C,C^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and a holomorphic function
r_{2}:U(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},r)\to{\mathbb{C}}
such that we have,
\forall\tilde{z}\in U(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},r)
,

\phi=a\tilde{z}+r_{2}(\tilde{z})

with

|r_{2}(\tilde{z})|\leq\max\left(|z|\!\times\!|\tilde{z}|,1\right)(C^{\prime}+C\log|\tilde{z}|).

In particular

\phi\underset{|\tilde{z}|\to\infty}{\sim}a\tilde{z}.

Proof.

We restrict
\zeta
to some
B(0,r)
so that

\zeta(z)=\frac{\operatorname{res}}{z}+h(z)

with
h
bounded, say
|h|\leq M
.
Let
v=\log z
and let us express the Christoffel symbol
\hat{\zeta}
in the
v
coordinate: since
z=e^{v}
, formula (
2
) gives

\hat{\zeta}(v)=e^{v}\zeta(e^{v})+1

and using the development of
\zeta
above we get

\hat{\zeta}(v)=1+\operatorname{res}+e^{v}h(e^{v}).

Let
\hat{\phi}(v)=\phi(z)
. Then
\hat{\phi}
is a solution of
\hat{\phi}^{\prime\prime}/\hat{\phi}^{\prime}=\hat{\zeta}
. Integrating on the half plane
{\operatorname{Re}\,}v<\log r
we get that

\log(\hat{\phi}^{\prime}(v))=c_{0}+(1+\operatorname{res})v+g(e^{v})

where
g
is the antiderivative of
h
mapping
0
to
0
and
c_{0}\in{\mathbb{C}}
is a constant.
Hence

\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial\log\tilde{z}}=\frac{1}{1+\operatorname{res}}\hat{\phi}^{\prime}(v)=a\tilde{z}(1+r_{1}(z))

for
a=e^{c_{0}}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
and for the holomorphic bounded function
r_{1}:B(0,r)\to{\mathbb{C}}
mapping
0
to
0
and defined by
r_{1}(z)=\exp(g(z))-1
.
This proves the first point.

We have
|r_{1}(z)|\leq c|z|
for some
c>0
and thus

(6)

\left|\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial\log\tilde{z}}-a\tilde{z}\right|\leq c^{\prime}|\tilde{z}|\times|z|

with
c^{\prime}=c|a|
.
Let us abbreviate
U=U(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},r)
.
The set of
\tilde{z}\in\partial U
such that
|z|=r
is a compact set for which
\phi
is hence bounded.
Any element
\tilde{z}\in U
satisfies
\log\tilde{z}=t+\log\tilde{z}_{0}
for a
t>0
and a
\tilde{z}_{0}
as above.
We have that
|\tilde{z}|
and
e^{t}
are comparable in the sense that their quotient is bounded away from
0
and
\infty
by factors depending only on
U
.
Similarly
|z|
and
e^{-\alpha t}
are comparable where

\alpha=-{\operatorname{Re}\,}(1/(1+\operatorname{res}))>0.

Consider the path
t\mapsto\tilde{z}(t)=e^{t}\tilde{z}_{0}
and the corresponding path
\log\tilde{z}(t)=\log\tilde{z}_{0}+t
.
Let us integrate
eq.

6
along the latter path.
Note that an antiderivative of
\tilde{z}
with respect to
\log\tilde{z}
is
\tilde{z}
.
We have
|\tilde{z}(t)|=|u_{0}|\int_{0}^{t}e^{t}
and
|z(t)|=|v_{0}|e^{-\alpha t}
.
Hence

\left|\phi(z(t))-a\tilde{z}(t)-\phi(z(0))+a\tilde{z}_{0}\right|\leq c^{\prime}|\tilde{z}_{0}||z_{0}|\int_{0}^{t}e^{s}e^{-\alpha s}ds

If
\alpha\geq 1
then
e^{s}e^{-\alpha s}\leq 1
whence
\int_{0}^{t}e^{s}e^{-\alpha s}ds\leq t
.
If
\alpha\leq 1
then
e^{s}e^{-\alpha s}\leq e^{(1-\alpha)t}
whence
\int_{0}^{t}e^{s}e^{-\alpha s}ds\leq t\leq te^{(1-\alpha)t}
.
∎

The sector in
\tilde{z}
coordinates defined by
\arg\tilde{z}\in(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
corresponds in
\log\tilde{z}
coordinate to the strip
{\operatorname{Im}\,}\log\tilde{z}\in(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
.
In this strip, the subset for which the corresponding
z
belongs to
B(0,r)
is the intersection with
\log HU(r)
.
In
\tilde{z}
coordinate, this intersection is a subset of the sector with a spiralling neighbourhood of
0
removed, see
Figure

7
.
We call this set

U(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},r)=\big\{{\tilde{z}\in U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}}\,\big|\,{\alpha_{0}<\arg\tilde{z}<\alpha_{1},\ |z|<r}\big\}\subset U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}

where
\arg\tilde{z}={\operatorname{Im}\,}\log\tilde{z}
is well-defined on
U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
and
\tilde{z}\mapsto z
also.
Note that it contains the sector defined by
\arg\tilde{z}\in(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
and
|\tilde{z}|>R
where
R=R(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},r,\operatorname{res})
can be computed easily.

Corollary 3
.

In the conditions of
Lemma

2
, for any
\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{0}^{\prime}<\alpha_{1}^{\prime}<\alpha_{1}
there exists
0<r^{\prime\prime}<r^{\prime}\leq r
such that for any
\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}
, if one denotes
S
the (simply connected) set of
z\in U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
for which
\tilde{z}\in U(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},r^{\prime})
, then the restriction of
\phi
to

avoids
0
, and has a lift to
U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
that is analytic, injective and whose image contains
a\times U(\alpha_{0}^{\prime},\alpha_{1}^{\prime},r^{\prime\prime})
, where

is the factor of
Lemma

2
.

Proof.

It follows from the definitions and from the estimate of the second point in
Lemma

2
using, for instance, the argument principle on
\phi
if
\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{0}<2\pi
or for the general case the argument principle on
\log\phi
.
16
16

16

To avoid the
\log
one can instead invoke homological arguments.

∎

For the next lemma, we call bounded resp. unbounded sector of apex
0
subsets of
{\mathbb{C}}
the form

\big\{{z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{*}}\,\big|\,{\alpha_{0}<\arg z<\alpha_{1}\text{ and }|z|<r}\big\}

resp.

\big\{{z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{*}}\,\big|\,{\alpha_{0}<\arg z<\alpha_{1}\text{ and }|z|>r}\big\}

with
\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{0}\in(0,2\pi]
and
r>0
.

Lemma 4
.

For any geodesic parametrized by
t
and tending to a bounded resp. unbounded singularity
z_{0}
:

(1)

The geodesic takes a finite time to reach the singularity in the bounded case, and an infinite time in the unbounded case.

(2)

There exists
r>0
and a simply connected subset
U
of
B(z_{0},r)-\{z_{0}\}
, and an (injective) affine chart
\phi:U\to{\mathbb{C}}
whose image is a bounded resp. unbounded sector with apex
0
, and such that
\phi
sends the geodesic, for
t
large enough, into the bisecting line of the sector, tending to
0
for a bounded singularity and to
\infty
for an unbounded one.

Note that the hypotheses exclude the case
{\operatorname{Re}\,}\operatorname{res}\neq-1
.

Proof.

We distinguish two overlapping cases:
\operatorname{res}\notin\{-2,-3,\ldots\}
and
{\operatorname{Re}\,}\operatorname{res}<-1
.

In the case
\operatorname{res}\notin\{-2,-3,\ldots\}
the lemma follows from the local model deduced from
Lemma

1
, both in the bounded and unbounded case.
In the bounded case, this model is the quotient of a sector (with the canonical atlas of
{\mathbb{C}}
) by a similarity fixing its apex, and a geodesic is a locally straight line in the atlas, hence one tending to the singularity must end up being a ray through the apex, as can be seen by the same trick as the fact that a billiard trajectory in a sector can only undergo finitely many bounces: if we have a a straight line geodesic that is not aimed at the apex then patching finitely many copies of the sector by the similarity covers a sector of angle
>\pi
that will contain the geodesic until it escapes.
In the unbounded case, tending to
0
in the
z
coordinate corresponds to tending to infinity in the model, hence the time tends to infinity, and similarly to the previous case, one see that the geodesic eventually remains in one fundamental domain, then we apply a translation to the chart so that the geodesic radiates from
0
, then the fundamental domain still contains an unbounded sector with
apex
0
.

In the case
{\operatorname{Re}\,}\operatorname{res}<-1
we first focus on proving that the time tends to infinity. For this we recall the first point of the conclusion of
Lemma

2
:

\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial\log\tilde{z}}=a\times\tilde{z}\times(1+r_{1}(z))

where
r
is holomorphic and bounded in
z\in B(0,r)
and
r_{1}(0)=0
.
By taking
r
smaller we can assume that
r^{\prime}(z)
is bounded too.
We have

\arg\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial\log\tilde{z}}=\arg a+{\operatorname{Im}\,}\log\tilde{z}+{\operatorname{Im}\,}\log(1+r_{1}(z)).

By taking
r
small enough we can assume that this quantity has modulus
<1/10
for all
\tilde{z}\in HR(r)
.
We have

\frac{\partial\,{\operatorname{Im}\,}\log(1+r_{1}(z))}{\partial\log\tilde{z}}={\operatorname{Im}\,}\left(\frac{r^{\prime}_{1}(z)}{1+r_{1}(z)}\times\frac{z}{1+\operatorname{res}}\right).

By taking
r
small enough we can assume that this quantity also has modulus
<1/10
for all
\tilde{z}\in HR(r)
.
We let
t_{0}
for which for all
t\geq t_{0}
then
|z(t)|<r
and we now only consider times
t\geq t_{0}
.
As
t
varies and
z
varies accordingly along the geodesic, the variables
\tilde{z}
, and
\phi
can be considered as depending on
t
.
The geodesic has the property that
\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}
remains constant, in particular the argument of this quantity remains constant.
Since

\frac{\partial\log\tilde{z}}{\partial t}=\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}\left/\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial\log\tilde{z}}\right.

we have that, as a function of
t
, the quantity
\log\tilde{z}
follows a field line of a vector field whose direction is given by

\theta(\log\tilde{z})=c-\,{\operatorname{Im}\,}\log\tilde{z}-{\operatorname{Im}\,}\log(1+r_{1}(z))

for some constant
c\in{\mathbb{R}}
that depends on
a
and on the direction of the geodesic in the
\phi
coordinate.
Isoclines of this vector field are given by the level lines of
\theta\bmod 2\pi
.
By the estimates on
\log(1+r_{1}(z))
and its derivative with respect to
\log\tilde{z}
we have that:

-

the isocline of angle
\beta
is a union of curves
I_{\beta+2k\pi}
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, each contained within distance
<1/10
of the horizontal line of ordinate
c-\beta
;

-

the tangent to
I_{\beta}
at every point makes an angle
<\operatorname{asin}\frac{1}{10}
with the horizontal direction;

-

each of these curves extends from
\partial HU(r)
to infinity;

-

each is asymptotic at infinity to the horizontal curve of imaginary part
c-\beta
.

We have that
\theta(\log\tilde{z})
strictly decreases when
\log\tilde{z}
moves upwards along a vertical line; we will say that
\log\tilde{z}
is
above

I_{\beta}
if
\theta(\log\tilde{z})<\beta
and
below
if
\theta(\log\tilde{z})>\beta
(note the inversion).
We have
\operatorname{asin}\frac{1}{10}<\pi/4
which, together with the above points, implies the following:
Consider the isocline
I_{\beta}
with
\beta=2k\pi-\pi/4
.
If
\log\tilde{z}(t_{0})
is below
I_{\beta}
then for
t>t_{0}
the curve
\log\tilde{z}(t)
is disjoint
I(\beta)
so
\log\tilde{z}(t)
stays below
I_{\beta}
.
Similarly if
\log\tilde{z}(t_{0})
is above
I_{\beta}
with
\beta=2k\pi-\pi/4
then after
t_{0}
,
\log\tilde{z}
stays above
I_{\beta}
.
Let
\beta_{*}
denote the isocline on which the geodesic starts.
We take the smallest
\beta_{0}\in 2\pi{\mathbb{Z}}-\pi/4
such that
\beta_{0}\leq\beta_{*}
and the biggest
\beta_{1}\in 2\pi{\mathbb{Z}}+\pi/4
such that
\beta_{1}\geq\beta_{*}
then
\log\tilde{z}(t)
has to remain between the isoclines of index
\beta_{0}
and
\beta_{1}
.
Note that the value of
\beta_{1}-\beta_{0}
is either
2\frac{\pi}{4}
or
2\pi+2\frac{\pi}{4}
, depending on
\alpha_{*}
.
In particular,
\log\tilde{z}(t)
stays within a horizontal strip of height
\leq 2\pi+2\frac{\pi}{4}+\frac{2}{10}
.
Now the only way for
z(t)
to tend to
0
while
\log\tilde{z}(t)
has bounded imaginary part is that
{\operatorname{Re}\,}\log\tilde{z}(t)\longrightarrow+\infty
.
By the second point of
Lemma

2
, we have that
\phi(t)
tends to infinity, hence the geodesic is defined for an infinite amount of time.
This proves the first claim.

With similar arguments the estimates on
r_{1}(z)
for
z
small imply that
\log\tilde{z}(t)
is asymptotic to a horizontal line of imaginary part
\alpha_{\infty}=c-2k\pi
for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Let
\alpha_{0}=c-\beta_{1}-1/10
and
\alpha_{1}=c-\beta_{0}+1/10
.
Then
\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{\infty}<\alpha_{1}
.
We choose any
\alpha^{\prime}_{0}
and
\alpha^{\prime}_{1}
such that
\alpha_{0}<\alpha^{\prime}_{0}<\alpha_{\infty}<\alpha^{\prime}_{1}<\alpha_{1}
and apply
Corollary

3
: the set
\tilde{z}\in U(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},r)
corresponds bijectively to a set of
\phi\in U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
that contains
a\times U(\alpha^{\prime}_{0},\alpha^{\prime}_{1},r^{\prime\prime})
.
(If one would need a definite margin between
\alpha_{\infty}
and
\alpha_{0}^{\prime}
and
\alpha_{1}^{\prime}
, it is always possible to change the value of
\alpha_{0}
to a smaller value and
\alpha_{1}
to a bigger value.)
We saw that
\tilde{z}(t)
remains in the domain
U(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},r^{\prime})
and since
\phi(t)\sim a\tilde{z}(t)
,

\phi(t)
eventually enters and remains in the domain
a\times U(\alpha_{0}^{\prime},\alpha_{1}^{\prime},r^{\prime\prime})
.
We have
\phi(t)=a^{\prime}t+b
for some
a^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
and
b\in{\mathbb{C}}

and
U(\alpha_{0}^{\prime},\alpha_{1}^{\prime},r^{\prime\prime})
contains a sector with apex
0
and central direction
a^{\prime}
.
We can replace
\phi
by
\phi-b
so that
\phi(t)=a^{\prime}t
is now a radial line.
Then the image of this
\phi
still contains an unbounded sector with apex
0
and central direction
a^{\prime}
, possibly with smaller angle.
∎

3.6.
Resting place and paths to a bounded singularity

As explained in
, given a continuous path
\gamma:[0,1]\to\cal S^{\prime}
and a germ of chart
[\phi_{0}]
(the square brackets refer to the fact that the germ is an equivalence class) at the beginning of the path
\gamma(0)
, there is a way to follow this germ along the path
\gamma(t)
as
[\phi_{t}]
.
The value
\phi_{t}(\gamma(t))
is well-defined and the function
\tilde{\gamma}:t\mapsto\phi_{t}(\gamma(t))
is called the
development
of the path
\gamma
w.r.t.
[\phi_{0}]
.
Let us call the point
\phi_{1}(\gamma_{1})
the
resting place
of the development (
principal value
could be a nice alternative denomination).

Can this be done for a path tending to a bounded singularity?
The germ will in general degenerate, but the development will have a limit, provided we take some precaution.
To see this, let us work in a Riemann chart where the singularity is at
0
and where for some
\alpha\in{\mathbb{C}}
with
{\operatorname{Re}\,}\alpha>0
, the similarity charts include the branches of
z\mapsto z^{\alpha}
.
Then if
{\operatorname{Im}\,}\alpha\neq 0
and
z
winds along a circle around
0
many times in the sense opposite to the sign of
{\operatorname{Im}\,}\alpha
, then
z^{\alpha}
tends to infinity.
Or if
z
spirals down to
0
too slowly, winding this way, then
z^{\alpha}
still tends to
\infty
.
On the other hand, if
z
tends to
0
along a well-defined tangent direction, then
z^{\alpha}
tends to
0
, and this limit
0
does not depend on the direction.
Hence the development
\tilde{\gamma}
of the path
\gamma
may or may not converge, but if we replace its last moments by a straight line (in any given Riemann chart), its development has a limit that is independent of the replacement done, and we can thus associate to
\gamma
and
[\phi_{0}]
a well-defined resting place.

Consider two paths
[0,1]\to\cal S
that both start from the same point and end at the same bounded singularity.
Assume that they take their values in
\cal S^{\prime}
except at
t=1
and
assume that they are homotopic in
\cal S
by a homotopy rel.
\{0,1\}
(i.e. that fixes the ends) and that takes value in
\cal S^{\prime}
between the ends.
Then their developments starting from the same germ
[\phi_{0}]
have the same resting place (indeed the development can be followed under the homotopy and the resting place stays immobile).

4.
Holomorphic dependence

4.1.
Holomorphic dependence of special coordinates.

We now state and prove a holomorphic dependence statement related to
Lemma

1
.
We will restrict to parameter spaces of dimension one to simplify the presentation and since in the application in
we only need that.

Let
\zeta_{0}
be a particular map satisfying the conditions of
Lemma

1
.
We assume that
r>0
and that we are given complex numbers

\operatorname{res}_{t}
and
z_{t}
that depend holomorphically on

.
We assume that
\varepsilon>0
, that
z_{t}\in B(z_{0},\varepsilon)
and that we have a family

(t,z)\mapsto\zeta_{t}(z)
for

and

given by

\zeta_{t}(z)=\frac{\operatorname{res}_{t}}{z-z_{t}}+h_{t}(z)

where the map
(t,z)\in B(0,r)\times B(z_{0},\varepsilon)\mapsto h_{t}(z)\in{\mathbb{C}}
is analytic.

Lemma 5
.

Under these conditions, for
\eta
small enough, there exists
r^{\prime}>0
such that for
t\in B(0,r^{\prime})
, one can choose the change of variable
(z\mapsto w)
of

1
to be defined on
B(z_{0},\eta)
and depend holomorphically on
t
.

Proof.

In
Section

4.4
.
∎

If for
t=0
we have
\operatorname{res}_{0}\in\{-2,-3,\ldots\}
then
\zeta_{0}
is not in the situation of

1
. Instead we adapt
Corollary

3
. Recall the definition of the
\tilde{z}
-coordinate given before

:
\tilde{z}=z^{1+\operatorname{res}}
, which is well-defined if

and

are considered as elements of the universal cover

of
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
.
Recall also the definition of the sets
U(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},r)=\big\{{\tilde{z}\in U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}}\,\big|\,{\alpha_{0}<\arg\tilde{z}<\alpha_{1},\ |z|<r}\big\}

Complete
U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
into a topological space
\widehat{U}{\mathbb{C}}^{*}=U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}\cup\{\hat{0}\}
by adding a point
\hat{0}
whose neighbourhoods consists in the sets containing

the set of points whose projection to

has modulus
<\varepsilon
.

Lemma 6
.

Assume that
{\operatorname{Re}\,}\operatorname{res}<-1
.
Denote
\tilde{z}=z^{1+\operatorname{res}_{t}}\in U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
where
z\in U{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
.
Then there exists
\varepsilon>0
and

such that for any

,

where

in

\phi_{t}

;

-

depends holomorphically on

for each fixed

when

within

.

Proof.

By the change of variable

, possibly reducing

, it is enough to treat the case where

for all

.
Recall that
\tilde{z}\mapsto z
is well-defined and note that

depends analytically on

.
Let
\tilde{\zeta}_{t}
be the pull-back of

in

-coordinate: it depends analytically on
(t,\tilde{z})
and hence there exists a global solution
\phi=\tilde{\phi}_{t}
of

on

that depends holomorphically on

.
Consider any.

The variable
\log\tilde{z}
depends analytically on

and by looking at the proof of

, one sees that

and thus

also depend analytically on

.
Moreover, in

2
, all estimates can be taken uniform provided

and

The same holds for

3
.
∎

4.2.
Holomorphic dependence: resting place

Let
U
be an open subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
.
Consider two distinct points
z_{0}\neq z_{1}
.
Consider the set
\cal G(z_{0},z_{1})
of paths
\gamma:[0,1]\to U
from
z_{0}
to
z_{1}
such that
\gamma(s)\neq z_{1}
when
s\neq 1
.
Consider a homotopy class
[\gamma]
in the set above, i.e. a path-connected component of this set.
To state the next lemma we endow the set
\Gamma
of all
(z_{0},z_{1},[\gamma])
with a complex manifold structure.
For this we need to determine when two homotopy classes are close when their endpoints do not match, i.e. we need a topology on
\Gamma
.

Given two paths
\gamma
,
\tilde{\gamma}
in
{\mathbb{C}}
, let

d(\gamma,\tilde{\gamma})=\sup_{s\in[0,1]}|\tilde{\gamma}(s)-\gamma(s)|.

Let
\cal G
be the set of paths
\gamma
in
U
that satisfy
\forall s\in[0,1]
,
\gamma(s)\neq\gamma(1)
: it is the disjoint union of the
\cal G(z_{0},z_{1})
for
z_{0}\neq z_{1}
, both in
U
.
For all
\varepsilon>0
and all
\gamma\in\cal G
, let
V_{\gamma,\varepsilon}

be the set of
(\tilde{z}_{0},\tilde{z}_{1},[\tilde{\gamma}])
where
\tilde{\gamma}\in\cal G
,
d(\gamma,\tilde{\gamma})<\varepsilon
,
\tilde{z}_{0}=\tilde{\gamma}(0)
and
\tilde{z}_{1}=\tilde{\gamma}(1)
.

We will use the following facts, see
Section

4.4
for proofs.

-

There exists
17
17

17

It is easily seen to be unique, since a set is open iff. it is a neighbourhood of all its points.
a topology on
\Gamma
such that for all
\gamma\in\cal G
, the collection of
V(\gamma,\varepsilon)
forms a basis of neighbourhoods.

-

For this topology the sets
V(\gamma,\varepsilon)
are open.

-

It is Hausdorff separated.

-

The map

(z_{0},z_{1},[\gamma])\in\Gamma\mapsto(z_{0},z_{1})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}

is a local homeomorphism.

This allows to endow the set
\Gamma
of all
(z_{0},z_{1},[\gamma])
with a two dimensional complex manifold structure, locally given by the position of the points
z_{0}
and
z_{1}
.

Lemma 7
.

Let
U
be an open subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
(or of a Riemann surface).
Consider a moving point
z_{0}(t)
and a family of Christoffel symbols
t\in B(0,r)\mapsto\zeta_{t}

of the form

\zeta_{t}(z)=h_{t}(z)+\frac{\operatorname{res}_{t}}{z-z_{1}(t)}

with
z_{0}(t)
,
z_{1}(t)
and
\operatorname{res}_{t}
holomorphic in

,

holomorphic in

and
t
, and for all

:
z_{0}(t)\neq z_{1}(t)
and
{\operatorname{Re}\,}\operatorname{res}_{t}>-1
(i.e. the singularity
z_{1}(t)
of

is bounded, possibly removable).
Choose

and

.

.

) of the development of

4.3.
Holomorphic dependence: following a saddle connection when varying
\zeta

A notion of
geodesic
can be defined on similarity surfaces: as parametrized curves which in similarity charts have a constant speed vector, a condition that is indeed independent of the chosen similarity chart.
Geodesics do not need to be injective.
A geodesic from a singularity to another one is called a
saddle connection
by analogy with dynamical sytems.

A geodesic reaching a bounded singularity does it in finite time: see
Lemma

4
.
A saddle connection between bounded singularities is hence parametrized by a bounded interval. We can reparametrize it by the segment
(0,1)
if needed, by a real affine change of variable.

Proposition 8
.

Let
U
be an open subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
and
z^{*}_{0}
,
z^{*}_{1}
two distinct points in
U
.
Assume
\zeta^{*}
is a Christoffel symbol holomorphic on
U-\{z^{*}_{0},z^{*}_{1}\}
and that

\zeta^{*}(z)=h^{*}(z)+\frac{\operatorname{res}^{*}_{0}}{z-z^{*}_{0}}+\frac{\operatorname{res}^{*}_{1}}{z-z^{*}_{1}}

with
h
holomorphic and bounded on
U
.
Assume that
{\operatorname{Re}\,}\operatorname{res}^{*}_{k}>-1
for
k\in\{0,1\}
(i.e. the singularities
z_{k}
are either bounded or better: removable).
Last, assume that a saddle connection
f^{*}:(0,1)\to U
exists between the two singularities.
Then there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that for all Christoffel symbols
\zeta

of the form

\zeta(z)=h(z)+\frac{\operatorname{res}_{0}}{z-z_{0}}+\frac{\operatorname{res}_{1}}{z-z_{1}}

with
h:U\to{\mathbb{C}}
holomorphic,
\sup_{z\in U}|h(z)-h^{*}(z)|<\varepsilon
, and for all
k\in\{0,1\}
,
|z_{k}-z_{k}^{*}|<\varepsilon
and
|\operatorname{res}_{k}-\operatorname{res}_{k}^{*}|<\varepsilon
,
then there exists a saddle connection
f:(0,1)\to U-\{z_{0},z_{1}\}
from
z_{0}
to
z_{1}
, and if
h
,
\operatorname{res}_{k}
,
z_{k}
depend analytically on a complex parameter
\tau
then
f
depends analytically on
\tau
.

Proof.

In
Section

4.4
.
∎

We extend the notion of saddle connection to include geodesics from a bounded singularity to an unbounded one (or in the opposite direction), under the assumption that the unbounded singularity has positive total angle.
Such a geodesic can be parametrized by
t\in(0,+\infty)
, but there is no more uniqueness of the parametrization: it can be reparametrized by a linear change of the variable
t
.
Moreover, since the total angle of the infinite vertex is positive, it follows that one can vary in some extent the direction, in a similarity chart, along which
\infty
is reached.
There are at least two ways to recover uniqueness: either by specifying the initial speed at the bounded singularity.
Or by specifying a marked point which it has to go through, and which must be the image of
t=1
by the parametrization.
We choose the second approach here and in
Section

4.4
.

Proposition 9
.

Let
U
be an open subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
and
z^{*}_{0}
,
z^{*}_{1}
,
z^{*}_{2}
three distinct points in
U
.
Assume
\zeta^{*}
is a Christoffel symbol holomorphic on
U-\{z^{*}_{0},z^{*}_{2}\}
and that

\zeta^{*}(z)=h^{*}(z)+\frac{\operatorname{res}^{*}_{0}}{z-z^{*}_{0}}+\frac{\operatorname{res}^{*}_{2}}{z-z^{*}_{2}}

with
h
holomorphic and bounded on
U
.
Assume that
{\operatorname{Re}\,}\operatorname{res}^{*}_{0}>-1
and
{\operatorname{Re}\,}\operatorname{res}^{*}_{2}<-1
(i.e.
z_{0}
is a bounded singularity and
z_{2}
is an unbounded one).
Last, assume that a saddle connection
f^{*}:(0,+\infty)\to U
exists from
z^{*}_{0}
to
z^{*}_{2}
with
f^{*}(1)=z^{*}_{1}
.
Then there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that for all Christoffel symbols
\zeta

of the form

\zeta^{0}(z)=h(z)+\frac{\operatorname{res}_{0}}{z-z_{0}}+\frac{\operatorname{res}_{1}}{z-z_{2}}

with
h:U\to{\mathbb{C}}
holomorphic,
\sup_{z\in U}|h(z)-h^{*}(z)|<\varepsilon
, for all
k\in\{0,1,2\}
,
|z_{k}-z_{k}^{*}|<\varepsilon
and for all
k\in\{0,2\}
,
|\operatorname{res}_{k}-\operatorname{res}_{k}^{*}|<\varepsilon
,
then there exists a saddle connection
f:(0,+\infty)\to U-\{z_{0},z_{2}\}
from
z_{0}
to
z_{2}
, with
f(1)=z_{1}
, and if
h
,
\operatorname{res}_{k}
,
z_{k}
depend analytically on a complex parameter
\tau
then
f
depends analytically on
\tau
.

Proof.

See
Section

4.4
.
∎

4.4.
Holomorphic dependence with respect to the polygons

Let us come back to our example of similarity surface
\cal S^{\prime}
constructed by gluing polygons, and its completion into a Riemann surface
\cal S
that is isomorphic to the Riemann sphere via

F:\cal S\to\hat{\mathbb{C}}.

The map
F
is unique up to an automorphism of
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
, i.e. a homography.
We recall that the group of homographies is sharply
3
-transitive.

In this section we allow unbounded polygons but we assume that their angle at infinity is always
>0
, which rules out for instance vertices with residue of real part
-1
.

We can always subdivide the polygons further (adding vertices is allowed) to meet the following conditions:

(1)

All polygons are strictly convex,

(2)

every unbounded polygon has exactly two unbounded edges, and their angle is
>0
,

(3)

\cal S
has at least three vertices.

The space of bounded strictly convex polygons with
n
indexed vertices is endowed with a complex structure, which is simply given by the affix of its vertices.
For (strictly convex)
unbounded polygons
satisfying (
2
), we need to add a supplementary information: a marked point in the interior of the unbounded edges, but this marked point is not considered as a vertex. For any paired unbounded sides, there is a unique orientation reversing affine map sending one to the other and matching their marked points.
Reciprocally, for any orientation reversing affine map sending one to the other we can choose (non unique) pairs of matching marked points.

Remark
.

The non-injectivity of this representation, due to this non-uniqueness, is not seen as a problem. It can be recovered by replacing the marked points by the dilation factor
a\in{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
of the affine map
z\mapsto az+b
pairing the unbounded sides.
Note that, anyway, the space of polygons can be factored further by considering polygons up to affine transformations.

In the two statements below, we fix the type of each polygon
P_{j}
—number of edges, boundedness thereof—but not the position of their vertices or marked points, and we fix the combinatorial data of which edges are paired together.

Lemma 10
.

Under these conditions, the residues
\operatorname{res}_{k}
depend holomorphically on the bounded polygons and marked unbounded polygons.

Proof.

Recall
Equations

3
and
4
in their unified version:

\operatorname{res}=\frac{\log\lambda+i\sigma}{2\pi i}-1

for some
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and that the monodromy factor of a small loop winding anticlockwise around the vertex is
\lambda e^{i\sigma}
.

For a finite vertex,
\sigma=\theta
is the total angle of the vertex. The monodromy is also a product of quotients
\displaystyle\frac{a^{\prime}-a}{b^{\prime}-b}
where
a
,
a^{\prime}
,
b
,
b^{\prime}
are vertices.

For an infinite vertex
\sigma=-\theta

and the monodromy factor is also a product of quotients
\displaystyle\frac{a^{\prime}-a}{b^{\prime}-b}
where
a
,
b
are vertices and
a^{\prime}
,
b^{\prime}
are vertices or marked points. The claim follows.
∎

Choose three distinct vertices
v_{1}
,
v_{2}
,
v_{3}
and choose the unique
F
as above that sends them respectively to
\infty
,
0
and
1
. Label the other vertices up to
v_{p}
for some
p\geq 3
.
Let
z_{k}=F(v_{k})
.

Proposition 11
.

Under these conditions and the ones stated before
Lemma

10
, the points
z_{k}
depend holomorphically on the bounded polygons and marked unbounded polygons.

Proof.

In
Section

4.4
.
∎

We will also use the following complement.
To state it, denote
\cal P
the finite collection of the polygons that we are gluing together. Let us note that the polygons, their edges and vertices define after the gluing a cell complex on
\cal S
.
Under
F
, the cell complex decomposition maps to a cell complex decomposition of
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
.
Let us focus on its
1
-skeleton
\operatorname{\mathit{Sk}}(F)
, together with the marked points.
The edges in this skeleton are geodesics of the similarity structure, and they are saddle connections (they run from vertices to vertices, possibly through marked points).

Proposition 12
.

Under the same conditions, the
1
-skeleton
\operatorname{\mathit{Sk}}(F)
moves holomorphically, and this motion extends to an isotopy matching the
2
-cells.

Proof.

See
Propositions

18
and
19
in
Section

4.4
.
∎

By this we mean that there is a family of homeomorphism
S_{\cal P}:\operatorname{\mathit{Sk}}(\cal S^{0})\to\operatorname{\mathit{Sk}}(F)
where
\cal P
varies in the set of allowed polygons and marked unbounded polygons, and where
\cal S^{0}
is the abstract surface, and a cell complex, associated to some arbitrary basepoint
\cal P^{0}
, such that

S_{\cal P}
matches corresponding vertices and marked points and
\forall x\in\operatorname{\mathit{Sk}}(\cal S^{0})
,
S_{\cal P}(x)
depends holomorphically on
\cal P
.
Moreover if one uses a geodesic parametrization of the edges (minus endpoints) by
(0,1)
, or by
(0,+\infty)
sending the marked point to
1
, then
S_{\cal P}
preserves the parametrization.
Last, the
2
-cells cut by the skeletons also match in a continuous fashion.

Remark
.

Propositions

8
and
9
already imply that saddle connections can be followed holomorphically. The point of
Proposition

12
is to prove that they form, as expected,
\operatorname{\mathit{Sk}}(F)
.

Appendix A Proofs

A.1.
Holomorphic dependence of special coordinates: proof of
Lemma

5

We do not provide every detail here as it would be tedious to read.
We assume that we have a change of variable for
\zeta_{0}
as in
Lemma

1
on
B(z_{0},\varepsilon)
.
For
t
small enough,
z_{t}
remains in
B(0,\varepsilon)
and
\operatorname{res}_{t}
remains in
{\mathbb{C}}-\{-1,-2,\ldots\}
.
Let us note that a first change of variable
z\mapsto z-z_{t}
obviously depends holomorphically on
\zeta
, which allows us to restrict to the case where
z_{t}
does not change and is equal to
0
.
We go through the computations of
Lemma

1
again:

\int\zeta_{t}=\operatorname{res}_{t}\log z+\int h_{t}

where
\int h_{t}
is the antiderivative of
h_{t}
that vanishes at the origin.
This map
\int h_{t}
depends holomorphically on
t
and
z
.
Then

\exp\left(\int\zeta_{t}\right)=z^{\operatorname{res}_{t}}\exp\left(\int h_{t}\right).

Denote
g_{t}=\exp\left(\int h_{t}\right)
and let
g_{t}(z)=\sum b_{n}(t)z^{n}
be its power series expansion with respect to
z
. We have

\int z^{\operatorname{res}_{t}}g_{t}(z)dz=z^{\operatorname{res}_{t}+1}\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\frac{b_{n}(t)z^{n}}{\operatorname{res}_{t}+n+1}=z^{\operatorname{res}_{t}+1}\frac{f_{t}(z)}{\operatorname{res}_{t}+1}

with

f_{t}(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\frac{\operatorname{res}_{t}+1}{\operatorname{res}_{t}+n+1}b_{n}(t)z^{n}=\sum a_{n}(t)z^{n}.

This series has at least the radius of convergence of
g_{t}
.
By using Cauchy’s estimates
|b_{n}(t)|\leq\eta^{-n}\sup_{B(0,\eta)}|g_{t}|
and that
|g_{t}(z)|
is uniformly bounded for
(z,t)
small enough we get that
t\mapsto f_{t}(z)
is for each fixed
z
a convergent series of holomorphic functions of
t
, hence holomorphic.

Then we take
R_{t}(z)=\log\frac{f_{t}(z)}{\operatorname{res}_{t}+1}
. For
\eta
and
r^{\prime}
small enough the map
f_{t}
will be non-vanishing and we can choose the branch of the log to be analytically varying with
(z,t)
.
It follows that
w=z\exp(R_{t}(z)/(\operatorname{res}_{t}+1))
is holomorphic in
t
.

For
t=0
,
z\mapsto w
it is the restriction of a change of variable defined on
B(0,\varepsilon)
. By a theorem of Hurwitz, it will still be injective on
B(0,\eta)
for
|t|<r^{\prime}
provided
r^{\prime}
is small enough.

A.3.
Following saddle connections: proof of
Propositions

8
and
9

U
z^{*}_{1}
z^{*}_{0}
saddle connection

Sketch in Riemann coordinates for
\zeta^{*}
.

w_{1}^{*}
w_{0}^{*}
W
L(t_{0})
L(t_{1})

Picture in developped similarity coordinates, before perturbation.
W
and the two sectors are contained in the domain of
\psi^{*}
.

If the perturbation is small enough, there is still a segment between the sector apexes in the domain of
\psi
. The sectors have scaled and rotated but the central bar has not changed.

Figure 8.
Objects in the proof of
Proposition

8
. The sector boundaries are not glued together (they are strict subsets of the sectors defining a neighbourhood of the vertices).

We will say that
\zeta
depends analytically on
\tau
whenever
h
,
\operatorname{res}_{k}
,

do.

Let us call
\varepsilon
-close (to
\zeta^{*}
) the maps
\zeta
as in the statement of

8
.
For
\varepsilon
small enough the two singularities remain of residue of real part
>-1
, i.e. bounded.

Since geodesics go straight with constant speed in affine charts and since the extent of times on which
f
is defined is finite, by pasting inverses of local charts, and of those of

1
at the extremities, one can prove that there exists an open segment
(w_{0}^{*},w_{1}^{*})\subset{\mathbb{C}}
, a neighbourhood

\psi^{*}:V\to U-\{z^{*}_{0},z^{*}_{1}\}
such that

\psi^{*}

,

-

contains a sector based on each end of

and having this segment as a symmetry axis (see also

4
).

Since

is not necessarily injective,
\psi^{*}
is not either.

Let

L(t)=(1-t)w_{0}^{*}+tw_{1}^{*}.

By a compactness argument, for all compactly contained open subset

of

containing the middle point

, there is
\varepsilon>0
such that a map

for all

-close

such that,

,
\psi^{\prime}(M)=(\psi^{*})^{\prime}(M)
,

-

\psi
is analytic, locally invertible with local inverses being similarity charts for
\zeta
.

Moreover,

depends analytically on
\tau
if
\zeta
does. This comes from the expression of local similarity charts in terms of
\zeta
(or as a variant: from the fact that
\psi
is a solution of the complex ODE
-\psi^{\prime\prime}/\psi^{\prime 2}=\zeta\circ\psi
.)

Near singularities, according to

, the formula for the holomorphic change of variable

in

1
depends holomorphically on
\tau
if
\zeta
does.
Recall that branches of

are similarity charts.
We can thus choose for

, independent of

, such that there are similarity chart inverses

,

, and such that, as

,

,
