Appendix B Example of a hole type infinite vertex

Consider the strip
P:``{\operatorname{Re}\,}z\in[1,2]"
and pair the left unbounded edge with the right one by sending
z\mapsto 2z
, i.e.
1+iy\to 2+2iy
.
(To respect the temporary restrictions of the section
Unbounded polygons
on page
2
one can cut
P
in two pieces along
{\mathbb{R}}
and pair the two bounded edges together.) Then the quotient
\cal S
is homeomorphic to a sphere and there are two vertices, both unbounded. It is then easy to uniformize the Riemann surface
\cal S^{\prime}
: it is isomorphic to
H/\sim
where
H
is the half plane “
{\operatorname{Re}\,}z>0
” and
z\sim 2z
. Mapping the situation by
z\mapsto\log z
the set
H
becomes the infinite band “
|{\operatorname{Im}\,}z|<\pi/2
” and the equivalence relation becomes
z\sim z+\log 2
. See
Figure

10
. Hence the Riemann surface
\cal S^{\prime}
is isomorphic to a cylinder of finite height, i.e. to a round annulus via the transformation
z\mapsto e^{2\pi iz/\log 2}
, and both vertices are holes.

Appendix C Proof of holomorphic dependence (
Proposition

11
)

This appendix introduces a lot of notations, and we apologize in advance to the reader.
However, most, if not all, will only be used here.

Adding the image of the marked points

For each paired unbounded edges there was a marked point on the interior of each.
We extend the sequence
z_{k}
by appending the image by
F:\cal S\to\hat{\mathbb{C}}
of the marked points, to get an (injective) element of
\hat{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
for some
m\geq p

and we will prove that this extended sequence depends holomorphically on the polygons.

Summary of the proof

Before entering in a fully detailed argument, let us give an overview of the proof.

The construction of
Proposition

11
starts from a collection of polygons and gluings.
We will define a space
\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}
of collections of strictly convex polygons up to
{\mathbb{C}}
-affine transform, which represent the possible geometric shapes that can take each of the polygons when they vary while remaining strictly convex.
(For unbounded polygons, marked points are taken into account.)
The space
\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}
is a complex manifold, on which the construction can be carried out and yields in particular the points
z_{k}\in\hat{\mathbb{C}}
and their associated residues
\operatorname{res}_{k}
.
We already know by
Lemma

10
that the
\operatorname{res}_{k}
depend holomorphically on the polygons and marked points.

In fact we will see that one can get from the construction not only the position of the points
z_{k}
(up to an automorphism of
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
) but an element of the Teichmüller space
\cal T
of the Riemann sphere
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
with
m
marked points.
So we consider the map

\operatorname{Glu}:\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}\to\cal{TR}

that sends a collection to the Teichmüller space element thus constructed, together with the collection of residues.
The name of the map reflects the fact that it is defined by a process that starts by
gluing
polygons.
A priori we do not know anything on
\operatorname{Glu}
, and even its continuity should not be considered as a trivial statement.

To prove analytic dependence of the
z_{k}
we will prove the stronger statement that
\operatorname{Glu}
is analytic. For this, we will introduce sort of a reciprocal map

\operatorname{Per}:\cal{TR}\to\mathrm{Conf}

where
\mathrm{Conf}
is a space containing
\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}
.
The name
\operatorname{Per}
is an abbreviation of
periods
.
It is defined as follows.
An element of
\cal{TR}
consists in an element of
\cal T
, in particular points
z_{k}
, and the specification of complex numbers
\operatorname{res}_{k}
associated to each
z_{k}
with some compatibility conditions.
One can associate the unique Christoffel symbol having these residues at the
z_{k}
, and we thus get a similarity surface structure on
\hat{\mathbb{C}}-\{z_{k}\}_{k=1}^{n}
.
To the element of
\cal T
corresponds a decomposition of the sphere into topological polygons, unique up to isotopy of the sphere fixing the marked points.
Local straightening maps of the Christoffel symbol can be extended to these topological polygons into holomorphic maps, which are not necessarily anymore injective.
However, one can look at the image of those topological polygons by these extended straightening maps, and the position of the vertices in these images, up to a
{\mathbb{C}}
-affine map, is independent of the isotopy.
This collection of positions, up to
{\mathbb{C}}
-affine maps, is what
\operatorname{Per}
associates to the element of
\cal{TR}
we started from.

The name
periods
of the map has been chosen because the relative position can be computed by integrating an O.D.E. along paths between the
z_{k}
(those that are bounded vertices or marked point) and the result only depends on the homotopy class of the path; this is akin to computing translation vectors between branch points of a translation surface by integration of a one-form, and those vectors are sometimes called periods by extension of the notion of period of a one-form along a closed loop.

One immediately gets that

\operatorname{Per}\circ\operatorname{Glu}=\operatorname{Id}_{\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}}.

The space
\mathrm{Conf}
is not an analytic manifold but we identify a subset
\mathrm{Conf}^{*}
that carries an analytic manifold structure and such that

\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}\subset\mathrm{Conf}^{*}\subset\mathrm{Conf},

\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}
is an open subset of
\mathrm{Conf}^{*}
and their analytic structures match.
We then check in
Lemma

16
that
\operatorname{Per}
is continuous on
\cal{TR}
, and analytic on
\operatorname{Per}^{-1}(\mathrm{Conf}^{*})
.

Let

\mathrm{Eff}:=\operatorname{Glu}(\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf})\subset\cal{TR}.

The name
\mathrm{Eff}
is an abbreviation for
effective
.
From
\operatorname{Per}\circ\operatorname{Glu}=\operatorname{Id}_{\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}}
it immediately follows that

\operatorname{Glu}\circ\operatorname{Per}|_{\mathrm{Eff}}=\operatorname{Id}_{\mathrm{Eff}}.

Intuition tells us that
\mathrm{Eff}
must be an open subset of
\cal TR
but this is a non-trivial fact.
Recall that at this point of the proof, we do not even know that
\operatorname{Glu}
is continuous, so
\mathrm{Eff}
could just be be any kind of uncountable subset of
\cal TR
. On the other hand, it is immediate that the subset
\operatorname{Per}^{-1}(\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf})
of
\cal{TR}
is open, since
\operatorname{Per}
is holomorphic hence continuous.

A key point (see below), which will require some effort, will be to prove that each point in
\mathrm{Eff}
has a neighbourhood
W
in
\operatorname{Per}^{-1}(\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf})
, on which the equality

\operatorname{Glu}\circ\operatorname{Per}|_{W}=\operatorname{Id}_{W}

holds.
This implies that
W\subset\mathrm{Eff}
and proves that
\mathrm{Eff}
is an open subset of
\cal{TR}
.

To conclude, we use a theorem that if one has an analytic map
\phi:U\to V
between open subsets of complex manifolds of dimensions
a
and
b
, and if
\phi
is a bijection, then
a=b
and
\phi^{-1}
is analytic too.
We apply this to
\phi=\operatorname{Per}|_{\mathrm{Eff}}:U=\mathrm{Eff}\to V=\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}
.

The aforementioned key point follows from
Lemma

17
and the paragraph after it. An effective element
x
of
\cal{TR}
corresponds to a collection of strictly convex polygons
P_{j}
.
Nearby elements
x^{\prime}
of
\cal{TR}
will map by
\operatorname{Per}
, which is continuous, to configurations of points for which there exists strictly convex polygons
P^{\prime}_{j}
having these points as vertices and marked points.
The difficulty is to prove that gluing these new polygons indeed gives back
x^{\prime}
. For this we follow the saddle connections as a function of
x^{\prime}
. This defines a cellular decomposition of the sphere and we check that the cells actually map to the
P^{\prime}_{j}
by the straightening maps of
x^{\prime}
, provided it is close to
x
. Since the cellular decomposition for
x^{\prime}
is obtained by deformation of the one for
x
, we can check that gluing the
P^{\prime}_{j}
also gives the right isotopy class for the Teichmüller part of
x^{\prime}
.

Polygon space

Consider strictly convex bounded polygons
P\subset{\mathbb{C}}
: they are assumed with non-empty interior and have at least three vertices. Let us orient the boundary of
P
in the counter clockwise way, and index its vertices accordingly as follows:
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}
.
The set of strictly convex polygons with indexed vertices thus forms an open subset of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
.

For the unbounded polygons, recall that we assumed: that they are strictly convex; that there are exactly two unbounded edges; that they make a positive angle (this is a supplementary assumption made in
Proposition

11
); that we consider
\infty
as a vertex; that we include a marked point in the interior of each of the two unbounded edges.
We still orient the boundary in the anti-clockwise way and index the vertices and marked points accordingly
but omitting
\infty
:
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}
. We still get an open subset of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
, where now
n
is the number of edges plus one, so
n\geq 3
.

Quotienting the polygon space

Changing one polygon by mapping it under an affine map does not change the quotient surfaces
\cal S
,
\cal S^{\prime}
(there are trivial canonical isomorphisms), nor the sequence
(z_{k})
, hence we can work in the space of (possibly marked) polygons
P_{j}
up to the action of affine maps: this is still a complex manifold (it amounts to fixing the affix of two particular vertices/marked points of
P_{j}
) and the quotient map is analytic and open.

In fact we will use later larger spaces. Let
\operatorname{Aff}{\mathbb{C}}
denote the group of
{\mathbb{C}}
-affine maps
w\mapsto aw+b
of
{\mathbb{C}}
and let it act on
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
component-wise: for
(w_{k})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
,
s\cdot(w_{k}):=(s(w_{k}))
.
Denote

A{\mathbb{C}}^{n}={\mathbb{C}}^{n}/\operatorname{Aff}{\mathbb{C}}.

The quotient map
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\to A{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
is open for the quotient topology.
19
19

19

I.e. the saturate of an open subset of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
is open, which is easily checked here since affine maps are open.

Let
A^{*}{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\subset A{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
be the quotient of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}-\Delta
where
\Delta=\big\{{(w,\ldots,w)}\,\big|\,{w\in{\mathbb{C}}}\big\}
i.e.
\Delta
represents the case where all points have merged into a single point.
The space
A^{*}{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
holds a complex manifold structure such that the quotient map
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}-\Delta\to A^{*}{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
is analytic. Locally, charts of
A^{*}{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
are given by fixing the affix of two distinct points.
The space
A{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
has only one point more than
A^{*}{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
and we do not seek to extend the complex structure to this point. For the quotient topology
A{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
is not Hausdorff separated: indeed the special point has only one neighbourhood, which is the whole space
A{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
(this is sometimes called a
focal point
).

Recall that we indexed the collection of polygons
P_{j}
by a finite set
J
.
Let
n_{j}
be the number of vertices on
P_{j}
(including marked points, excluding infinity).
Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{Conf}=\prod_{j\in J}A{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{j}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Conf}^{*}=\prod_{j\in J}A^{*}{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{j}}

For each
j
, the number
n_{j}
is fixed and we also fix the set of
j
for which the
w_{k}
include marked points, whose indexes we fix and must be two integers that are consecutive modulo
n_{j}
.
Let
\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}\subset\mathrm{Conf}
denote the elements such that for each
j
without marked point, the
(w_{k})
are given by the anti-clockwise indexed vertices of a strictly convex bounded
P_{j}
, and in the case with marked point, the
(w_{k})
are given as explained in the paragraph above called “Polygon space”.
Then the subset
\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}
of
\mathrm{Conf}
is open and

\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}\subset\mathrm{Conf}^{*}\subset\mathrm{Conf}.

As a product of analytic manifolds,
\mathrm{Conf}^{*}
is an analytic manifold and this makes
\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}
an analytic manifold too.

Moduli space and Teichmüller space of the sphere with marked points

We fix a particular polygon configuration in
\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}
, which we will call
[\cal P^{0}]
.
Denote
\cal S^{0}
,
\cal V^{0}=\big\{{v_{k}^{0}}\,\big|\,{1\leq k\leq m}\big\}
, and
(\cal S^{\prime})^{0}=\cal S^{0}-\cal V^{0}
the respective Riemann surface, vertices, and similarity surface associated to
\cal P^{0}
.

A quick reminder on the moduli and Teichmüller spaces of the sphere with marked points can be found in
Section

4.4
. However, for the reader’s convenience, we copy here some of the definitions given there.

The moduli space is the subset
\cal M\subset\hat{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
of
n
-uplets
(z_{k})\in\hat{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
for which the
z_{k}
are distinct and
z_{1}=0
,
z_{2}=1
,
z_{3}=\infty
. It is a complex manifold of dimension
n-3
.
To a configuration
\cal P\in\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}
we associate the image in
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
of its vertices and marked points:
Z=(z_{k})=(F(v_{k}))\in\cal M
for the associated conformal isomorphism
F:\cal S\to\hat{\mathbb{C}}
sending
v_{1}
to
0
,
v_{2}
to
1
and
v_{3}
to
\infty
(as defined before
Proposition

11
), and we let
\cal Z=\big\{{z_{k}}\,\big|\,{1\leq k\leq n}\big\}
.
To
\cal P
we also associate the Christoffel symbol
\zeta_{\cal P}
on
\hat{\mathbb{C}}-\cal Z
that our construction yields.
Naturally, we denote
F^{0}
,
Z^{0}
and
\cal Z^{0}
the objects associated to
\cal P^{0}
.

Let
\cal T
be the Teichmüller space associated to
\cal S^{0},\cal V^{0}
defined (without the use of quasiconformal maps) as follows:

\cal T=\cal F/\cal H^{0}

where
\cal F
is the set of orientation preserving homeomorphisms
f:\cal S^{0}\to\hat{\mathbb{C}}
sending respectively
v^{0}_{1}
,
v^{0}_{2}
and
v^{0}_{3}
to
0
,
1
and
\infty
and
\cal H^{0}
is the set of orientation preserving self homeomorphisms of
\cal S^{0}
that are isotopic to the identity rel.
\cal V^{0}
, i.e. by an isotopy fixing each vertex
v_{k}^{0}
.

If
f\in\cal F
, its equivalence class modulo right composition with
\cal H^{0}
will be denoted
[f]\in\cal T
. Hence,
[f_{1}]=[f_{2}]
iff
\exists\phi\in\cal H^{0}
such that
f_{2}=f_{1}\circ\phi
. But note that this is equivalent to:
f_{2}
is isotopic to
f_{1}
rel.
\cal Z=\{z_{k}=f_{1}(v_{k}^{0})\}
.

Let us endow
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
with a spherical metric
20
20

20

A metric for which the circles are geometric circles via a stereographic projection.

d
and the set
\cal F
with the metric

d(f_{1},f_{2})=\sup\big\{{d(f_{1}(x),f_{2}(x))}\,\big|\,{x\in\cal S^{0}}\big\}.

The space
\cal T
is endowed with the quotient topology.
It is separated (distinct points have some disjoint neighbourhoods).
The projection
\Pi:\cal F\to\cal T
is open and has continuous local sections (it is a fibre bundle, see
Section

4.4
).
Since
\Pi
is open, a subset of
\cal T
is a neighbourhood of
[f]\in\cal T
iff for some
\varepsilon>0
it contains the set of
[f^{\prime}]
for which
d(f,f^{\prime})<\varepsilon
.
We will use the following topological lemma.
Endow
\cal S^{0}
with a distance
d
inducing its topology and
\cal H^{0}
with the the distance

d(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})=\sup\big\{{d(\phi_{1}(x),\phi_{2}(x))}\,\big|\,{x\in\cal S^{0}}\big\}.

Then (see
Section

4.4
,
Lemma

29
):

Lemma 13
.

There exists
\varepsilon>0
such that for every orientation preserving self-homeomorphism
\phi
of
\cal S^{0}
, if
\phi
fixes every point in
\cal V^{0}
and satisfies
d(\phi,\operatorname{id}_{\cal S^{0}})<\varepsilon
then
\phi\in\cal H^{0}
(i.e.
\phi
is isotopic to the identity rel.
\cal V^{0}
).

Stating the objective in terms of analyticity of a function
\operatorname{Glu}

For a polygon configuration in
\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}
, let us explain why our gluing construction not only gives us an element of
\cal M
but in fact an element of
\cal T
.
For this, given a polygon configuration
[\cal P]\in\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}
we build below a homeomorphism
\psi:\cal S^{0}\to\cal S
. It is not unique but its class modulo
\cal H^{0}
will be unique.

Consider a collection
\phi=(\phi_{j})
of homeomorphisms
\phi_{j}:\partial P^{0}_{j}\to\partial P_{j}
that send vertices and marked points of
P^{0}_{j}
to their corresponding points in
P_{j}
(in particular, they are orientation preserving) and such that the
\phi_{j}
are compatible with the identification of paired edges:
if
(e\,{:}\,j)
and
(e^{\prime}\,{:}\,j^{\prime})
are paired in
\cal P^{0}
, and
s
is the
{\mathbb{C}}
-affine map matching them, calling
(\tilde{e}\,{:}\,j)
,
(\tilde{e}^{\prime}\,{:}\,j^{\prime})
and
\tilde{s}
the corresponding objects in
\cal P
, we ask that
\phi_{j^{\prime}}\circ s=\tilde{s}\circ\phi_{j}
.
Such a collection exists, but is not unique.
Then
\phi_{j}
can be extended to an orientation preserving homeomorphism
\phi_{j}:P^{0}_{j}\to P_{j}
.
Let us join all these maps into a map
\phi:\bigsqcup_{j}P^{0}_{j}\to\bigsqcup_{j}P_{j}
.
This join descends to an orientation preserving homeomorphism
\psi:\cal S^{0}\to\cal S
that depends only on
\phi
such that
\pi\circ\phi=\psi\circ\pi^{0}
, where

\pi:\bigsqcup_{j}P_{j}\to\cal S\text{ and }\pi^{0}:\bigsqcup_{j}P^{0}_{j}\to\cal S^{0}

are the projection to the respective quotients.
This is summed up in the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigsqcup_{j}P^{0}_{j}}

Different choices for

yield different maps
\psi
but they are all equivalent modulo

.
21
21

21

This is directly seen to be equivalent to: the maps
\psi^{-1}
are all isotopic rel. the vertices and marked points of
\cal S^{0}
. Actually by taking inverse this is also equivalent to stating that the maps
\psi
are isotopic rel the vertices and marked points of
\cal S
.

This follows from the following topological facts, via a homeomorphism from
P^{0}_{j}
to
\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
: the set of orientation preserving self-homeomorphisms of a segment is connected (it is convex!), an isotopy of the circle can be radially extended to an isotopy of the closed unit disk
\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
(immediate), the set of self-homeomorphisms of
\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
that are the identity on
\partial{\mathbb{D}}
is connected (easy thanks to Alexander’s trick
[
Ale24
]
).

Recall that
F
denotes a specific Riemann surface isomorphism from
\cal S
to
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
.
Then the map

f:=F\circ\psi

is an element of
\cal F
.
(By what we proved in the previous paragraph,) its class modulo pre-composition by elements of
\cal H^{0}
is independent of the choice of
\phi
and is the element of
\cal T
we associate to
\cal P
. This independence is used in the proof of

.

We partition the index set
\{1,\ldots m\}
into three subsets as follows: let
F\subset\{1,\ldots m\}
denote the set of those indices
k
for which the vertex
v^{0}_{k}
of the base surface
\cal S^{0}
is bounded,

I\subset\{1,\ldots m\}
those for which it is unbounded and
M\subset\{1,\ldots m\}
be the marked points.
Let

(7)

\cal R=\left\{{(r_{k})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m}}\,\middle|\,{\begin{array}[]{c}\sum r_{k}=-2\\
\forall k\in F,\ {\operatorname{Re}\,}r_{k}>-1\\
\forall k\in I,\ {\operatorname{Re}\,}r_{k}<-1\\
\forall k\in M,\ r_{k}=0\end{array}}\right\}

and

\cal{TR}=\cal T\times\cal R.

To a polygon configuration
[\cal P]\in\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}
let us associate the element
[f]
of
\cal T
that the construction above yields. Let us also associate the set of residues
\operatorname{res}_{k}
of the Christoffel symbol
\zeta_{P}
at the
m
vertices/marked point and temporarily denote

\operatorname{res}=(\operatorname{res}_{k})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m}.

We saw earlier that
\sum\operatorname{res}_{k}=-2
, and the types of the singularities (bounded or unbounded) of
\zeta
are the same as for
\cal S^{0}
according to
Section

3.2
and for marked points there is no singularity, so
\operatorname{res}\in\cal R
.
We denote

\operatorname{Glu}:\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}&\to&\cal{TR}\\
{}[\cal P]&\mapsto&([f],\operatorname{res})\end{array}\right.

where
f
and
\operatorname{res}
are defined above.
We call
\operatorname{Glu}
the
gluing
map.
We want to prove that
\operatorname{Glu}
is analytic.
But we do not even know yet that it is continuous.

An analytic inverse candidate
\operatorname{Per}

Consider any element
([f],\operatorname{res})
of
\cal{TR}
,
f:\cal S^{0}\to\hat{\mathbb{C}}
and
(\operatorname{res}_{k})\in\cal R
.
Let
z_{k}=f(v_{k}^{0})
be the affixes that any map in
[f]
gives to the vertices and let
\cal Z=\big\{{z_{k}}\,\big|\,{1\leq k\leq n}\big\}
denote their set.
Consider the unique Christoffel symbol
\zeta
that is holomorphic on
\hat{\mathbb{C}}-\cal Z
and whose singularities are at most simple poles and have residues
\operatorname{res}_{k}
at
z_{k}
.
By definition of
\cal R
if the type of
v_{k}^{0}
is finite (F), infinite (I), marked (M) then the corresponding singularity
z_{k}
of
\zeta
is respectively bounded (
{\operatorname{Re}\,}\operatorname{res}_{k}>-1
), unbounded (
{\operatorname{Re}\,}\operatorname{res}_{k}<-1
), erasable (
{\operatorname{Re}\,}\operatorname{res}_{k}=0
).
Consider a polygon
P_{j}^{0}
of
\cal P^{0}
, choose any point
w_{*}
in the interior of
P^{0}_{j}
, and let
z_{*}=f\circ\pi^{0}(w_{*})
(recall that
\pi^{0}:\bigsqcup_{j}P^{0}_{j}\to\cal S^{0}
).
Choose any germ
\phi_{0}
of similarity chart for
\zeta
at
z_{*}
.
For all bounded vertices and all marked points
w
of
P^{0}_{j}
, the germ can be developed along any path within
f\circ\pi^{0}(P^{0}_{j})
going to
z=f\circ\pi^{0}(w)
without hitting the vertices (except at the end) and the associated resting place (see
Section

3.6
) is independent of the choice of the path since
P^{0}_{j}
is simply connected.
Let

R\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{j}}

be the indexed collection of all these resting places.

Lemma 14
.

The projection of
R
in
A{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{j}}
is independent of the representative of
f
in
[f]
, of the choice of
w_{*}
and of the choice of
\phi_{0}
.

Before proving it, let us gather all those projections for the different
j\in J
: we have hence defined a map

\operatorname{Per}:\cal{TR}\to\mathrm{Conf}

that we call the
periods
map.

To prove
Lemma

14
, and for further uses, we extend the definition of
R
.
Assume that
z_{*}\in\hat{\mathbb{C}}-\cal Z
is any point, that
\phi_{0}
is a germ of straightening chart at
z_{*}
, that
\gamma_{i}:[0,1]\to\hat{\mathbb{C}}
,
i\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
,
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
are paths such that
\gamma_{i}(0)=z_{*}
,
\gamma_{i}([0,1))\subset\hat{\mathbb{C}}-\cal Z
and
\gamma_{i}(1)\in\cal Z
is a bounded singularity of
\zeta
(i.e. corresponds to a
v_{k}^{0}
that is either a finite vertex or a marked point). Develop
\phi_{0}
along each
\gamma_{i}
and call
c_{i}\in{\mathbb{C}}
its resting point. Let
R=(c_{i})_{i=1}^{p}
, which depends on all the choices above.

Lemma 15
.

Consider two choices of
z_{*}
,
\phi_{0}
,
\gamma_{i}
and
z_{*}^{\prime}
,
\phi^{\prime}_{0}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{i}
as above and assume that there exists a path
\delta
from
z_{*}^{\prime}
to
z_{*}
such that each path
\gamma^{\prime}_{i}
is homotopic to the concatenation
\delta\cdot\gamma_{i}
by a homotopy leaving fixed the starting and endpoints, and avoiding
\cal Z
except at the endpoint.
Then the projections of the corresponding
R
and
R^{\prime}
in
A{\mathbb{C}}^{p}
are identical.

Proof.

Let us develop
\phi^{\prime}_{0}
along
\delta
. We obtain at the end a germ
\tilde{\phi}_{0}
at
z_{*}
. This germ takes the form
\tilde{\phi}_{0}=s\circ\phi_{0}
for some
{\mathbb{C}}
-affine map
s
.
The development of
s\circ\phi_{0}
along
\gamma_{i}
is the post-composition by
s
of the development of
\phi_{0}
along the same path
\gamma_{i}
and its resting place is
s(c_{i})
.
By the homotopy assumption and properties of resting places (see
Section

3.6
) the resting places
c_{i}^{\prime}
composing
R^{\prime}
are the same as the resting places obtained from
z^{\prime}_{*}
,
\phi^{\prime}_{0}
,
\delta\cdot\gamma_{i}
.
The development of
\phi^{\prime}_{0}
along
\delta\cdot\gamma_{i}
is the concatenation of the development of
\phi^{\prime}_{0}
along
\delta
and the development of
\tilde{\phi}_{0}
along
\gamma_{i}
.
Hence the
c^{\prime}_{i}
are the same as the resting places obtained from

z_{*}
,
s\circ\phi_{0}
,
\gamma_{i}
, i.e.
c^{\prime}_{i}=s(c_{i})
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

14
.

Assume that
[f]=[f^{\prime}]
, that
w_{*}
and
w_{*}^{\prime}
are in the interior of
\cal P^{0}_{j}
and that
\phi_{0}
and
\phi_{0}^{\prime}
are germs of straightening coordinates of
\zeta
at these points.
Let
z_{*}=f(w_{*})
and
z^{\prime}_{*}=f^{\prime}(w_{*}^{\prime})
.
Consider paths
\eta_{i}
,
i\in\{1,\ldots,n_{j}\}
, in
\cal P^{0}_{j}
from
w_{*}
to the finite vertices and marked points and a path
\beta
from
w^{\prime}_{*}
to
w_{*}
within
\cal P^{0}_{j}
.
Let
\gamma_{i}=f\circ\eta_{i}
.
Let
\gamma^{\prime}_{i}=f^{\prime}\circ(\beta\cdot\eta_{i})=(f^{\prime}\circ\beta)\cdot(f^{\prime}\circ\eta_{i})
.

Since
[f]=[f^{\prime}]
there is an isotopy
s\in[0,1]\mapsto f_{s}
from
f^{\prime}
to
f
fixing the images of all vertices and marked points.
22
22

22

Write
f^{\prime}=f\circ h
for some
h\in\cal H_{0}
, let
h_{s}
be a path from
h
to
\operatorname{Id}
in
\cal H^{0}
and let
f_{s}=f\circ h_{s}
.

In particular
f_{0}=f^{\prime}
and
f_{1}=f
.
Let
\alpha:\,t\in[0,1]\mapsto\alpha(t)=f_{t}(w_{*})
which goes from
\alpha(0)=f^{\prime}(w_{*})
to
\alpha(1)=f(w_{*})=z_{*}
.
Let
\delta
be the concatenation
(f^{\prime}\circ\beta)\cdot\alpha
.
Then the conditions of
Lemma

15
are satisfied, with the following homotopy from
\delta\cdot\gamma_{i}
to
\gamma^{\prime}_{i}
:
first
f^{\prime}\circ\eta_{i}
is homotopic to
\alpha\cdot\gamma_{i}
via

(s,t)\in[0,1]^{2}\mapsto\alpha(2t)
if
t\leq s/2
and
f_{s}\left(\eta_{i}\left(\frac{t-\frac{s}{2}}{1-\frac{s}{2}}\right)\right)
if
t\geq s/2
.
Then
\delta\cdot\gamma_{i}=((f^{\prime}\circ\beta)\cdot\alpha)\cdot\gamma_{i}

is homotopic to
(f^{\prime}\circ\beta)\cdot(\alpha\cdot\gamma_{i})
hence to
(f^{\prime}\circ\beta)\cdot(f^{\prime}\circ\eta_{i})=\gamma^{\prime}_{i}
.

The conclusion of
Lemma

15
proves
Lemma

14
.
∎

Recall that we have put a complex manifold structure on
\mathrm{Conf}^{*}
but not on
\mathrm{Conf}
.

Lemma 16
.

The subset
\operatorname{Per}^{-1}(\mathrm{Conf}^{*})\subset\cal{TR}
is open and on it, the map
\operatorname{Per}
is analytic.

Proof.

We will work locally so we consider an element
(\tau^{\prime},\operatorname{res}^{\prime})\in\cal{TR}=\cal T\times\cal R
such that
\operatorname{Per}(\tau^{\prime},\operatorname{res}^{\prime})\in\mathrm{Conf}^{*}
, and we will allow ourselves to restrict a finite number of times to smaller and smaller neighbourhoods of this element.
Let us focus on one polygon
P^{0}_{j}
and the collection of resting places
R\in\hat{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{j}}
used in the definition of
\operatorname{Per}
.

Fix any
f^{\prime}\in\cal F
such that
\tau^{\prime}=[f^{\prime}]
. Fix a point
w_{*}^{\prime}\in P_{j}^{0}
and let

z_{*}^{\prime}=f^{\prime}\circ\pi^{0}(w_{*}^{\prime})\in\hat{\mathbb{C}}.

We recall that the collection
R\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{j}}
is associated to
([f],\operatorname{res})\in\cal{TR}
and to a choice of point
w_{*}
in the interior of
P^{0}_{j}
and of an initial germ
\phi_{0}
of similarity chart at
z_{*}=f\circ\pi^{0}(w_{*})
.

We want to keep
z_{*}
fixed and equal to
z_{*}^{\prime}
, and hence we want to define
w_{*}=(f\circ\pi_{0})^{-1}(z_{*}^{\prime})
.
This poses several problems.
For one thing,
f
is only defined up to isotopy rel. the vertices and marked points, and thus
w_{*}
depends on the choice of the representative
f
of
[f]
, and for some representatives, the point
w_{*}
is not even in
P^{0}_{j}
.
We could prove that the construction can be carried out for such
w_{*}
and work from that, but by simplicity we prefer here to use that the map
\cal F\to\cal T:\ f\mapsto[f]
has local sections (
Proposition

33
in
Section

4.4
).
So we restrict to a neighbourhood
V
of
\tau^{\prime}
in
\cal F
on which there is a continuous map
V\to\cal F:\tau\mapsto f_{\tau}
with
[f_{\tau}]=\tau
.
We then define

w_{*}(\tau)=(f_{\tau}\circ\pi_{0})^{-1}(z_{*}^{\prime}).

By making
V
smaller, we ensure that
w_{*}(\tau)
remains in the interior of
P^{0}_{j}
. Note that
\tau\mapsto w_{*}(\tau)
is continuous.

We fixed
z_{*}
. We also want to keep fixed the value and derivative at
z_{*}
of the initial germ used in the definition of
R
.
Hence given any point
(\tau,\operatorname{res})\in\cal{TR}
such that
\tau\in V
, we choose, as initial germ of straightening coordinate
\phi
at
z_{*}
for the Christoffel symbol associated to
(\tau,\operatorname{res})
, the unique one satisfying
\phi(z_{*})=\phi_{0}(z_{*})
and
\phi^{\prime}(z_{*})=\phi_{0}^{\prime}(z_{*})
.
Note that
z_{*}=z_{*}^{\prime}\neq\infty
, because
\infty
is one of the vertices or marked points by the convention that
z_{3}
is always
\infty
.

Let us prove that
(\tau,\operatorname{res})\in V\times\cal R\mapsto R(\tau,\operatorname{res})
is analytic (recall that
\cal{TR}=\cal T\times\cal R
and that
\cal R
is defined by
eq.

7
).
We will take advantage of a theorem of Hartogs that asserts that a function
F
from a finite dimensional complex manifold
X
to another is analytic if and only if
F\circ\xi
is analytic for all one complex variable analytic map
\xi
taking values in
X
.
The space
\cal{TR}
is finite-dimensional.
By Hartogs’ theorem, it is enough to prove that
t\mapsto R(\tau_{t},\operatorname{res}(t))
is analytic for all analytic map
t\mapsto\tau_{t}\in V
and all analytic map
t\mapsto\operatorname{res}(t)\in\cal R
, where
t
is a complex number.

Let us show how this follows from
Lemma

7
.
Since
R(\tau_{t},\operatorname{res}(t))
is an element of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{j}}
, we focus on one of its components.
This component corresponds to some vertex or marked point
v\in P^{0}_{j}
, more precisely it is the resting place of any path
\gamma_{t}
of the form
\gamma_{t}=f_{\tau_{t}}\circ\pi^{0}\circ\delta_{t}
where
\delta_{t}
is a path in
P^{0}_{j}
from
w_{*}(\tau_{t})
to
v
that avoids the vertices and marked points of
P^{0}_{j}
except at the end.
We choose
\delta_{t}
to depend continuously on
t
(for instance a segment, linearly parametrized, works since
P^{0}_{j}
is convex and
w_{*}(\tau)
is in its interior).
Then
\gamma_{t}
depends continously on
t
.

Let
(z^{\prime}_{k})=M(\tau^{\prime})
, i.e.
z_{k}^{\prime}=f^{\prime}(v^{0}_{k})
.
Let
z^{\prime}_{k_{0}}
be the one that corresponds to the specific vertex we called
v
.
The set
U
on which we will apply
Lemma

7
is the complement in
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
of the union of a small closed disk around each
z^{\prime}_{k}
, except
z^{\prime}_{k_{0}}
.

The holomorphy hypotheses of
Lemma

7
are immediately seen to be satisfied.
Continuity of
t\mapsto X_{t}=(\gamma_{t}(0),\gamma_{t}(1),[\gamma_{t}])\in\Gamma
follows from the continuity of
t\mapsto\gamma_{t}
and
Lemma

26
.

We have thus proved that the
R
, normalized as described earlier in this proof, depends analytically on
(\tau,\operatorname{res})
near
(\tau^{\prime},\operatorname{res}^{\prime})
.
Since we assumed that
R(\tau^{\prime},\operatorname{res}^{\prime})
, which is an element of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{j}}
, does not have all its components equal, this is still the case for nearby
(\tau,\operatorname{res})
.
Hence
\operatorname{Per}^{-1}(\mathrm{Conf}^{*})\subset\cal{TR}
is open.

Analyticity of
\operatorname{Per}
follows from the analyticity of
R
.
∎

Left inverse property of
\operatorname{Per}

We have

(8)

\operatorname{Per}\circ\operatorname{Glu}=\operatorname{Id}_{\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}}.

Indeed, we defined
\operatorname{Glu}([\cal P])=([f],\operatorname{res})
for some, non-unique yet specific,
f\in\cal F
.
Using this specific
f
in the definition of
\operatorname{Per}
above immediately yields, from the way
f
is defined, that for all
P=P^{0}_{j}\in\cal P_{0}
there is a similarity chart on
f\circ\pi^{0}(P)
whose image is exactly
P_{j}
(minus the vertices).
The resting places hence correspond to the vertices/marked points of
P_{j}
, whence
\operatorname{Per}([f],\operatorname{res})=[\cal P]
.

Right inverse property of
\operatorname{Per}
and conclusion

\cal S^{0}
\cal S^{0}
\cal S^{0}
P^{1}_{1}
P^{1}_{2}
P^{1}_{3}
\cal P^{1}
F^{1}\circ\pi^{1}
\operatorname{Glu}
f^{1}
f
\operatorname{Per}
\cal P
\tilde{f}
\operatorname{Glu}

Figure 12.
Illustration of the proof of
Lemma

17
.
Solid lines on the sphere depict geodesics while dashed lines are not necessarily geodesics.
We are given
f
close to
f_{1}
and from this we construct
\cal P=\operatorname{Per}([f])
close to
\cal P^{1}
and we must justify that we can choose a representatife
\tilde{f}
of
\operatorname{Glu}(\cal P)
that is close to
f^{1}
too. Since the vertices
z_{k}
are at the same place on the bottom sphere, this will prove that
[f]=[\tilde{f}]=\operatorname{Glu}([\cal P])
.
The map
\tilde{f}
is actually defined by following the saddle connections on the
1
-skeleton on and then by some interpolation in the
2
-cells, and we prove that
[\tilde{f}]=\operatorname{Glu}([\cal P])
.

Let

\mathrm{Eff}=\operatorname{Glu}(\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf})

where the notation
\mathrm{Eff}
stands for
effective
.

Lemma 17
.

Every point in
\mathrm{Eff}
has an open neighbourhood
W
in
\cal{TR}
on which
\operatorname{Glu}\circ\operatorname{Per}
is defined (i.e.
\operatorname{Per}(W)\subset\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}
) and equals the identity of
W
.

Proof.

Let
\mathrm{eff}^{1}=\operatorname{Glu}([\cal P^{1}])=([f^{1}],\operatorname{res}^{1})
for some
f^{1}\in\cal F
,
\operatorname{res}^{1}\in\cal R
and
\cal P^{1}=(P^{1}_{j})\in\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}
.
We recall that the homeomorphism
f^{1}:S^{0}\to\hat{\mathbb{C}}
is obtained by mapping each polygon
P^{0}_{j}
to
P^{1}_{j}
and passing to the quotients, then composing with a uniformization of the Riemann surface
\cal S^{1}
to
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
.
By
Equation

8
we have
\operatorname{Per}(\mathrm{eff}^{1})=[\cal P^{1}]
.
Since
\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}
is open and
\operatorname{Per}
is continuous at
\mathrm{eff}^{1}
, there is some neighbourhood
W
of
\mathrm{eff}^{1}
such that
\operatorname{Per}(W)\subset\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}
, so
\operatorname{Glu}\circ\operatorname{Per}
is defined in particular on
W
.

There remains to check that, for a possibly smaller
W
, for all
([f],\operatorname{res})\in W
, denoting
[\cal P]=\operatorname{Per}([f],\operatorname{res})
we have
\operatorname{Glu}([\cal P])=([f],\operatorname{res})
.
By taking
W
small we can ensure that the strictly convex polygon configuration
[\cal P]
is close to
[\cal P^{1}]
.
For any
\varepsilon>0
, by taking
W
small we also know that for any
([f],\operatorname{res})\in W
, there is a representative
f
of
[f]
that is
\varepsilon
-close to
f^{1}
(see
Proposition

30
in
Section

4.4
).

Let
\zeta
be the Christoffel symbol associated to
([f],\operatorname{res})
and
\zeta^{1}
the one associated to
([f^{1}],\operatorname{res}^{1})
.
The image by
F^{1}
of an edge of
\cal S^{1}
is a saddle connection of
\zeta^{1}
.
Let us recall our notation:

\cal P^{1}=(P^{1}_{j})_{j\in J}\text{ and }\cal P=(P_{j})_{j\in J}.

Let
Q^{1}_{j}=F^{1}\circ\pi^{1}(P^{1}_{j})
.
This forms a cellular decomposition of
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
.
By
Propositions

8
and
9
, we can follow continuously (analytically) all the saddle connection mentioned earlier as a function of
([f],\operatorname{res})\in W
provided
W
is small enough.
Since the saddle connections are disjoint in the case of
[\cal P^{1}]
, except at the vertices, a continuity argument proves they are still disjoint away from any neighbourhood of the vertices provided
W
is small enough.
Saddle connections tend to finite vertices along a straight line to the vertex in the similarity charts whose images are sectors based on the vertex and whose sides are glued by a similarity.
Hence distinct saddle connections remain disjoint near the finite vertices too.
The same is true near infinite vertices because the direction at which the different connections converge to infinity make a non-zero angle with respect to each other, because of the restriction we put on the unbounded polygons.
It follows that the saddle connections remain disjoint in their interiors.
They hence define a cellular decomposition of
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
, with cells
Q_{j}
corresponding to deformations of the cells
Q^{1}_{j}
.
More precisely let
\operatorname{\mathit{Sk}}^{1}\subset\hat{\mathbb{C}}
denote 1-skeleton of this cell decomposition for
(Q_{j})
and
\operatorname{\mathit{Sk}}
the same for
(Q^{1}_{j})
.
Matching the saddle connections as parametrized curve defines a homeomorphism
h:E^{1}\to E
that is
\varepsilon
-close to the identity provided
W
is small enough.
It extends to a homeomorphism
h
of the sphere that is
\eta
-close to the identity, with
\eta\longrightarrow 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
: this claim follows from a general theorem of topology.
23
23

23

For instance apply on the image of each polygon the isotopy extension theorem of a circle imbedding in the plane, see for instance Corollary 1.4 of
[
EK71
]
.

We then let
Q_{j}=Q_{j}^{1}

and

\tilde{f}=h\circ f^{1}.

See the following footnote for an alternative approach.
24
24

24

Another way to proceed would be to use the regularity of the curves and a control on how they reach the vertices, to identify which region
Q_{j}
correspond to
Q_{j}^{1}
, then to prove, in a way similar to what we do below, that
Q_{j}
is mapped bijectively by any solution of
\phi^{\prime\prime}/\phi^{\prime}=\zeta
to a
{\mathbb{C}}
-affine image of
P_{j}
, then to exploit continuous dependence of
\phi
w.r.t
\zeta
to reduce to finding homeomorphisms close to the identity between
P^{1}_{j}
and
P_{j}
.

The map
\tilde{f}
is an orientation preserving homeomorphism from
\cal S^{0}
to
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
and maps each cell
\pi(P^{0}_{j})
to
Q_{j}
.
Let
\tilde{h}:\cal S^{0}\to\cal S^{0}
be such that
\tilde{f}=f\circ\tilde{h}
, i.e.
\tilde{h}=f^{-1}\circ\tilde{f}=f^{-1}\circ h\circ f^{1}
.
The map
\tilde{h}
fixes every vertex and marked point (but it does not send the edges and polygons of
\cal S^{0}
to themselves), and is close to the identity.
By
Lemma

13
we have
\tilde{h}\in\cal H^{0}
, i.e.

[f]=[\tilde{f}].

Since our polygons are simply connected, there is a solution
\phi_{j}=\phi
of
\phi^{\prime\prime}/\phi^{\prime}=\zeta
defined on
Q_{j}-\{z_{k}\}
.
This solution maps the edges to straight lines and between two finite vertices or a vertex and a marked point, the image is a bounded open segment whose ends are the resting place of the paths used to define
\operatorname{Per}([\tilde{f}],\operatorname{res})
. But recall that
[\tilde{f}]=[f]
hence
\operatorname{Per}([\tilde{f}],\operatorname{res})=\operatorname{Per}([f],\operatorname{res})=[\cal P]
.
The image by
\phi_{j}
of an edge of
Q_{j}
from a marked point to an infinite vertex is a half-line tending to
\infty
.
Hence all these straight lines form the boundary the strictly convex, possibly marked, polygon
P_{j}
composing
[\cal P]
(up to a
{\mathbb{C}}
affine map).
Replacing
\phi_{j}
by its post-composition with an affine map and adding the vertices to its domain of definition, we may assume that
\phi_{j}(Q_{j})=P_{j}
:

\phi_{j}:Q_{j}\to P_{j}.

By the winding number theorem, the map
\phi_{j}
is a bijection from
Q_{j}
to
P_{j}
, holomorphic in the interior.

Hence
\tilde{f}
realizes the gluing of the
P_{j}
.
More precisely let
\cal S
be the abstract surface constructed from the
P_{j}
and
\pi:\coprod P_{j}\to\cal S
the quotient map.
Let
\cal S^{\prime}
be
\cal S
minus the vertices.
The maps
\phi_{j}\circ\tilde{f}
send
\pi^{0}(P^{0}_{j})
to
P_{j}
in a way that is compatible with the gluings of the
P_{j}
(recall that
\tilde{f}=h\circ f^{1}
, and
h
and
\phi_{j}
respect the linear parametrization of geodesics) and define a map

\psi:\cal S^{0}\to\cal S

such that
\psi=\pi\circ\phi_{j}\circ\tilde{f}
on each
\pi^{0}(P^{0}_{j})
.
Let now

F:=\tilde{f}\circ\psi^{-1}:\cal S\to\hat{\mathbb{C}}

which satisfies

F\circ\pi|_{P_{j}}=\phi_{j}^{-1}.

Claim: The map
F:\cal S\to\hat{\mathbb{C}}
is an analytic isomorphism.
We first prove it on
\cal S^{\prime}
, where it coincides with
\phi_{j}^{-1}\circ\pi^{-1}
on
\pi(P_{j})
, which is immediately seen to be holomorphic in the interior of
\pi(P_{j})
since
\pi
and
\phi_{j}
are holomorphic.
On a an edge minus endpoints
e^{*}
, recall that charts are given as follows: let
\pi(P_{j})
and
\pi(P_{k})
be the cells adjacent to
e^{*}
in
\cal S
.
There is a neighbourhood
V
of
e^{*}
and a similarity chart
\phi:V\to{\mathbb{C}}
for which

\phi(\pi(z))=z
when
z\in P_{j}\cap\pi^{-1}(V)
and
\phi(\pi(z))=s(z)
when
z\in P_{k}\cap\pi^{-1}(V)
where
s
is the
{\mathbb{C}}
-affine map gluing the corresponding edges of
P_{k}
and
P_{j}
.
The image of this chart is the open neighbourhood
W=W_{1}\cup W_{2}
of the appropriate edge of
P_{j}
where
W_{1}=P_{j}\cap\pi^{-1}(V)
and
W_{2}=s(P_{k}\cap\pi^{-1}(V))
.
Then the expression of
F
from this chart is
F\circ\phi^{-1}
, which is equal to
\phi_{j}^{-1}
on
W_{1}
and to
\phi_{k}^{-1}\circ s^{-1}
on
W_{2}
.
These two maps are holomorphic and coincide on the (straight) edge of
P_{j}
, hence their join is holomorphic.
We have thus proved that the map
F=\tilde{f}\circ\psi^{-1}
is an analytic isomorphism from
\cal S^{\prime}
to
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
minus a finite number of points. These singularities are erasable since
F
is continuous, hence actually
F
is an analytic isomorphism from
\cal S
to
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
.

The surface
\cal S^{\prime}
carries a similarity surface structure (an atlas) coming from the polygons under the quotient map
\pi
, and
F
sends this structure to
\hat{\mathbb{C}}-\cal Z
where
\cal Z
is the set of images of the vertices.
To this structure corresponds a Christoffel symbol
\tilde{\zeta}
, and we claim that this symbol is exactly
\zeta
.
Indeed, it is given by
\tilde{\zeta}=\phi^{\prime\prime}/\phi^{\prime}
for any similarity chart
\phi
.
We can take
\phi=\phi_{j}
and by definition
\phi_{j}^{\prime\prime}/\phi_{j}^{\prime}=\zeta
holds on
Q_{j}
.
We conclude by analytic continuation of identities that
\tilde{\zeta}=\zeta
.

Coming back to the definition of the map
\operatorname{Glu}
, all this implies that
\operatorname{Glu}([\cal P])=([\tilde{f}],\operatorname{res})
.
We then conclude as follows:
\operatorname{Glu}\circ\operatorname{Per}([f],\operatorname{res})=\operatorname{Glu}([\cal P])=([\tilde{f}],\operatorname{res})=([f],\operatorname{res})
.
∎

Consider the union
\bigcup W
of the open sets
W
in
Lemma

17
.
We claim that

\bigcup W=\mathrm{Eff}.

Indeed, the lemma states that for each point in
\mathrm{Eff}
there is such a
W
containing it.
Conversely, each such
W
is contained in
\mathrm{Eff}=\operatorname{Glu}(\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf})
because
\operatorname{Glu}(\operatorname{Per}(W))=W
and
\operatorname{Per}(W)\subset\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}
.
This proves the claim.

So
\mathrm{Eff}
is open and the restriction
\operatorname{Per}|_{\mathrm{Eff}}
is an injective analytic map that satisfies
\operatorname{Glu}\circ\operatorname{Per}|_{\mathrm{Eff}}=\operatorname{Id}_{\mathrm{Eff}}
.
By
Equation

8
,
\operatorname{Per}|_{\mathrm{Eff}}\circ\operatorname{Glu}=\operatorname{Id}_{\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}}
.
Hence
\operatorname{Per}|_{\mathrm{Eff}}
is an analytic bijection between open subsets of complex manifolds and
\operatorname{Glu}
is its inverse.
It follows that these manifolds have the same dimension and that
\operatorname{Per}|_{\mathrm{Eff}}
and
\operatorname{Glu}
are analytic isomorphisms (
[
Gun90
]
, Theorem I.11).

This proves
Proposition

11
.

Remark
.

As an interesting consequence we got that
\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}=\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\cal{TR}
.
We can also check this identity directly: let
a_{i}
be the number of cells of dimension
i
in the cellular decomposition of
\cal S
induced by the polygons
P_{j}
(not including the marked points).
Let
a^{\prime}_{2}
be the number of unbounded polygons:
a^{\prime}_{2}\leq a_{2}
.
Let
a^{\prime}_{0}
be the number of marked points in
\cal S
.
Each unbounded polygon has two marked points but the marked points are paired under the gluing, hence
a^{\prime}_{0}=a^{\prime}_{2}
.
We have

\operatorname{dim}\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}=\sum_{j\in J}\operatorname{dim}A^{*}{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{j}}=\sum(n_{j}-2)

(note that each
n_{j}\geq 3
).
If
P_{j}
is a bounded polygon then
n_{j}
is its number of vertices hence its number of edges.
If
P_{j}
is unbounded then
n_{j}
is one plus its number of edges.
Adding up, each edge will be counted twice.
It follows that
\operatorname{dim}\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}=2a_{1}+a^{\prime}_{2}-2a_{2}
.
On the other hand,
\dim\cal{TR}=\dim\cal M+\dim\cal R=(a_{0}+a^{\prime}_{0}-3)+(a_{0}-1)
.
The equality
\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathrm{S\text{-}Conf}=\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\cal{TR}
then follows from cancellation of
a^{\prime}_{2}=a^{\prime}_{0}
and from Euler’s identity
a_{0}-a_{1}+a_{2}=2
.

We state a direct corollary of the proof of
Lemma

17
.

Proposition 18

(holomorphic motion of the
1
-skeleton)
.

In
Lemma

17
, one can choose
W
so that there is a continuous family
(S_{fr})_{fr\in W}
where
S_{fr}
is a homeomorphism from the
1
-skeleton of
\cal S^{0}
to the
1
-skeleton of the surface
\cal S
associated to
\operatorname{Per}(fr)
, matching corresponding edges, vertices and marked points, and such that
F_{fr}\circ S_{fr}
depends holomorphically on
fr
where
F_{fr}
is the analytic isomorphism from
\cal S
to
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
that sends
v_{1}
,
v_{2}
,
v_{3}
to
0
,
1
and
\infty
. They map geodesic parametrization of these curves to geodesic parametrizations.

By geodesic parametrization we mean parametrizations for which the curve has locally constant speed in charts.
By
matching
we mean the following. The surface
\cal S
is built by gluing polygons
P_{j}
that are deformations of the polygons
P_{j}^{0}
forming
\cal S^{0}
, the gluing map being denoted
\pi
in both cases.
We say that the
2
-cells
\pi(P_{j})
and
\pi(P_{j}^{0})
correspond. The polygons
P_{j}
and
P_{j}^{0}
have the same number of vertices and marked points, which are indexed similarly
w_{1}
, …,
w_{n_{j}}
and
w_{1}^{0}
, …,
w_{n_{j}}^{0}
we say that the
0
-cells
\pi(w_{j})
and
\pi(w_{j}^{0})
correspond.
Similarly we define a correspondence between
1
-cells.
Consider corresponding objects
O
and
O^{\prime}
that are
0
,
1
or
2
-cell.
We say that the
f:\cal S^{0}\to\cal S
matches them if
f(O)=O^{\prime}
.

The homeomorphism in
Proposition

18
is necessarily unique under the condition of mapping geodesic parametrization.

Lemma 19
.

The homeomorphisms
S_{fr}
can be extended into homeomorphisms of the sphere matching the
2
-cells and varying continuously with
fr
.

Proof.

At the level of the original polygons,
S_{fr}
lifts to homeomorphisms of their matching sides which are linear and match vertices and marked points.
They form homeomorphisms
\partial P_{j}^{0}\to\partial P_{j}
(we include
\infty
in the boundary for unbounded polygons).
It is enough to extend these homeomorphisms to the inside of the polygons, in a way that depends continuously on
fr
.
Then the union of these homeomorphisms will pass to the quotient and yield the sought-for extension of
S_{fr}
.
It is possible to triangulate
P_{j}^{0}
by triangles whose vertices are only vertices or marked points of
P_{j}^{0}
. Consider the corresponding triangulation of
P_{j}
. There is a unique
{\mathbb{R}}
-affine map mapping matching triangles and matching their vertices.
∎

These extensions are not unique.

Appendix D Topologizing a space of paths

Let
U
be an open subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
.
Denote
\cal G
the set of paths
\gamma
in
U
that satisfy
\forall s\in[0,1]
,
\gamma(s)\neq\gamma(1)
.
For
z_{0}\neq z_{1}
, both in
U
, denote
\cal G(z_{0},z_{1})
the subset of
\cal G
of paths
\gamma
with
\gamma(0)=z_{0}
and
\gamma(1)=z_{1}
.
Given two paths
\gamma
,
\tilde{\gamma}
in
{\mathbb{C}}
, let

d(\gamma,\tilde{\gamma})=\sup_{s\in[0,1]}|\tilde{\gamma}(s)-\gamma(s)|.

As in
Section

4.2
, we define
\Gamma
as the quotient space of
\cal G
under the relation that
\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}
if and only if
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
have the same endpoints
z_{0}
and
z_{1}
and belong to the same path-connected component of
\cal G(z_{0},z_{1})
.
We denote
[\gamma]
the equivalence class of
\gamma
.

We could use this distance on
\cal G
to topologize
\cal G
and define the topology on
\Gamma
as the quotient under the natural map
\cal G\to\Gamma
and leave to the reader the work to check that it has all the properties stated in
Section

4.2
. Instead, we choose the following more concrete approach (in the end, we get the same topology, see the end of the present appendix).

For all
\varepsilon>0
, we denote
V_{\gamma,\varepsilon}
as the image by the quotient map
\cal G\to\Gamma
of the ball
B(\gamma,\varepsilon)\subset\cal G
for the distance
d
introduced above. In other words,
V_{\gamma,\varepsilon}
is the set of
(\tilde{z}_{0},\tilde{z}_{1},[\tilde{\gamma}])
where
\tilde{\gamma}
is a path in
U
,
d(\gamma,\tilde{\gamma})<\varepsilon
,
\tilde{z}_{0}=\tilde{\gamma}(0)
and
\tilde{z}_{1}=\tilde{\gamma}(1)
.

We will use the notation

d(\gamma,\partial U):=\inf_{s\in[0,1],z\in\partial U}d(\gamma(s),z).

In all the statements below, we assume that all the paths take value in
U
.

Lemma 20
.

For all
\gamma
there is
\varepsilon>0
such that if
\tilde{\gamma}(0)=\gamma(0)
,
\tilde{\gamma}(1)=\gamma(1)
and
d(\gamma,\tilde{\gamma})<\varepsilon
then
[\tilde{\gamma}]=[\gamma]
.

Proof.

A linear interpolation works except near
z_{1}
: the condition that the path cannot hit
z_{1}=\gamma(1)
at times
s\neq 1
may fail to hold for the interpolation.
To solve this, let
r>0
be such that
B(z_{1},r)\subset U
.
Let
s_{1}\in(0,1)
such that
\gamma([s_{1},1])\subset B(z_{1},r/2)
.
Let
\varepsilon=\min(r/2,d(\gamma,\partial U),d(\gamma|_{[0,s_{1}]},\{z_{1}\}))
.

As in the statement we assume
d(\gamma,\tilde{\gamma})<\varepsilon
.
Let
\delta(s)=(1-s)\gamma(s_{1})+s\tilde{\gamma}(s_{1})
.
Since
\varepsilon<r/2
,
\delta
is contained in
B(z_{1},r)
.
The path
\gamma
is homotopic within
\cal G(z_{0},z_{1})
to
(\gamma|_{[0,s_{1}]}\cdot\delta)\cdot(\delta^{-1}\cdot\gamma|_{[s_{1},1]})
where the restrictions are appropriately reparametrized.

Dealing with
\gamma|_{[0,s_{1}]}\cdot\delta
: We have a homotopy from
\gamma|_{[0,s_{1}]}\cdot\delta
to
\tilde{\gamma}|_{[0,s_{1}]}

by
t\in[0,1]\mapsto((1-t)\gamma+t\tilde{\gamma})|_{[0,s_{1}]}\cdot\delta|_{[t,1]}
(with
\delta|_{[t,1]}
appropriately reparametrized) ending on the concatenation of
\tilde{\gamma}|_{[0,s_{1}]}
with a constant path, and this is homotopic to
\tilde{\gamma}|_{[0,s_{1}]}
.
The two homotopies avoid
z_{1}
because
\varepsilon<d(\gamma|_{[0,s_{1}]},\{z_{1}\})
and stay in
U
because
\varepsilon<d(\gamma,\partial U)
.

Dealing with
\delta^{-1}\cdot\gamma|_{[s_{1},1]}
: Denote
\gamma^{\prime}
this part and note that it is contained in
B(z_{1},r)
.
Since
\varepsilon<r/2
the path
\tilde{\gamma}|_{[s_{1},1]}
is also contained in
B(z_{1},r)
.
Both
\gamma^{\prime}
and
\tilde{\gamma}|_{[s_{1},1]}
are thus contained in
B(z_{1},r)
, they have the same starting point and they avoid
z_{1}
except at the end.
A linear interpolation in (lifted) polar coordinates provides a homotopy between them.
∎

Lemma 21
.

Let
z_{0}=\gamma(0)
.
If
0<\varepsilon<d(z_{0},\partial U)
and
\delta
is any path in
B(z_{0},\varepsilon)
that avoids
z_{1}=\gamma(1)
and with
\delta(1)=z_{0}
then
[\delta\cdot\gamma]\in V_{\gamma,\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

Let
t_{0}\in(0,1)
and consider the path
\tilde{\gamma}
that sends
s\in[0,t_{0}]
to
\delta(s/t_{0})
and
s\in[t_{0},1]
to
\gamma\big(\frac{s-t_{0}}{1-t_{0}}\big)
.
Then
[\tilde{\gamma}]=[\delta\cdot\gamma]
.
For
t_{0}
small enough we have
d(\tilde{\gamma},\gamma)<\varepsilon
.
∎

In the next statement we denote
z_{0}=\gamma(0)
,
z_{1}=\gamma(1)
,
\tilde{z}_{0}=\tilde{\gamma}(0)
,
\tilde{z}_{1}=\tilde{\gamma}(1)
.

Lemma 22
.

Given
\gamma
, there exists
\varepsilon>0
with
\varepsilon<d(\gamma,\partial U)
and
\varepsilon<|z_{0}-z_{1}|
such that for all
\tilde{\gamma}
, if
d(\gamma,\tilde{\gamma})<\varepsilon
, if
\delta
is any path within
B(z_{0},\varepsilon)
from
\tilde{z}_{0}
to
z_{0}
and if
\psi
is a self homeomorphisms of
U
that is the identity outside
B(z_{1},\varepsilon)
and such that
\psi(z_{1})=\tilde{z}_{1}
then
[\tilde{\gamma}]=[\delta\cdot(\psi\circ\gamma)]
.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon_{0}
be the
\varepsilon
given by
Lemma

20
.
Choose any
\varepsilon>0
with
\varepsilon<\min(\varepsilon_{0}/4,|z_{1}-z_{0}|/2,d(\gamma,\partial U))
.
The relation to prove
[\tilde{\gamma}]=[\delta\cdot(\psi\circ\gamma)]
is equivalent to

[\psi^{-1}\circ(\delta^{-1}\cdot\tilde{\gamma})]=[\gamma]
.
We have
\psi^{-1}\circ(\delta^{-1}\cdot\tilde{\gamma})=(\psi^{-1}\circ\delta^{-1})\cdot(\psi^{-1}\circ\tilde{\gamma})
.
Since
\varepsilon<|z_{1}-z_{0}|/2
, it follows that
\psi^{-1}\circ\delta^{-1}=\delta^{-1}
.
By
Lemma

21
,
[\delta^{-1}\cdot(\psi^{-1}\circ\tilde{\gamma})]=[\alpha]
for some path
\alpha
with
d(\alpha,\psi^{-1}\circ\tilde{\gamma})<\varepsilon
.
We have
d(\psi^{-1}\circ\tilde{\gamma},\tilde{\gamma})<2\varepsilon
.
Hence
d(\alpha,\gamma)<4\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
so we can apply
Lemma

20
:
[\alpha]=[\gamma]
.
∎

Lemma 23
.

If
[\gamma]=[\gamma^{\prime}]
then for all
\varepsilon^{\prime}<d(\gamma^{\prime},\partial U)
, there exists
\varepsilon<d(\gamma,\partial U)
such that
V_{\gamma,\varepsilon}\subset V_{\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Indeed take
\varepsilon<\min(\varepsilon^{\prime}/3,d(\gamma,\partial U),|z_{1}-z_{0}|)
.
Consider any
[\tilde{\gamma}]\in V(\gamma,\varepsilon)
, for which by definition we can take a representative
\tilde{\gamma}
with
d(\tilde{\gamma},\gamma)<\varepsilon
.
By
Lemma

22
(explicit objects
\delta
and
\psi
exist that satisfy the hypotheses), we have
[\tilde{\gamma}]=[\delta\cdot(\psi\circ\gamma)]
.
By post-composing a homotopy from
\gamma
to
\gamma^{\prime}
with
\psi
we get that
[\psi\circ\gamma]=[\psi\circ\gamma^{\prime}]
.
Hence
[\tilde{\gamma}]=[\delta\cdot(\psi\circ\gamma^{\prime})]
.
By
Lemma

21
,
[\delta\cdot(\psi\circ\gamma^{\prime})]
is homotopic in
\cal G(\tilde{z}_{0},\tilde{z}_{1})
to a path
\alpha
with
d(\alpha,\psi\circ\gamma^{\prime})<\varepsilon<\varepsilon^{\prime}/3
.
Moreover,
d(\psi\circ\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})<2\varepsilon<2\varepsilon^{\prime}/3
.
Hence
d(\alpha,\gamma^{\prime})<\varepsilon^{\prime}
.
∎

It follows from
Lemma

23
that if we define a “neighbourhood” of
[\gamma]
as a set that contains
V(\gamma,\varepsilon)
for some
\varepsilon>0
, then this definition is independent of the choice of representative
\gamma
of
[\gamma]
.
Declare a subset of
\Gamma
“open” if for all of its points it contains a “neighbourhood” of it.

Note that “neighbourhoods” of
[\gamma]
exist, contain
[\gamma]
, the intersection of two “neighbourhoods” is still a “neighbourhood” (they both contain
V(\gamma,\varepsilon)
for the min of their
\varepsilon
).
It follows that the collection of “open” subsets of
\Gamma
forms a topology. We can thus remove the quotes on the word: open.
Moreover
V(\gamma,\varepsilon)
is open: all its elements are of the form
[\tilde{\gamma}]
with
d(\tilde{\gamma},\gamma)<\varepsilon
and thus
[\tilde{\gamma}]\in V(\tilde{\gamma},\varepsilon-d(\tilde{\gamma},\gamma))\subset V(\gamma,\varepsilon)
.
It follows that our notion of “neighbourhood” coincides with the notion of neighbourhood associated to the topology: we can remove the quotes here too.

Lemma 24
.

This topology is Hausdorff separated.

Proof.

If
(z^{\prime}_{0},z^{\prime}_{1})\neq(z_{0},z_{1})
then it is enough to take
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}\max(|z^{\prime}_{0}-z_{0}|,|z^{\prime}_{1}-z_{1}|)
for
V_{\varepsilon,\gamma}\cap V_{\varepsilon,\gamma^{\prime}}=\varnothing
to hold.

If
(\tilde{z}_{0},\tilde{z}_{1})=(z_{0},z_{1})
but
[\tilde{\gamma}]\neq[\gamma]
then consider the value
\varepsilon
that
Lemma

22
associates to
\gamma
and the value
\varepsilon^{\prime}
that it associates to
\gamma^{\prime}
.
Let
\varepsilon_{0}=\min(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime},|z_{1}-z_{0}|/2)
.
Then we claim that
V_{\varepsilon_{0},\gamma}\cap V_{\varepsilon_{0},\gamma^{\prime}}=\varnothing
.
Otherwise there are two paths
\tilde{\gamma}
and
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}
with
[\tilde{\gamma}]=[\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}]
and
d(\gamma,\tilde{\gamma})<\varepsilon_{0}
and
d(\gamma^{\prime},\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime})<\varepsilon_{0}
.
Let
\delta
be a segment from
\tilde{\gamma}(0)=\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}(0)
to
z_{0}=\gamma(0)=\gamma^{\prime}(0)
and
\psi
be a self homeomorphism of
U
that sends
\tilde{z}_{1}=\tilde{\gamma}(1)=\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}(1)
to
z_{1}=\gamma(1)=\gamma^{\prime}(1)
and is the identity outside
B(z_{1},\varepsilon)
.
Then by
Lemma

22
,
[\tilde{\gamma}]=[\delta\cdot(\psi\circ\gamma)]
from which it follows that
[\gamma]=[\delta^{-1}\cdot(\psi^{-1}\circ\tilde{\gamma})]
.
Similarly

[\gamma^{\prime}]=[\delta^{-1}\cdot(\psi^{-1}\circ\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime})]
.
From the homotopy between
\tilde{\gamma}
and
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}
one defines a homotopy between
[\delta^{-1}\cdot(\psi^{-1}\circ\tilde{\gamma})]
and
[\delta^{-1}\cdot(\psi^{-1}\circ\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime})]
.
hence
[\gamma]=[\gamma^{\prime}]
, leading to a contradiction.
∎

Consider the map

(z_{0},z_{1},[\gamma])\in\Gamma\mapsto(z_{0},z_{1})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}.

It is continuous: indeed on
V(\gamma,\varepsilon)
it takes values in
B(z_{0},\varepsilon)\times B(z_{1},\varepsilon)
.

Lemma 25
.

For the
\varepsilon
of
Lemma

22
the map above is a homeomorphism from
V(\gamma,\varepsilon)
to
B(z_{0},\varepsilon)\times B(z_{1},\varepsilon)
.

Proof.

We saw continuity.
Injectivity follows from
Lemma

22
: for two
\tilde{\gamma}
with the same extremities
\tilde{z}_{0}
and
\tilde{z}_{1}
we can choose the same
\delta
and the same
\psi
.
Surjectivity can be achieved by letting
\tilde{\gamma}=\delta\cdot(\psi\circ\gamma)
, choosing any
\delta
from
\tilde{z}_{0}
to
z_{0}
and choosing
\psi
sending
z_{1}
to
\tilde{z}_{1}
so that it moves each point of
B(z_{1},\varepsilon)
by at most
\varepsilon
(this is possible for the Euclidean distance
25
25

25

This may look specific to the distance we are working with on the plane. The reader may find more satisfying the weaker statement that for the
\varepsilon
of
Lemma

22
, the map above is a homeomorphism from an open subset of
V(\gamma,\varepsilon)
containing
\gamma
to
B(z_{0},\varepsilon)\times B(z_{1},\varepsilon/2)
. Then any
\psi
works.
).
Continuity of the inverse is achieved by definining explicit
\delta
(a straight segment) and
\psi
that continuously depend on respectively
\tilde{z}_{0}
and
\tilde{z}_{1}
(i.e. we have explicit sections taking values in
V(\gamma,\varepsilon)
).
∎

Consider the natural surjective map

Q:\cal G\to\Gamma

that sends
\gamma
to
(z_{0},z_{1},[\gamma])
.

Lemma 26
.

The map
Q
is continuous and open.

Proof.

Let
B(\gamma,\varepsilon)
denote the ball of center
\gamma
and radius
\varepsilon
for the distance
d
on
\cal G
.

The map
Q
is open: The elements
V(\gamma,\varepsilon)
form a basis of open neighbourhoods for
\Gamma
and
V(\gamma,\varepsilon)=Q(B(\gamma,\varepsilon))
.

The map
Q
is continuous: It is equivalent to: for all
\gamma\in\cal G
for all neighbourhood of
Q(\gamma)
, its preimage by
Q
is a neighbourhood of
\gamma
. This is immediate as we saw that the sets
V(\gamma,\varepsilon)
forms a basis of neighbourhoods at
[\gamma]
and that
B(\gamma,\varepsilon)\subset Q^{-1}(V(\gamma,\varepsilon))
.
∎

As a consequence we obtain the following alternative presentation of the topology.

Corollary 27
.

The topology we defined on
\Gamma
coincides with the quotient topology under
Q
.

Appendix E Teichmüller space

We only focus here on the Teichmüller space of a sphere with marked points.

There are several points of view on Teichmüller spaces:
sometimes they are seen as spaces of hyperbolic metrics, or spaces of conformal structures, spaces of deformations, spaces of marked points, etc.
Most references on Teichmüller space
26
26

26

There are a lot of books treating the subject. See for instance
[
GL00
,
Hub06
]
.
study this space using quasiconformal maps and the Measurable Riemann Mapping Theorem (MRMT), and its version with holomorphic dependence. But in the present article, we want to prove the MRMT’s, so we cannot follow this approach.
Fortunately, for the study of the Teichmüller space of the sphere with marked points, the MRMT is not required to define and prove the basic properties that we need in the present article.
In particular, instead of defining the Teichmüller space via equivalence classes of quasiconformal maps, we use equivalence classes of homeomorphisms.
27
27

27

The proof of some classical theorems that we use about spaces of homeomorphisms may look hard. It is likely that the use of another space of maps, like smooth maps, would make the theory a bit easier, but we have not checked that.

All the material here is classical, but it is hard to find references not involving the use of quasiconformal maps.

Let
\cal S^{0}
be a topological sphere and
v_{k}^{0}
for
1\leq k\leq m
be
m
distinct points of
\cal S^{0}
.
We assume that

m\geq 3.

Let
V^{0}
denote the
m
-uplet
(v_{k}^{0})
and
\cal V^{0}
denote the set
\{v_{k}^{0}\}
.

Let
\cal F
be the set of orientation preserving homeomorphisms
h:\cal S^{0}\to\hat{\mathbb{C}}
such that

h(v_{1}^{0})=0,\ h(v_{2}^{0})=1\text{ and }h(v_{3}^{0})=\infty.

Let us endow
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
with a spherical metric
28
28

28

A metric for which the circles are geometric circles via a stereographic projection.

d
and the set
\cal F
with the metric

d(f_{1},f_{2})=\sup\big\{{d(f_{1}(x),f_{2}(x))}\,\big|\,{x\in\cal S^{0}}\big\}.

This defines a topology on
\cal F
.
The space
\cal F
is path connected
[
Kne26
]
, in particular connected.

Let
\cal H^{0}
be the group of orientation preserving self homeomorphisms of
\cal S^{0}
that are isotopic to the identity rel.
\cal V^{0}
, i.e. by an isotopy fixing each marked point
v_{k}^{0}
.
It follows immediately from this definition that
\cal H^{0}
is path connected.
The group
\cal H^{0}
acts on the right on the set
\cal F
by composition:
for
\phi\in\cal H^{0}
and
f\in\cal F
we let
f\cdot\phi=f\circ\phi
.

Definition 28
.

The Teichmüller space
\cal T
associated to
\cal S^{0},V^{0}

is the quotient of
\cal F
for this action:

\cal T=\cal F/\cal H^{0}.

We endow
\cal T
with the quotient topology. We denote

\Pi:\cal F\to\cal T

the quotient map.

A useful lemma

Let us endow
\cal S^{0}
with a metric
d
compatible with its topology and the set
\cal H(\cal S^{0})
of self homeomorphisms
\phi
of
\cal S^{0}
with the metric

d(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})=\sup\big\{{d(\phi_{1}(x),\phi_{2}(x))}\,\big|\,{x\in\cal S^{0}}\big\}.

This endows
\cal H^{0}
with a topology,
29
29

29

The metric
d
is not complete on
\cal H^{0}
. A complete metric would be
d(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})+d(\phi_{1}^{-1},\phi_{2}^{-2})
. Yet, these two metrics induce the same topology on
\cal H^{0}
.
for which
it is a topological group.
30
30

30

By uniform continuity of
\phi\in\cal H^{0}
, left multiplication by
\phi
is continuous.
Right multiplication by
\phi
is an isometry.
It follows that multiplication is continuous.
Inversion is continuous at
\phi
by uniform continuity of
\phi^{-1}
.

We copy here for convenience
Lemma

13
:

Lemma 29
.

There exists
\varepsilon>0
such that for all orientation preserving self homeomorphism
\phi
of
\cal S^{0}
, if
\phi
fixes every point in
\cal V^{0}
and satisfies
d(\phi,\operatorname{id}_{\cal S^{0}})<\varepsilon
then
\phi\in\cal H^{0}
(i.e.
\phi
is isotopic to the identity rel.
\cal V^{0}
).

It follows for instance from
[
Rob55
]
, which states in particular that the space of self-homeomorphisms of
S^{2}
is locally arcwise connected, together with the use of an explicit correction to leave the points in
\cal V^{0}
fixed, for instance using the tool described later here.

Moduli space
\cal M

Let
\cal M
be the set of
m
-uplets
(z_{k})
of pairwise distinct points in
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
such that
z_{1}=0
,
z_{2}=1
and
z_{3}=\infty
.
It is a complex manifold of dimension
m-3
.
For
f\in\cal F
we define

M:\begin{array}[]{rcl}\cal F&\to&\cal M\\
f&\mapsto&(f(v_{k}^{0}))\end{array}

which is a continuous map.
There are many ways
31
31

31

We leave it as an exercise to the reader. Igor Belegradek pointed to us an interesting generalization that can be found in
[
AB18
]
.
to see that the map
M
is surjective, i.e. that for all
Z=(z_{k})\in\cal M
there exists an orientation preserving homeomorphism from
\cal S^{0}
to
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
sending each
v^{0}_{k}
to
z_{k}
.
We also define

\pi:\cal T\to\cal M

as follows: to
[f]\in\cal T
we associate
M(f)
, which does not depend on the representative
f
of
[f]
.
So

\pi\circ\Pi=M

Since
M
is surjective,
\pi
is surjective too.

The map
M
is open. Indeed, given
f\in\cal F
and
Z=(z_{k})\in\cal M
close to
M(f)
, it is very easy to construct a homeomorphism close to
f
and that send each
f(v^{0}_{k})
to
z_{k}
.

More topology

By definition of the quotient topology, open sets are subsets of
\cal T
whose preimages in
\cal F
are open.
This coincides with the projection in
\cal T
of open subsets of
\cal F

(i.e. the map
\Pi:\cal F\to\cal T
is open): indeed we have a group action of
\cal H^{0}
and for all
\phi\in\cal H^{0}
, the map
f\mapsto f\circ\phi
is continuous, and its inverse too, so is a homeomorphism, thus the saturation of an open set is open.
Hence:

Proposition 30
.

A basis of neighbourhoods of
[f]
in
\cal T
is given by the sets

\big\{{[g]}\,\big|\,{g\in\cal F,\ d(g,f)<\varepsilon}\big\}

where
f
is fixed and
\varepsilon>0
vary.

Figure 13.
Example of a homeomorphism
h_{a}
of a Euclidean disk in the plane that is the identity on its boundary, sends its center to
a
and depends continuously on
a
. It is defined by
h_{a}(re^{i\theta})=(1-r)a+re^{i\theta}
. For a closed disk on the Euclidean sphere, conjugacy of
h_{a}
by one’s preferred homeomorphism to a Euclidean disk can be used.

Note that these particular neighbourhoods are open since
\Pi
is open. Note also that they depend on
(f,\varepsilon)
and not just on
([f],\varepsilon)
.

Lemma 31
.

The quotient space
\cal T
is separated.

Proof.

Consider
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
in
\cal T
and
\varepsilon>0
.
Assume that the associated neighbourhoods defined in
Proposition

30
intersect.
Call them
V(f_{1},\varepsilon)
and
V(f_{2},\varepsilon)
.
This means that
[g_{1}]=[g_{2}]
for some
g_{i}
in
\cal F
with
d(g_{i},f_{i})<\varepsilon
.
Hence
g_{2}=g_{1}\circ h_{0}
with
h_{0}\in\cal H^{0}
.
In particular
g_{2}(v^{0}_{k})=g_{1}(v^{0}_{k})
.

Let us assume that
V(f_{1},\varepsilon)
and
V(f_{2},\varepsilon)
intersect for all
\varepsilon>0
.
So for all
\varepsilon>0
we get maps
g_{1}
,
g_{2}
and
h_{0}
as above, that depend on
\varepsilon
.
By passing
g_{2}(v^{0}_{k})=g_{1}(v^{0}_{k})
to the limit as
\varepsilon\longrightarrow 0
we get
f_{2}(v^{0}_{k})=f_{1}(v^{0}_{k})
for all
k
.
Now write
f_{2}=f_{1}\circ h
with
h:=f_{1}^{-1}\circ f_{2}
.
We have
h(v^{0}_{k})=f_{1}^{-1}(f_{2}(v^{0}_{k}))=v^{0}_{k}
.
Hence
h_{0}\circ h^{-1}
fixes all
v^{0}_{k}
too.
We also have
h_{0}\circ h^{-1}=(g_{1}^{-1}\circ f_{1})\circ h\circ(f_{2}^{-1}\circ g_{2})\circ h^{-1}
.
So by continuity of composition and inversion, the map
h_{0}\circ h^{-1}
is close to the identity when
\varepsilon
is small (
h
does not depend on
\varepsilon
).
It follows from
Lemma

29
that
h_{0}\circ h^{-1}\in\cal H^{0}
(i.e. it is isotopic to the identity rel
\cal V^{0}
).
Since
h_{0}\in\cal H^{0}
and
\cal H^{0}
is a group, we get
h\in\cal H^{0}
.
Since
f_{2}=f_{1}\circ h
, we get
[f_{2}]=[f_{1}]
.
∎

Complex structure on
\cal T

Recall that

\cal F\overset{\Pi}{\longrightarrow}\cal T\overset{\pi}{\longrightarrow}\cal M.

Lemma 32
.

The map
\pi
is a local homeomorphism.

Proof.

The map
\pi
is continuous: indeed, given an open subset
U
of
\cal M
, we have
\pi^{-1}(U)=\Pi\circ M^{-1}(U)
(
\Pi
is surjective), and since
\Pi
is open and
M
continuous, it follows that
\pi^{-1}(U)
is open.

The map
\pi
is also open: indeed, consider an open subset
O\subset\cal T
. By continuity of
\Pi
, the set
O^{\prime}=\Pi^{-1}(O)
is open. Since
\pi(O)=M(O^{\prime})
(
\Pi
is surjective) and
M
is open, it follows that
\pi(O)
is open.

There remains to check that the map
\pi
is locally injective.
That is, using the neighbourhoods of
Proposition

30
, to check that for every
f
, there is
\varepsilon>0
small enough such that for all
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
with
d(f_{i},f)<\varepsilon
, if
\pi([f_{1}])=\pi([f_{2}])
then
[f_{1}]=[f_{2}]
.

So we assume
\pi([f_{1}])=\pi([f_{2}])
, i.e.
M(f_{1})=M(f_{2})
.
Let us write
f_{i}=f\circ\phi_{i}
with
\phi_{i}:=f^{-1}\circ f_{i}\in\cal H(\cal S^{0})
.
Then the hypothesis
M(f_{1})=M(f_{2})
says that
\phi_{1}=\phi_{2}
on
\cal V_{0}
.
Since
\phi_{i}=f^{-1}\circ f_{i}
, by uniform continuity of
f^{-1}
, for all
\varepsilon^{\prime}
we can choose
\varepsilon>0
such that
d(\phi_{i},\operatorname{id}_{\cal S^{0}})<\varepsilon^{\prime}
.
The map
\phi_{2}^{-1}\circ\phi_{1}
fixes every point of
\cal V_{0}
and is close to the identity, hence by
Lemma

29
belongs to
\cal H_{0}
provided we chose
\varepsilon^{\prime}
small enough.
Since
f_{1}=f_{2}\circ\phi_{2}^{-1}\circ\phi_{1}
, we get
[f_{1}]=[f_{2}]
.
∎

The fact that
\pi:\cal T\to\cal M
is a local homeomorphism allows to endow
\cal T
with a complex manifold atlas, of dimension
m-3
, for which
\pi
is analytic.

Actually the map
\pi
is a covering (better: a universal covering) but we do not use this fact in the present article.

A useful tool

Consider an indexed set of points
Z^{*}=(z_{k}^{*})\in\cal M
(so
z^{*}_{1}
,
z^{*}_{2}
and
z^{*}_{3}
are fixed to
0
,
1
and
\infty
) and recall that we have put a spherical metric on
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
.
Choose
\varepsilon>0
so that the balls
B_{k}=B(z_{k}^{*},\varepsilon)
, for
k\geq 4
, are pairwise disjoint.
The product

U=\{0\}\times\{1\}\times\{\infty\}\times\prod_{k\geq 4}B_{k}

is an open subset of
\cal M
.
The set
B_{k}
is a spherical disk on the Riemann sphere.
It is easy to construct an explicit orientation preserving homeomorphism of the disk
B_{k}
such that
z_{k}^{*}
is mapped to a given
z_{k}\in B_{k}
and that depends continuously on
z_{k}
. See for instance
Figure

13
.
By taking the join over
k
and completing with the identity, we obtain an orientation preserving homeomorphism

\psi_{Z}:\hat{\mathbb{C}}\to\hat{\mathbb{C}}

that depends continuously on
Z=(z_{k})\in U
and such that

-

\psi_{Z}(z^{*}_{k})=z_{k}
for all
k
,

-

\psi_{Z^{*}}=\operatorname{Id}
,

-

d(\psi_{Z},\operatorname{Id})\leq 2\varepsilon
and
d(\psi_{Z}^{-1},\operatorname{Id})\leq 2\varepsilon
.

This family depends on
Z^{*}
and
\varepsilon
but we chose to omit them in the notation
\psi_{Z}
.

Proposition 33
.

The map
\Pi
is a fibre bundle.

Proof.

Let
\tau^{*}=[f^{*}]\in\cal T
.
We saw in
Proposition

30
that for any
\eta>0
, the set
V=\big\{{[f^{\prime}]}\,\big|\,{f^{\prime}\in\cal F,d(f^{\prime},f^{*})<\eta}\big\}
is an open a neighbourhood of
\tau
.
Let
Z^{*}=M(f^{*})=\pi(\tau^{*})
and for
\varepsilon
small enough consider the set
U\subset\cal M
and the family
\psi_{Z}
,
Z\in U
, introduced above.
We choose
\eta=\varepsilon/2
, so
\eta<\varepsilon
.
Then

\forall\tau\in V,\ \pi(\tau)\in U.

Consider the continuous map

\Theta:\begin{array}[]{rcl}V\times\cal H^{0}&\to&\cal F\\
(\tau,h)&\mapsto&\psi_{\pi(\tau)}\circ f^{*}\circ h\end{array}

We claim that
\Theta
is a local trivialization of
\Pi
provided
\varepsilon
is small enough.

We must first check that
\Pi\circ\Theta(\tau,h)=\tau
.
We have
\Pi\circ\Theta(\tau,h)=[\psi_{\pi(\tau)}\circ f^{*}\circ h]
and
\tau=[f^{\prime}]
for some
f^{\prime}\in\cal F
with
d(f^{\prime},f^{*})<\eta<\varepsilon
.
We must check that
(f^{\prime})^{-1}\circ\psi_{\pi(\tau)}\circ f^{*}\in\cal H^{0}
.
Note that this composition fixes every point in
\cal V^{0}
.
By
Lemma

29
is is enough to prove that the composition is close enough to the identity.
Now,
d(f^{\prime},f^{*})<\eta<\varepsilon
and
d(\psi_{\pi(\tau)},\operatorname{Id})<\varepsilon
so by continuity of composition and inversion, if
\varepsilon
is small enough,
(f^{\prime})^{-1}\circ\psi_{\pi(\tau)}\circ f^{*}
is close to
(f^{*})^{-1}\circ\operatorname{Id}\circ f^{*}=\operatorname{Id}
.

Then we must check that the image of
\Theta
is equal to
\Pi^{-1}(V)
(the latter is automatically an open set since
\Pi
is continuous).
By the previous paragraph it is contained in
\Pi^{-1}(V)
.
Let us prove the converse inclusion.
Let
f\in\cal F
with
\Pi(f)\in V
, i.e. there exists
f^{\prime}\in\cal F
and
h^{0}\in\cal H^{0}
such that
d(f^{\prime},f^{*})<\eta
and
f=f^{\prime}\circ h^{0}
.
Let
\tau=[f]
, so
\pi(\tau)=M(f)\in U
.
Then
f=\psi_{\pi(\tau)}\circ f^{*}\circ g
for the map
g=(f^{*})^{-1}\circ\psi_{M(f)}^{-1}\circ f=(f^{*})^{-1}\circ\psi_{M(f)}^{-1}\circ f^{\prime}\circ h^{0}
and because
\cal H_{0}
is a group, it is enough to check that

(f^{*})^{-1}\circ\psi_{M(f)}^{-1}\circ f^{\prime}\in\cal H^{0}.

This is checked using
Lemma

29
exactly as in the previous paragraph.

Last, the reciprocal of
\Theta
is given by

\Xi:\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Pi^{-1}(V)&\to&V\times\cal H^{0}\\
f&\mapsto&([f],(f^{*})^{-1}\circ\psi_{M(f)}^{-1}\circ f)\end{array}

which is a continuous expression.
∎

Fibre bundles have in particular the homotopy lifting property and local sections. Let us make this last statement explicit.

Proposition 34
.

For all
\tau^{*}\in\cal T
, for all representative
f^{*}
of
\tau
(i.e.
\tau=[f^{*}]
), there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that for the open neighbourhood
V=\big\{{[f^{\prime}]}\,\big|\,{f^{\prime}\in\cal F,d(f^{\prime},f^{*})<\eta}\big\}
of
\tau
and for the family
Z\mapsto\psi_{Z}
described before
Proposition

33
,
the following map is a local section of
\Pi
:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}V&\to&\cal F\\
\tau&\mapsto&\psi_{\pi(\tau)}\circ f^{*}\end{array}

Part II

def:be
Given
\mu
in the unit ball of
\cal B
, and
z\mapsto\mu(z)
a representative, a function
f:{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
is called a
solution of the Beltrami equation associated to
\mu
if:

-

f
is a homeomorphism from
{\mathbb{C}}
to
{\mathbb{C}}
,

-

the distribution derivative of
f
is locally
L^{2}
,

-

the following holds almost everywhere:
32
32

32

The notation
\partial f
and
\bar{\partial}f
is explained below.

\bar{\partial}f(z)=\mu(z)\,\partial f(z).

Such a map is called a
quasiconformal homeomorphism of
{\mathbb{C}}
and
\mu
is called the
Beltrami differential
of
f
. The map
f
is also said to
straighten

\mu
. Sometimes,
\mu
is also called a
Beltrami form
.

The second condition in the list means that the distribution partial derivatives
\partial f/\partial x
and
\partial f/\partial y
have locally
L^{2}
representatives
f_{x}
and
f_{y}
and we let

df=f_{x}dx+f_{y}dy

which is a
1
-form (a current), but a priori not the differential of
f
(see
1
).
There are too many good introductions and reference books to distributions to be cited here. Beyond the seminal
[
Sch66
]
, let us cite
[
GS68
]
and
[
LL01
]
. Let us also cite
[
Str94
]
, which is pleasant to read (but is, on purpose, sweeping details under the carpet).
See
Section

5.4
for information on distributions with locally
L^{1}
or locally
L^{2}
derivatives.

Bear in mind that we are asking that
\mu
is in the unit ball of
\cal B
and that this means that there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
|\mu(z)|\leq 1-\varepsilon
for almost every
z
.

Remark
.

In his definition of a quasiconformal map, Definition B
′
in
[
Ahl06
]
page 19,
Ahlfors only asks
df
to be locally
L^{1}
.
He proves, and this is remarkable, (bottom of page 18) that
df
is actually locally
L^{2}
, so
is equivalent.
However, we will not need that implication here and we will work directly with the
L^{2}
version of the definition, which is also the most commonly used.

In the third condition
the complex numbers
\partial f(z)
and
\bar{\partial}f(z)
are defined by

df(z)=\partial f(z)\cdot dz+\bar{\partial}f(z)\cdot\overline{dz}

where
\cdot
is the multiplication of two complex numbers. This coincides with the classical operators
\partial
and
\bar{\partial}
if
f
is
C^{1}
.
See
for a geometric interpretation of the ratio
\bar{\partial}f/\partial f
.
The usual partial derivatives of
f
and the quantities above are related as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phantom{i(}\partial f+\bar{\partial}f

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{y}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i(\partial f-\bar{\partial}f)

\displaystyle\partial f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x}}-i\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{y}}\right)

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x}}+i\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{y}}\right)

Remark 1
.

Note that we did
not
claim that
f
is differentiable almost everywhere. It turns out that it is true (
[
Ahl06
]
, Lemma 1 page 17).
We believe that this result is not needed to prove our main result, but that it is needed when one want to prove useful properties of quasiconformal maps in order to actually use them for deformation, surgeries, and other applications.
Note also that we did
not
define
df
as the differential of
f
in the classical sense, but via the partial derivatives of
f
. Of course in the particular case where
f
is
C^{1}
then this coincides with the classical differential.

Theorem 2

(Measurable Riemann Mapping Theorem, existence)
.

The Beltrami equation
\bar{\partial}f=\mu\,\partial f
has a solution.

It is due to Gauss in the case
\mu
is real-analytic, to Lavrentiev in the continuous case, to Morrey in the general case, with alternative proofs by Bers, Nirenberg, Boyarskii and others. These authors possibly use other formulations of the Beltrami equation. See the introduction to the present article and
[
Glu08
]
for more references.

Definition 36
.

A solution of the Beltrami equation is called
normalized
if moreover

-

f(0)=0
and
f(1)=1
.

From any solution
f
, one easily defines a normalized solution

\tilde{f}(z)=\frac{f(z)-f(0)}{f(1)-f(0)}.

Theorem 3

(Uniqueness, not proved here)
.

The normalized solutions of the equation
\bar{\partial}f=\mu\,\partial f
are unique.

The proof of
Theorem

3
is out of the scope of the present article, see
[
Ahl06
]
, Theorem 2 page 16 together with the implication “
B\implies A
” page 20.

Definition 37
.

We will say that a function
\mu:t\in{\mathbb{D}}\mapsto\mu_{t}\in\cal B
is holomorphic if there is a power series expansion
\mu_{t}=\sum t^{n}a_{n}
, where
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
a_{n}\in\cal B
, whose radius of convergence is at least one, i.e.
\limsup\|a_{n}\|^{1/n}\leq 1
.

In this definition the sum is assumed to converge in the Banach space
\cal B
. For all
t\in{\mathbb{D}}
,
\mu_{t}
is a well-defined element of
\cal B
, in particular it is an equivalence class of
L^{\infty}
function from
{\mathbb{C}}
to
{\mathbb{C}}
.
For the reader’s interest, equivalent definitions of holomorphy are given in
Section

5.4
, but not used in this article.

Theorem 4

(Holomorphic dependence)
.

Under the holomorphy condition of
Definition

37
,
assuming moreover that for all
t\in{\mathbb{D}}
,
\|\mu_{t}\|_{\infty}<1
,
then there is a function
(t,z)\in{\mathbb{D}}\times{\mathbb{C}}\mapsto f_{t}(z)\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that:

(1)

for every
t\in{\mathbb{D}}
,
f_{t}
is a normalized solution of the Beltrami equation associated to
\mu_{t}
,

(2)

for every
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
, the function
t\in{\mathbb{D}}\mapsto f_{t}(z)\in{\mathbb{C}}
is holomorphic.

This is due to Ahlfors and Bers
[
AB60
]
, and possibly others. They also studied other kind of smooth dependence. Of course by
Theorem

3
,
f_{t}
is unique, so in fact the theorem can be stated as follows: the unique normalized solution
f_{t}
is, pointwise in
z
, holomorphic in
t
.

In this section we propose a proof of
Theorem

2
and of
Theorem

4
which uses the density argument and similarity surfaces.
The maps
f
of
Theorem

2
and
f_{t}
of
Theorem

4
will be obtained as extracted limits of maps
f_{n}
