straightening Beltrami forms that are piecewise constant with polygonal pieces.
The proof bears resemblance with Lavrentiev’s approach
[
Lav35
,
Lav20
]
, which we sum up in
Section

5.1
.

Remark
.

Actually, using uniqueness (
Theorem

3
) one can prove that the full sequence
f_{n}
converge to the solution of the Beltrami equation. See
Section

5.4
.

Remark
.

This hence defines an approximation scheme
f_{n}\longrightarrow f
for solving the Beltrami equation.
However, we do not claim that this scheme is efficient.

5.1.
Lavrentiev’s approach

We sum up here
[
Lav35
]
, whose translation in English can be found in
[
Lav20
]
.

The Beltrami form
\mu
is assumed
continuous
.

A notion of
\varepsilon
-near-solution to the Beltrami equation is defined with a parameter
\varepsilon>0
, by asking that asymptotically near each point, small ellipses defined by
\mu
are nearly mapped to small circles, up to
\varepsilon
(see the precise definition in Lavrentiev’s article).
It is proved with relatively simple computations (including an equicontinuity statement on a class of
C^{1}
diffeomorphisms that are quasiconformal) that if
\varepsilon_{n}\longrightarrow 0
then a sequence of
\varepsilon_{n}
-near-solutions tends to a solution for
\mu
.

Then a sequence of near-solutions is constructed by

-

taking simple
\varepsilon
-near solutions defined on small squares: for instance by choosing
f:z\mapsto az+b\bar{z}
; this is one of the places where the continuity assumption on
\mu
is used;

-

proving that one can patch together these
\varepsilon
-near solutions into a global
\varepsilon
-near-solution; this is done by proving a sewing lemma (sewing two rectangles along an edge with a real and analytic sewing function) with an astute use of the Schwarz reflection principle.

In fact the second point could be proven by just invoking the Poincaré-Koebe theorem. What Lavrentiev does, besides the sewing lemma, is to argue (without giving details) that one can slightly modify the near-solutions before patching so that the sewing map is always analytic, while keeping a near-solution.

Moreover, if one chooses to takes the
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear functions
az+b\bar{z}
on small squares, finding a global sewing is equivalent to uniformizing the surface
\cal S
defined by gluing together linearly the many parallelograms that are the images of each square by the corresponding
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear function; though this is not what Lavrentiev does since he changes the maps slightly before each sewing.
The method that we are about to describe uses the uniformization of
\cal S
and our proof of analytic dependence uses knowledge about its expression via the Schwarz-Christoffel formula.

5.2.
The use of similarity surfaces

An introduction to similarity surfaces built from polygons is given in
.

Assume that we are given a Beltrami form
\mu
that is piecewise constant on
{\mathbb{C}}
, where the pieces are finitely many bounded or unbounded closed polygons
(Q_{j})_{j\in J}
without restriction, in particular they are not assumed convex nor simply connected, yet we assume that each has finitely many sides.
The polygons come with
vertices
and its boundary is straight between vertices but we allow for consecutive edges to make a flat angle (
\pi
radians), so it the set
Q_{j}
alone is not enough to determine the collection of its vertices.
We assume that if a point is a vertex for some
Q_{j}
then it is also a vertex of any other polygon that contains it
(in a polygonal decomposition, there could be a corner of one polygon
Q_{j}
belonging to the interior of the side of another
Q_{k}
; the required condition is easily achieved by just adding this point in the list of vertices of
Q_{k}
).
We make one more assumption: that no edge bounds the same polygon on its two sides.
This is easily achieved by removing any edge with this property.

To the unbounded edges
e
we add marked points, and we mark the same point of
e
for the two polygons on each side of
e
.

All our polygons are considered to be closed subsets of
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
.
We can associate to this a similarity surface as follows: for each polygon
Q_{j}
choose an
{\mathbb{R}}
-affine map
a_{j}
such that the Beltrami differential of
a_{j}
, which is constant, coincides with the value
\mu_{j}
of
\mu
on
Q_{j}
.
Now glue the polygons
P_{j}:=a_{j}(Q_{j})
along their sides the same way that the polygons
Q_{j}
were glued together, i.e. on each side with the affine map
a_{k}\circ a_{j}^{-1}
for appropriate
(k,j)
(the marked points are automatically matched: they are not needed here but later).

As in
Section

2
we call
\cal S
the topological surface thus obtained,

\pi:\coprod P_{j}=\coprod a_{j}(Q_{j})\to\cal S

the quotient map,
\cal V
the the set of vertices, and
\cal S^{\prime}=\cal S-\cal V
.
To avoid the problem where two polygons
P_{i}
and
P_{k}
intersect as subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
and
z
belongs to this intersection, we use the notation
(z:i)
and
(z:k)
to distinguish them in the disjoint union
\coprod P_{j}
. This is particularly useful since it is likely that
\pi(z:i)
and
\pi(z:k)
are different.

Let us recall how we defined in
Section

2
a similarity surface atlas on
\cal S^{\prime}
(see the part “Removing vertices and getting a similarity surface”; there are slight differences due to the fact that we allow here more kinds of polygons than there).
On the interior of
\pi(P_{j})
we just use the identity.
Now consider an edge
e
of
P_{j}
and let
P_{k}
be the polygon glued to
P_{j}
along
e
by
a_{k}\circ a_{j}^{-1}
. Denote
e^{*}
the edge minus its endpoints.
The map
a_{k}\circ a_{j}^{-1}
is a priori only
{\mathbb{R}}
-affine on
{\mathbb{C}}
.
There is a unique
{\mathbb{C}}
-affine map
s
that extends the restriction of
a_{k}\circ a_{j}^{-1}
to
e
.
Then a neighbourhood
V
of
\pi(e^{*}:j)
was identified,
of the form
V=\pi(V_{k}:k)\cup\pi(V_{j}:j)
for some open sets
V_{k}\subset P_{k}
and
V_{j}\subset P_{j}
that intersect
\partial P_{k}
resp.
\partial P_{j}
exactly on the corresponding edges minus endpoints.
In
Section

2
the polygons were convex so one could just take
V_{j}
to be the interior of
P_{j}
union
e^{*}
and similarly for
V_{k}
. Here we will have to take smaller subsets.
A chart
\phi:V\to{\mathbb{C}}
can be defined by
\phi(\pi(z:j))=z
if
z\in V_{j}
, and
\phi(\pi(z:k))=s^{-1}(z)
if
z\in V_{k}
, provided
V_{j}
and
V_{k}
are chosen so that
\phi(\pi(V_{j}:j))\cap\phi(\pi(V_{k}:k))=\phi(\pi(e^{*}:j))
, which is possible.
Note that its image
\phi(V)
is a neighbourhood of
e^{*}
in
{\mathbb{C}}
.
See
Figure

14
.

Q_{j}
Q_{k}
P_{j}
V_{j}
e
P_{k}
V_{k}
P_{j}
s^{-1}(P_{k})

Figure 14.
Left: two polygons
Q_{j}
,
Q_{k}
. Middle: images of these polygons by the
{\mathbb{R}}
-affine maps
a_{j}
and
a_{k}
. Right: straightened polygons glued along one edge, together with a neighbourhood in gray of the edge, yielding a chart.

This atlas on
\cal S^{\prime}
is in particular a Riemann surface atlas. Using
Section

2
we can see that all points in
\cal V
are punctures, including
\infty
, which allows to extend the Riemann atlas to
\cal S
.
Indeed, near finite vertices of
Q_{j}
we get what is called a bounded vertex in
Section

2
, and all bounded vertices are punctures according to the part called “Conformal erasability of the singularities”.
Near
\infty
, we have a positive total angle (negative signed angle), hence we also get a puncture as explained near the end of the part called “Unbounded polygons”.
Since
\cal S
is homeomorphic to a sphere, the
Poincaré Koebe theorem
implies that it is conformally isomorphic to the Riemann sphere
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
: call
F:\cal S\to\hat{\mathbb{C}}
such an isomorphism, chosen so that
\infty
is mapped to
\infty
.
Let now
f
be defined as follows: on each polygon
Q_{j}
,
f(z)=F\circ\pi(a_{j}(z):j)
. This definition coincides on the shared edges and vertices.

Lemma 38
.

The map
f
thus constructed is a solution of the Beltrami equation associated to
\mu
in the sense of
.

Proof.

See
Section

5.4
.
∎

We can normalize
f
(i.e. we can impose that
f(0)=0
and
f(1)=0
) by choosing the isomorphism
F
above appropriately.

Lemma 39
.

Fix the polygons
Q_{j}
and assume that no unbounded polygon has an infinite vertex with angle
0
.
Then the normalized solution
f
constructed above depends holomorphically on the family of values
(\mu_{j})\in{\mathbb{D}}^{J}
that
\mu
takes on the
Q_{j}
.

Proof.

It is enough to prove the claim locally, in the sense that
\mu
can be restricted to be in a small neighbourhood of a given
\mu^{0}
.

We can refine the polygons so that the conditions of
Section

4.4
are satisfied, which we recall here:

(1)

All polygons are strictly convex,

(2)

every unbounded polygon has exactly two unbounded edges, and their angle is
>0
,

(3)

\cal S
has at least three vertices.

Let us pick three vertices
w_{1}=\infty
and
w_{2}
and
w_{3}
in the initial polygonal decomposition of the plane
{\mathbb{C}}
where the Beltrami form
\mu
lives.
Number
w_{4}
to
w_{k}
the remaining ones.
They are independent of
\mu
.
Let
v_{k}
be the corresponding points in
\cal S
.
They depend on
\mu
since
\cal S
does.
This gives a consistent labelling of the set of vertices
\cal V
of
\cal S
.
The isomorphism
F:\cal S\to\hat{\mathbb{C}}
is normalized so that
f=F\circ\pi\circ\coprod_{j}a_{j}
fixes
0
,
1
and
\infty
.
We choose another isomorphism
\widehat{F}:\cal S\to\hat{\mathbb{C}}
by post-composing
F
with a homography (fixing
\infty
, so actually an affine map):
\widehat{F}=h\circ F
,
so that
\widehat{F}
sends
v_{1}
to
\infty
,
v_{2}
to
0
and
v_{3}
to
1
, or equivalently that
\hat{f}:=\widehat{F}\circ\pi\circ\coprod_{j}a_{j}=h\circ f
sends
w_{1}=\infty
to
\infty
,
w_{2}
to
0
and
w_{3}
to
1
.

It is enough to prove that:

(A)

\hat{f}
depends holomorphically on
\mu
.

Indeed,
h(0)=\hat{f}(0)
and
h(1)=\hat{f}(1)
vary holomorphically so the affine map
h
and its inverse
h^{-1}
have their coefficients that depend holomorphically on
\mu
, hence
\hat{f}(z)=h^{-1}\circ f(z)
depends holomorphically on
\mu
for a fixed
z
.

Let us prove claim (A). The affine maps
a_{j}
can be chosen so that the vertices (and marked points for unbounded polygons) of the polygons
a_{j}(Q_{j})
depend holomorphically on
\mu
:
for instance, one can choose
a_{j}(z)=z+\mu_{j}\overline{z}
.

The polygons
Q_{j}
define a cell complex on
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
.
Its 1-skeleton consists in its edges.
We call its image by
\hat{f}
the associated
1
-skeleton.
This is also the image by
\widehat{F}
of the edges of the cellular decomposition of
\cal S
by the
P_{j}
.

Consider now the Christoffel symbol associated to
\cal S
in the global chart
\hat{\mathbb{C}}-\widehat{F}(\cal V)={\mathbb{C}}-\widehat{F}(\cal V)
.
By
Theorem

1
, we have

\widehat{\zeta}(z)=\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k=1}}{{z_{k}\neq\infty}}}^{m}\frac{\operatorname{res}_{k}}{z-z_{k}}.

By
Lemmas

10
and
11
, the quantities
\operatorname{res}_{k}
and
z_{k}
depend holomorphically on
\mu
and by
Proposition

12
the associated
1
-skeleton moves holomorphically: we ask the reader to look at the paragraph after
Proposition

12
for the precise meaning.

The map
\hat{f}
sends linear parametrizations of the (straight) edges of the
Q_{j}
to affine parametrization of the saddle connections.
Let us by convenience denote

U_{j}=\hat{f}(\operatorname{Int}Q_{j}).

One of the solutions of the (complex) O.D.E.

(9)

\phi^{\prime\prime}/\phi^{\prime}=\hat{\zeta}

on the simply connected set
U_{j}
maps it to
a_{j}(\operatorname{Int}Q_{j})=\operatorname{Int}P_{j}
, and the other ones to images of this set by
{\mathbb{C}}
-affine maps.
Since by
Proposition

12
the set
U_{j}
moves continuously as
\mu
varies, and since the term
\hat{\zeta}
in the O.D.E. depends holomorphically on
\mu
, it follows that there is a solution
\phi
of
eq.

9
on
U_{j}
that depends holomorphically on
\mu
in a neighbourhood of
\mu_{0}
.
The inverse of
\widehat{F}\circ\pi\big|_{\operatorname{Int}P_{j}}
is also a solution, and by simple connectedness of
U_{j}
, there exists a
{\mathbb{C}}
-affine map
s
such that

(\widehat{F}\circ\pi\big|_{\operatorname{Int}P_{j}})^{-1}=s\circ\phi

holds on
U_{j}
.
Then
s(\phi(U_{j}))=a_{j}(\operatorname{Int}Q_{j})
.
Since the vertices and marked points of the polygon
a_{j}(Q_{j})
depend holomorphically on
\mu
, since the same holds for those of the polygon
\phi(U_{j})
(they are images by
\phi
of the
z_{k}
, which depend holomorphically on
\mu
), since the map
s
matches them and since there are at least two vertices or marked points (actually at least three), it follows that
s
depends holomorphically on
\mu
.
Then for
z\in Q_{j}
, we have
\hat{f}(z)=(s\circ\phi)^{-1}\circ a_{j}(z)
, which also depends holomorphically on
\mu
.
∎

Remark
.

Lemma

39
also holds in the presence of one or several unbounded polygons with zero angle at infinity, as will follow from the MRMT with parameter (theorem
Theorem

4
). But our approach required us to exclude this case.
If one wants to prove this without using
Theorem

4
one can proceed by perturbing the polygons
Q_{j}
so that all angles are strictly positive then taking limits of the solutions as some angles tend to
0
, and conclude by the fact that limits of holomorphic functions are holomorphic.
One has to prove bounds to justify that limits can be taken and that they are homeomorphisms straightening the intended Beltrami form.
Due to the simple form of the situation this should be doable by more direct and simpler methods than the proof of
Theorem

4
(in particular no need to use
Lemmas

42
and
43
, the use of Koebe’s distorsion theorems should suffice together with the explicit form of the maps
a_{j}
).
However, we will not do this here.

5.3.
The density argument, without parameter

We start with
Theorem

2
, i.e. without the parameter
t
, and explain in
Section

5.4
how to adapt the proof for holomorphic families
\mu_{t}
.

Remark
.

Density arguments like the one presented here have been known for a while.
A nice example is given in
[
Hub06
]
, Section 4.6, which presents a proof of the measurable Riemann mapping theorem, and Theorem 4.7.2.
Our presentation here is quite similar, with more details.
A slightly different type of density argument is performed in
[
Lav35
]
(translated in
[
Lav20
]
).

Approximation

We are given some
\mu\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}})
whose essential supremum is
<1
.
Let
\mu_{n}
be the following approximation of
\mu
:

-

divide the square defined by
|{\operatorname{Re}\,}z|<n
,
|{\operatorname{Im}\,}z|<n
into small squares of side
1/n
(there will be
4n^{4}
of them); let
\mu_{n}
be constant in the interior of each of those squares, equal to the average
33
33

33

About the choice of averaging, see
Section

8
.
of
\mu
on this square.

-

elsewhere let
\mu_{n}(z)=0
.

Note that

\|\mu_{n}\|\leq\|\mu\|.

Lemma 40
.

For all compact subset
S
of
{\mathbb{C}}
,

\|\mu_{n}-\mu\|_{L^{1}(S)}\longrightarrow 0.

Proof.

We may as well suppose that
S
is the square “
|{\operatorname{Im}\,}z|<N
,
|{\operatorname{Re}\,}z|<N
” with
N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
.
The Lebesgue differentiability theorem states that for any
L^{1}
function, a fortiori for
\mu
, almost every
z_{0}\in{\mathbb{C}}
is a Lebesgue continuity point in the following sense: the average
\operatorname{avg}(z_{0},r)
of
|\mu-\mu(z_{0})|
on
B(z_{0},r)
tends to
0
as
r\longrightarrow 0
.
Fix
\eta>0
temporarily.
For
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
let

r_{0}(z)=\sup\big\{{s\in[0,1]}\,\big|\,{\forall r\in(0,s),\ \operatorname{avg}(z,s)<\eta}\big\}\in[0,1]

(
s=0
is always in the set whose supremum is considered, because the interval
(0,0)
is empty).
This function is measurable and by the discussion above,
r_{0}\neq 0
almost everywhere.
So for all
\varepsilon>0
there exist
s_{0}=s_{0}(\varepsilon,\eta)>0
such that
\operatorname{Leb}(S\cap r_{0}^{-1}([0,s_{0}]))<\varepsilon
, i.e. there is a measurable
E=E(\varepsilon,\eta)\subset{\mathbb{C}}
such that
\operatorname{Leb}E<\varepsilon
and
r_{0}(z)>s_{0}(\varepsilon,\eta)
for all
z\in S-E
.
Recall that, to define
\mu_{n}
we divided the square
S
(actually a bigger square) into squares of side
1/n
.
Call them
little squares
.
Fix temporarily
\varepsilon>0
, and let
s_{0}
and
E
be as above.
Assume that
1/n<s_{0}/2
.
For all little square
S^{\prime}
not contained in
E
, consider any point
z^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}-E
.
Then
\operatorname{avg}(z^{\prime},2/n)<\eta
by definition of
E
, so we get that

\int_{B(z^{\prime},2/n)}|\mu-\mu(z^{\prime})|<\operatorname{Leb}B(z^{\prime},2/n)\eta=\pi(2/n)^{2}\eta
and since

S^{\prime}\subset B(z^{\prime},2/n)
we get that
\int_{S^{\prime}}|\mu-\mu(z^{\prime})|/\operatorname{Leb}S^{\prime}<4\pi\eta
.
Now

\left|\frac{\int_{S^{\prime}}\mu}{\operatorname{Leb}S^{\prime}}-\mu(z^{\prime})\right|\leq\frac{\int_{S^{\prime}}|\mu-\mu(z^{\prime})|}{\operatorname{Leb}S^{\prime}}

and by definition
\mu_{n}(z^{\prime})=\frac{\int_{S^{\prime}}\mu}{\operatorname{Leb}S^{\prime}}
, so we proved that
\forall n>2/s_{0}
,

\sup_{z\in S-E(\varepsilon)}|\mu_{n}(z)-\mu(z)|\leq 4\pi\eta.

On the other hand, since
\mu
has an
L^{\infty}
norm less than
1
, and hence
|\mu_{n}|<1
, we get the obvious bound

\mathrm{ess\ sup}_{z\in E(\varepsilon)}|\mu_{n}(z)-\mu(z)|\leq 2.

and summing up:
\forall n\geq 2/s_{0}(\varepsilon,\eta)
,

\int_{S}|\mu_{n}-\mu|\leq 4\pi\eta\operatorname{Leb}S+2\varepsilon.

∎

Usually local
L^{2}
convergence is stronger than local
L^{1}
convergence but here it is equivalent because the functions involved have uniformly bounded
L^{\infty}
norm:

Corollary 41
.

For all compact subset
S
of
{\mathbb{C}}
,

\|\mu_{n}-\mu\|_{L^{2}(S)}\longrightarrow 0.

Proof.

Almost everywhere
|\mu|\leq 1
, so
|\mu_{n}|\leq 1
, and almost everywhere

|\mu_{n}-\mu|^{2}\leq 2|\mu_{n}-\mu|
.
∎

Remark
.

It is true that
\mu_{n}
tends to
\mu
for the weak topology (against continuous maps with compact support), and the proof is easier (uniform continuity of the test function). It also follows from local
L^{1}
convergence. However,
Section

5.4
gives an example that shows that weak convergence is not enough to extract a correct solution for
\mu
from a sequence of solutions for
\mu_{n}
.

Let
f_{n}
be the normalized solution of the Beltrami equation for
\mu_{n}
constructed in
Section

5.2
.

Compactness statements

As announced in the introduction, we do not include here the proof of the following two statements, but indicate where they can be found in
[
Ahl06
]
and a few other references.

To match the terminology of references we say that a quasiconformal map
f
with
\|\mu\|\leq\kappa
is
K
-quasiconformal with
K=\frac{1+\kappa}{1-\kappa}
. The justification of this terminology comes from the geometric interpretations of quasiconformality, see
in the present article and
[
Ahl66
,
Ahl06
]
for complements.

Lemma 42

(Normal family)
.

For a fixed real
K>1
, the set of normalized
K
-quasiconformal homeomorphisms from
{\mathbb{C}}
to
{\mathbb{C}}
forms a compact family for the notion of uniform convergence on compact subsets of
{\mathbb{C}}
.

This is Theorem 2 page 33 in Chapter III of
[
Ahl06
]
. For the closely related case of quasiconformal maps from
{\mathbb{D}}
to
{\mathbb{D}}
or between hyperbolic Riemann surfaces, see
[
Hub06
]
Section 4.4.

Lemma 43

(
L^{2}
bound for
df
)
.

For any
K
-quasiconformal map
f
on an open subset
U
of
{\mathbb{C}}
,

\displaystyle\int_{U}\left|\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}\right|^{2}\leq K\operatorname{Leb}f(U),

\displaystyle\int_{U}\left|\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}\right|^{2}\leq K\operatorname{Leb}f(U).

This is a direct consequence of
[
Ahl06
]
, Chapter II, last line of page 18 and the formulas
\partial f/\partial x=\partial f+\bar{\partial}f
and
\partial f/\partial y=i(\partial f-\bar{\partial}f)
.
An alternative method can be found in
[
Ahl06
]
, via Theorem 3 page 22 (which uses Lemma 2 page 19 and the assumption that
f
has
L^{2}
distributional partial derivatives).
See also in
[
Hub06
]
, Proposition 4.2.4 and Corollary 4.2.6.

Extraction of a limit

From
Lemmas

42
and
43
it follows that we can extract a subsequence of
f_{n}
(more generally from any subsequence of
f_{n}
) such that:

(1)

f_{n}
uniformly converges on every compact subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
to a
K
-quasiconformal map
f
,

(2)

u_{n}=\partial f_{n}/\partial x
and
v_{n}=\partial f_{n}/\partial y
converge to some locally
L^{2}
functions
u
and
v
, for the weak convergence defined as follows: for all
L^{2}
function
\phi
with compact support,
\int(u_{n}-u)\phi\longrightarrow 0
and
\int(v_{n}-v)\phi\longrightarrow 0
.

The first claim is an immediate consequence of
Lemma

42
and the second claim is obtained by a further extraction justified as follows: for any (compact) rectangle
R\subset U
, by the first claim, the area of
f_{n}(R)
is a bounded sequence. Hence the
L^{2}
norm on
R
of
\partial f_{n}/\partial x
and
\partial f_{n}/\partial y
are bounded sequences too by
Lemma

43
.
By the weak-star compactness theorem for
L^{2}(R)
we can extract a subsequence such that the partial derivatives converge weakly in
L^{2}(R)
. By varying the rectangle and performing a diagonal extraction, the second claim follows.

Final checks

Let us first check that
u
and
v
are the distribution derivatives of the extracted limit
f
. For any test function
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{C}})
, denote by
S
its support

\left\langle\frac{\partial f_{n}}{\partial x},\phi\right\rangle=\left\langle-f_{n},\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial x}\right\rangle=\int_{S}-f_{n}\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial x}.

On one hand by uniform convergence of
f_{n}
to
f
on
S
, as
n\to+\infty

\int_{S}-f_{n}\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial x}\longrightarrow\int_{S}-f\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial x}=D_{x}f(\phi)

where
D_{x}f
denotes the distribution partial derivative of
f
.
Hence

\left\langle\frac{\partial f_{n}}{\partial x},\phi\right\rangle\longrightarrow D_{x}f(\phi).

On the other hand by weak convergence on
S
of
\partial f_{n}/\partial x
to
u
:

\left\langle\frac{\partial f_{n}}{\partial x},\phi\right\rangle=\int_{S}\frac{\partial f_{n}}{\partial x}\,\phi\longrightarrow\int_{S}u\,\phi.

So we proved that for all test functions
\phi
,

D_{x}f(\phi)=\int_{S}u\,\phi.

In other words the distribution derivative
\partial f/\partial x
is represented by the locally
L^{2}
(hence locally
L^{1}
) function
u
.
The proof for
\partial f/\partial y
and
v
is similar.

There remains to check that
f
is a solution of the Beltrami equation associated to
\mu
.
Let
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{C}})
. We know that

\left\langle\partial f_{n},\phi\right\rangle=\left\langle\mu_{n}\bar{\partial}f_{n},\phi\right\rangle

where by
\partial f_{n}
,
\bar{\partial}f_{n}
we refer to the
L^{2}
functions.
The fact that

\left\langle\partial f_{n},\phi\right\rangle\longrightarrow\left\langle\partial f,\phi\right\rangle

follows from the formulas
\partial f_{n}=(u_{n}-iv_{n})/2
,
\partial f=(u-iv)/2
and the weak convergence of
u_{n}
,
v_{n}
to
u
,
v
. We will be done if we can prove that

\left\langle\mu_{n}\bar{\partial}f_{n},\phi\right\rangle\longrightarrow\left\langle\mu\,\bar{\partial}f,\phi\right\rangle
.
For this let us write

\left\langle\mu_{n}\bar{\partial}f_{n}-\mu\,\bar{\partial}f,\phi\right\rangle=\underbrace{\left\langle(\mu_{n}-\mu)\bar{\partial}f_{n},\phi\right\rangle}_{\displaystyle A}+\underbrace{\left\langle(\bar{\partial}f_{n}-\bar{\partial}f)\mu,\phi\right\rangle}_{\displaystyle B}.

Let
S
be the support of
\phi
.

The function
\mu\phi
is
L^{\infty}
with support contained in
S
, hence
L^{2}
with support contained in
S
, so by the local weak
L^{2}
convergence, we have
B\longrightarrow 0
.

Concerning

A=\int_{S}(\mu-\mu_{n})\bar{\partial}f_{n}\phi

by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality

|A|\leq\left\|\mu-\mu_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}(S)}\times\|\bar{\partial}f_{n}\phi\|_{L^{2}(S)}.

Now
\|\mu-\mu_{n}\|_{L^{2}(S)}\longrightarrow 0
according to
Corollary

41
,

\|\bar{\partial}f_{n}\phi\|_{L^{2}(S)}\leq\max|\phi|\times\|\bar{\partial}f_{n}\|_{L^{2}(S)}
and we have seen that
\|\bar{\partial}f_{n}\|_{L^{2}(S)}
is a bounded sequence (
Lemma

43
).
It follows that
A\longrightarrow 0
as
n\to+\infty
.

Note that if we only had extracted a weakly convergent sequence in
L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}
, we could still argue that
B\longrightarrow 0
but not
A
, because instead of Cauchy-Schwarz we would use
|A|\leq\left\|\mu-\mu_{n}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(S)}\times\|\bar{\partial}f_{n}\phi\|_{L^{1}(S)}
but

\|\mu-\mu_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(S)}
is unlikely to tend to
0
.

5.4.
Holomorphic dependence

We now explain how to adapt the proofs in
Section

5.3
to get
Theorem

4
.

We start from the hypothesis that
\forall t\in{\mathbb{D}}
,
\|\mu_{t}\|_{\infty}<1
and improve this inequality as follows:

Lemma 44
.

\forall\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
\exists\kappa(\varepsilon)<1
such that

\forall t\in B(0,1-\varepsilon),\ \|\mu_{t}\|_{\infty}\leq\kappa(\varepsilon).

Proof.

By hypothesis
\mu_{t}=\sum t^{k}a_{k}
with
\limsup\|a_{k}\|_{\infty}^{1/k}\leq 1
.
It follows that
t\mapsto\|\mu_{t}\|_{\infty}
is continuous on
{\mathbb{D}}
.
It thus reaches a maximum on
\overline{B}(0,1-\varepsilon)
, and at this point its value is
<1
by hypothesis.
∎

Let the Beltrami form
\mu_{n}(t)
be defined from
\mu(t)
exactly as in
Section

5.3
.
Denote the square
S_{n}:
“
|{\operatorname{Re}\,}z|<n
,
|{\operatorname{Im}\,}z|<n
” and call
little square
the squares of side
1/n
into which we have cut it.
In a little square
S^{\prime}
we have

\mu_{n}(t)=\frac{\int_{S^{\prime}}\mu_{t}}{\operatorname{Leb}S^{\prime}}=\int_{S^{\prime}}\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}t^{k}\frac{a_{k}}{\operatorname{Leb}S^{\prime}}=\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}t^{k}\frac{\int_{S^{\prime}}a_{k}}{\operatorname{Leb}S^{\prime}}

and the convergence of the series is normal over
t\in B(0,1-\varepsilon)
for all
\varepsilon>0
since
\limsup\|a_{k}\|_{\infty}^{1/k}\leq 1
.
This quantity is a holomorphic function of
t\in{\mathbb{D}}
for all little squares
S^{\prime}
.

By
Lemma

39
there are normalized solutions
z\mapsto f_{n}(t,z)
of the Beltrami equation for
\mu_{n}(t)
, which vary holomorphically with
t
for all fixed
n,z
.

Corollary 45
.

For all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
, the sequence of holomorphic functions
t\in{\mathbb{D}}\mapsto f_{n}(t,z)
is equicontinuous.

Proof.

Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.
For
t\in B(0,1-\varepsilon)
, we have
\|\mu_{n}(t)\|_{\infty}\leq\|\mu_{t}\|_{\infty}\leq\kappa(\varepsilon)<1

by
Lemma

44
, so for all
t\in B(0,1-\varepsilon)
and all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the functions
z\mapsto f_{n}(t,z)
are
K
-quasiconformal for the same
K=(1+\kappa(\varepsilon))/(1-\kappa(\varepsilon))
.
By
Lemma

42
they form a normal family with respect to the variable
z
.
In particular for all compact subset
C
of
{\mathbb{C}}
, there is a bound
M>0
such that
\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\forall t\in B(0,1-|\varepsilon|)
, and
\forall z\in C
,
|f_{n}(t,z)|\leq M
.
In particular, for all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
, the sequence of holomorphic functions
t\in B(0,1-\varepsilon)\mapsto f_{n}(t,z)
is bounded, hence equicontinuous.
Since this is valid for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, we get the result.
∎

We will give two slightly different ways to conclude.
The first one avoids using uniqueness of the normalized solution of the Beltrami equation (
Theorem

3
).

Method 1.

By
Section

5.3
for each fixed
t
, and every subsequence of the sequence of functions
z\mapsto f_{n}(t,z)
, there is a sub-subsequence that tends uniformly on compact subsets of
{\mathbb{C}}
to a solution of the Beltrami equation for
\mu(t)
.
Consider a dense countable subset
T
of
{\mathbb{D}}
. By a diagonal extraction there is an infinite subset
I\subset{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
t\in T
, if
n\in I\longrightarrow+\infty
then the function
z\mapsto f_{n}(t,z)
converges uniformly on compact subsets of
{\mathbb{C}}
.
In particular, for every
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
and
t\in T
, the sequence of complex numbers
f_{n}(t,z)
converges as
n\in I\longrightarrow+\infty
.

Hence for all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
, as
n\in I\longrightarrow+\infty
, the sequence of holomorphic functions
t\in B(0,1-\varepsilon)\mapsto f_{n}(t,z)
converges pointwise on the dense subset
T
.
By equicontinuity w.r.t.
t
(
Corollary

45
), it converges uniformly on
B(0,1-\varepsilon)
for all
\varepsilon
.
Denote
f(t,z)
the limit.

Since the uniform limit of holomorphic functions is holomorphic, the function
t\mapsto f(t,z)
is holomorphic for each fixed
z
.

There remains to check that for all
t\in{\mathbb{D}}
, the function
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\mapsto f(t,z)
is a solution of the Beltrami equation for
\mu_{t}
.
By
Section

5.3
from the subsequence of functions
z\mapsto f_{n}(t,z)
where
n\in I
, one can extract a sub-subsequence
n\in J\subset I
that tends uniformly on compact subsets of
{\mathbb{C}}
to a solution
g
of the Beltrami equation for
\mu_{t}
. Since the
I
-subsequence converges pointwise to the function
z\mapsto f(t,z)
, it follows that
g(z)=f(t,z)
.

Method 2.

By
Section

5.4
,
for each fixed
t
, the sequence of functions
z\mapsto f_{n}(t,z)
converges uniformly on compact subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
to a normalized solution
z\mapsto f(t,z)
of the Beltrami equation for
\mu_{t}
.
The function
(t,z)\mapsto f(t,z)
in is in particular the simple limit of the functions
(t,z)\mapsto f_{n}(t,z)
. For each
z
, the function
t\in{\mathbb{D}}\mapsto f_{n}(t,z)
is holomorphic.
The function
t\mapsto f(t,z)
is hence the simple limit of a sequence of holomorphic functions, and this sequence is equicontinuous by
Corollary

45
. Hence the convergence is uniform on compact subsets of
{\mathbb{D}}
, and the limit is holomorphic.

Note
: See
Section

8
for a study of what happens if we average
\mu
through a (reasonable) function of
\mu
instead of directly taking
\mu_{n}=\int_{S}\mu/\int_{S}1
on each square
S
.

Appendix F Uniqueness and convergence

It is not the point in the present article to prove uniqueness of the solution of the Beltrami equation (
Theorem

3
), so we admit it (see the references below the statement of that theorem).
Let us explain how one deduces from it that the full sequence
f_{n}
tends to this unique solution
f
(uniformly on compact subsets of
{\mathbb{C}}
).

Since
\|\mu_{n}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\mu\|_{\infty}
, the sequence
f_{n}
is
K
-quasiconformal for some common
K
.
By
Lemma

42
, from any subsequence of
f_{n}
one can extract a sub-subsequence that converges uniformly on every compact subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
to some function
f
.
The procedure of
Section

5.3
can then be applied to this sub-subsequence, which prove that
f
is a solution of the Beltrami equation.
Since the solution is unique, this means that all extracted limits
f
of the sequence
f_{n}
are identical.
Now in a Hausdorff-separated topological space, if a sequence
f_{n}
has all its subsequences that contain sub-subsequence converging to
f
, then the full sequence
f_{n}
tends to
f
.

Appendix G Equivalent formulations of the holomorphy condition

In this appendix we recall equivalent definitions of being holomorphic, for functions from the unit disk to a Banach space.
The statements in the present section is not used in the article.

Proposition 46
.

Let
E
be a Banach space, and
f:{\mathbb{D}}\to E
a function.
Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

(power series expansion) There is a power series expansion
f(t)=\sum t^{n}a_{n}
over
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
where
a_{n}\in E
, whose radius of convergence is at least one, i.e.
\limsup\|a_{n}\|^{1/n}\leq 1
.

(2)

(differentiable) The function
f
is differentiable, i.e. for all
t\in{\mathbb{D}}
,
\frac{1}{h}(f(t+h)-f(t))
has a limit in
E
as the complex number
h\to 0
.

(3)

(weak) Denote
E^{\prime}
the dual space to
E
. For any
\phi\in E^{\prime}
, the function
\phi\circ f:{\mathbb{D}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
is holomorphic.

(4)

(weak*) Assume that
E
is the dual to some Banach space
V
. For any
v\in V
, the function
t\in{\mathbb{D}}\mapsto f(t)(v)\in{\mathbb{C}}
is holomorphic.

(5)

(very weak) The function
f
is bounded and there is a separating subset
X\subset E^{\prime}
(separating means
\forall e\in E-\{0\}
,
\exists\phi\in X

\phi(e)\neq 0
) such that
\forall\phi\in X
, the function
\phi\circ f
is holomorphic.

In either case,
f
is called holomorphic.

The implications (
1
)
\implies
(
2
)
\implies
(
3
)
\implies
(
4
) and (
3
)
\implies
(
5
) are immediate or easy.
That (
3
) implies (
1
) is proved for instance in
[
Muj86
]
, Lemma 8.13. In fact the proof adapts to give (
4
)
\implies
(
1
) because the principle of uniform boundedness can still be applied.
That (
5
) implies (
1
) is proved in
[
AN00
]
, Theorem 3.1 and is an extension of a result whose proof can be found in
[
Kat95
]
(Remark 1.38, page 139) for which
X
is assumed with dense span in
E^{\prime}
.

Let us specialize this to the Banach space
\cal B
of complex-valued
L^{\infty}
functions on
{\mathbb{C}}
, endowed with the essential supremum norm, which we will denote
\|\cdot\|
, and add one characterization. First note that
Definition

37
matches definition (
1
) above.

Proposition 47
.

Let
\mu:t\in{\mathbb{D}}\mapsto\mu_{t}\in\cal B
be a function.

Then
t\mapsto\mu_{t}
being holomorphic in any of the senses of
Proposition

46
is equivalent to any of the following criteria:

(1)

For every
L^{1}
function
\tau:{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
, the function
t\in{\mathbb{D}}\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{C}}\mu_{t}\tau
is holomorphic.

(2)

The function
t\mapsto\|\mu_{t}\|
is locally bounded and for every
C^{\infty}
function with compact support
\tau:{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
, the function
t\in{\mathbb{D}}\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{C}}\mu_{t}\tau
is holomorphic.

(3)

The function
t\mapsto\|\mu_{t}\|
is locally bounded and there is a function
(t,z)\mapsto\mu_{t}(z)\in{\mathbb{C}}
defined for
t\in{\mathbb{D}}
and
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that for all
t\in{\mathbb{D}}
,
z\mapsto\mu_{t}(z)
is a measurable function and is a representative of
\mu_{t}
and for all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
, the function
t\mapsto\mu_{t}(z)
is holomorphic.

Proof.

Point (
1
) is the criterion (
4
) of
Proposition

46
specialized to
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}})
being the dual space to
L^{1}({\mathbb{C}})
.

Point (
2
) is the criterion (
5
) of
Proposition

46
with the set of smooth function with compact suport known to be separating.

Let us prove that if criterion (
1
) of
Proposition

46
holds, then (
3
) holds.
Indeed, each
a_{n}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}})
has a representative that we denote
z\mapsto A_{n}(z)
. This representative could take values of modulus greater than
\|a_{n}\|
. It can only happen on a set of measure
0
and we modify
A_{n}
to take value
0
on this set, which does not change its class in
L^{\infty}
. Then for
all

z\in{\mathbb{C}}
, the power series
\sum A_{n}(z)t^{n}
converges on
{\mathbb{D}}
, we call
\mu_{t}(z)
its value. Let us check that
z\mapsto\mu_{t}(z)
is a representative of
\mu_{t}
.
By the criterion (
1
) of
Proposition

46
,
\mu_{t}
satisfies

\|\mu_{t}-\sum_{k=0}^{n}a_{k}t^{k}\|\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

and this means
\mu_{t}
has a representative for which the partial sums
z\mapsto\sum_{k=0}^{n}A_{k}(z)t^{k}
tend to it almost everywhere (if a sequence of functions has its essential sup tending to
0
then it tends to
0
almost everywhere). But the sum tends to
\mu_{t}(z)
everywhere, which is hence representative of
\mu_{t}
.

Let us now prove that if (
3
) holds, then
t\mapsto\mu_{t}
satisfies criterion (
1
) of
Proposition

46
.
By hypothesis, for each
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
t\mapsto\mu_{t}(z)
is holomorphic, so it has a power series expansion

\mu_{t}(z)=\sum A_{n}(z)t^{n},

and its radius of convergence is at least
1
.
Let
r=1/2
.
We have for all
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
:

A_{d}(z)=\lim_{N\to+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\frac{\mu_{re^{ik/N}}(z)}{(re^{ik/N})^{d}}.

As a simple limit of measurable functions, the map
A_{d}
is thus measurable.
This implies that
(t,z)\mapsto\mu_{t}(z)=\sum A_{n}(z)t^{n}
is itself measurable (note the passage to two variables).

Let
r<1
. Local boundedness implies that
t\mapsto\|\mu_{t}\|
is bounded on the compact set
\overline{B}(0,r)\subset{\mathbb{D}}
. Let
M=M_{r}
be a bound.
Consider the set
E\subset\overline{B}(0,r)\times{\mathbb{C}}
of pairs
(t,z)
for which
|\mu_{t}(z)|\geq M
.
By definition of
\|\cdot\|
, for each
t\in\overline{B}(0,r)
, the section
E_{t}=\big\{{z\in{\mathbb{C}}}\,\big|\,{(t,z)\in E}\big\}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
has Lebesgue measure
0
.
Since
(t,z)\mapsto\mu_{t}(z)
is measurable,
E
is measurable.
It follows that if we denote
P
the set of
z
for which the section
E^{z}=\big\{{t\in{\mathbb{D}}}\,\big|\,{(t,z)\in E}\big\}\subset{\mathbb{D}}
has positive Lebesgue measure, then the Lebesgue measure of
P
is
0
. For every
z\in{\mathbb{C}}-P
, the function
t\mapsto\mu_{t}(z)
takes value of modulus
\leq M
for almost every
t
, hence for every
t
since it is holomorphic. It follows that for all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}-P
,
|A_{n}(z)|\leq Mr^{-n}
for all
n
by Cauchy’s integral formula (alternatively, to avoid integration, one can use the average formula given earlier).
Then for all
t\in\overline{B}(0,r)
and all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}-P
,

|\mu_{t}(z)-\sum_{k=0}^{n}A_{k}(z)t^{k}|\leq M\frac{(|t|/r)^{n+1}}{1-|t|/r}.

Since
P
has measure
0
, it follows that, denoting
a_{n}
the element of
\cal B
represented by
A_{n}
:
\forall t\in\overline{B}(0,r)
,

\|\mu_{t}-\sum_{k=0}^{n}a_{k}t^{k}\|\leq M\frac{(|t|/r)^{n+1}}{1-|t|/r}.

Hence the power series
\sum a_{n}t^{n}
has a radius of convergence
\geq r
and
t\mapsto\mu_{t}
is is its sum on
B(0,r)
.
Since
r
can be chosen arbitrary in
(0,1)
, this concludes.
∎

Appendix H Counterexample to weak continuity

Fix
\kappa\in(0,1)
.
Let
\operatorname{Int}
denote the integer part of a real number.
Let
\mu_{1}(z)=0
if
\operatorname{Int}{\operatorname{Re}\,}z
is even and
\mu_{1}(z)=\kappa
otherwise.
The ellipse associated to
\mu=\kappa
has vertical major axis and ratio
K=h(\kappa)
where

h:[0,1)\to[1,+\infty),\ \kappa\mapsto\frac{1+\kappa}{1-\kappa}.

Note that
h
is non-linear (it is strictly convex).

The normalized solution of the Beltrami equation for
\mu_{1}
is given by
f(x+iy)=g(x)+iy
where
g
is the piecewise
C^{1}
function with
g(0)=0
,
g^{\prime}(x)=1
whenever
\operatorname{Int}(x)
is even and
g^{\prime}(x)=K
otherwise. See
Figure

15
.

Let
\mu_{n}(z)=\mu_{1}(nz)
.
Note that
\|\mu_{n}\|_{\infty}=\kappa
for all
n
.
A normalized solution of the Beltrami equation for
\mu_{n}
is given by
f_{n}(z)=f(nz)/f(n)
.
As
n\to\infty
, a computation gives

f_{n}(x+iy)\longrightarrow f(x):=x+iy/K^{\prime}

with
K^{\prime}=\frac{1+K}{2}=\frac{h(0)+h(\kappa)}{2}
.
On the other hand
\mu_{n}
tends weakly to the constant
\mu=(0+\kappa)/2
, whose normalized straightening is
x+iy\mapsto x+iy/K^{\prime\prime}
with
K^{\prime\prime}=h(\frac{0+\kappa}{2})
so

K^{\prime\prime}\neq K^{\prime}.

Remark
.

The dependence of
f
on
\mu
is non-linear and this is essentially the cause of the non-continuity with respect to the weak topology on
\mu
.
For instance, the simpler non-linear map
\mu\in L^{\infty}\mapsto\mu^{2}\in L^{\infty}
is not continuous if we take weak topology on both sides.

Figure 15.
Graph of the function
g
in
Section

5.4
for
\kappa=1/2
, hence
K=3
, in black. In blue the rescaling limit when conjugating by
x\mapsto nx
, i.e.
\lim g(nx)/n=K^{\prime}x
with
K^{\prime}=2
. In this example the weak limit of
\mu_{n}
is the constant
\mu=1/4
and
K^{\prime\prime}=5/3\neq K^{\prime}
.

Appendix I Distributions with
L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}
derivatives

Let
U
be an open subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
The set of locally
L^{1}
functions
f:U\to{\mathbb{R}}
whose distribution derivatives are also locally
L^{1}
is known as the set of
locally Sobolev functions
and denoted
W^{1,1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U)
.
Recall that such functions are equivalence classes up to modification on a set of Lebesgue measure
0
.

Remark
.

In fact, we may drop the first condition in the definition: a distribution whose partial derivatives are in
L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}
is necessarily in
L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}
(and this generalizes, see
[
Maz85
]
, the theorem of Section 1.1.2 page 7). However we will not need that fact. Moreover the functions
f
we will consider in the end are maps that are assumed to be homeomorphisms, so they are continuous, which is better than
L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}
. So we stick with the original definition of
W^{1,1}_{\operatorname{loc}}
.

More generally for
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
p\in[1,+\infty]
,
W^{k,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U)
denotes the Sobolev space of elements of
L^{p}_{\operatorname{loc}}
whose distributional derivatives up to order
k
are
L^{p}_{\operatorname{loc}}
.
In the version of the definition of quasiconformal maps
f
that we chose (
) we assume that
f\in W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}({\mathbb{C}})
and is continuous. In fact we assume still stronger: that
f
is a homeomorphism from
{\mathbb{C}}
to
{\mathbb{C}}
.

Remark
.

In one dimension, i.e. if
U
is a subset of
{\mathbb{R}}
, then the elements of
W^{1,1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U)
have continuous representatives (in fact, absolutely continuous), and they are differentiable almost everywhere.
34
34

34

See for instance
[
Rud87
]
, Theorem 7.20 and the paragraph before 7.17.

If
n>1
, it is not true that any
W^{1,1}_{\operatorname{loc}}
function has a continuous representative, nor an almost everywhere differentiable one (in the classical sense of being differentiable), not even that for almost every
x\in U
there is a representative that is differentiable at
x
: a counterexample to these claims is given by the function

f(x)=\sum_{k}\varepsilon_{k}g(x-x_{k})

where
x_{k}
is a dense sequence in
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
g\geq 0
is a well chosen function described below and
\varepsilon_{k}>0
decreases sufficiently fast. To define
g
, let
r=\|x\|_{2}
and if
n=2
let
g(x)=\max(0,\log\frac{1}{r})
or if
n>2
let
g(x)=1/r^{n-2}
.
Then
f\in W^{1,1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U)
but the essential supremum of
f
is infinite on every open set, because
f^{-1}((N,+\infty])
is open and dense for all
N
, hence
f
is differentiable nowhere even after modification on a set of measure
0
.
Yet, for any
W^{1,1}_{\operatorname{loc}}
function
f
, on almost every line parallel to the main axes,
f
is almost everywhere equal to an absolutely continuous function (and this remains true even after a
C^{1}
change of variable
\psi
:
f\circ\psi
is also
W^{1,1}_{\operatorname{loc}}
).
This is called the ACL property, but we will not use it.
The interested reader may consult
[
Ahl66
,
Ahl06
]
, Chapter II B, and in particular Lemma 2.

Remark
.

A similar counterexample for
W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}
functions can also be built using
g(x)=\max(0,\log\frac{1}{r})
.
Actually for
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
p\in[1,+\infty)
, then all
W^{k,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}
functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
have continuous representatives if and only if
n<kp
or (
p=1
and
n=k
) where
n
is the dimension number and where
W^{k,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}
is the Sobolev space of functions whose distributional derivatives up to order
k
are
L^{p}_{\operatorname{loc}}
, see
[
AF03
]
, Theorem 4.12 page 85 and Sections 4.40 to 4.43.

The next result is
[
Ahl06
]
, Lemma 3 page 20, we do not reprove it here.

Lemma 48

(Change of variable)
.

Consider a continuous function
f:U\to{\mathbb{C}}
whose distribution derivatives
\partial f/\partial x_{i}
are locally
L^{1}
.
Let
\psi:V\to U
be a
C^{2}
diffeomorphism from a subset
V
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
to
U
.
Then the function
f\circ\psi
has locally
L^{1}
distribution derivatives, and the chain rule holds, i.e.

\frac{\partial(f\circ\psi)}{\partial y_{j}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial\psi_{i}}{\partial y_{j}}\times\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}\circ\psi.

Remark
.

Actually
f
does not need to be continuous:
f\in W^{1,1}_{\operatorname{loc}}
is enough for the proof in
[
Ahl66
]
to work.
Weaker conditions on the change of variables can also be made to work, for instance
C^{1}
, using different proofs.
Note that we will only need the lemma and its corollary below for
f
continuous and a change of variable
\psi
that is a rotation.

Corollary 49
.

In the previous lemma, if the distribution derivatives of
f
are locally
L^{2}
then the same holds for
f\circ\psi
.

Proof.

Locally
L^{2}
functions are in particular locally
L^{1}
so we can apply the previous lemma.
The right hand side of the formula in the conclusion of this lemma adds up to a locally
L^{2}
function.
∎

This also true with
L^{2}
replaced by
L^{p}
,
p\in[1,+\infty]
.

Appendix J Proof of
Lemma

38

We already know that
f
is a homeomorphism.
The main technical detail is to check that the distribution partial derivatives of
f
are locally
L^{2}
.
Note that
f
is smooth inside the polygons
Q_{j}
, which is even better.
There, the differential
da_{j}
sends the ellipses
E
associated to
\mu_{j}
to circles while
F\circ\pi
, being holomorphic, sends circles to circles, so
df
straightens the ellipses
E
to circles, i.e. satisfies the Beltrami equation on
\operatorname{Int}Q_{j}
.
The rest of
{\mathbb{C}}
consists points that are either interior points of edges or vertices.
Since this rest has Lebesgue measure
0
, once we know that
f\in W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}
the above discussion immediately implies that
\bar{\partial}f=\mu\,\partial f
holds almost everywhere.

There remains to check that
f\in W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}
. It is obvious inside the polygons: the map
f
is smooth there, which is even better.
Near the edges and vertices we may use some erasability theorem available in the literature.
35
35

35

Like
[
Ahl54
]
, Theorem 4 page 9 (quasiconformal erasability of analytic arcs, here the arc is an open straight segment, so
f
is q.c. at least on the complement of a finite set; a fortiori isolated points are erasable so
f
is actually q.c.) or by repeated use of Rickman’s lemma
[
Ric69
]
, Theorem 1 page 6 (near an inner point on an edge let
D
be neighbourhood in
{\mathbb{C}}
and
E
the intersection of this neighbourhood with a closed polygon on one side of the edge; near a vertex use Rickman’s lemma repeatedly to add the polygons one by one in trigonometric order, the last polygon added allows to include the vertex).

Note that near an interior point of an edge one could also use the decomposition
f=F\circ\psi
where
\psi
is the join of
\pi\circ(a_{j}:j)
and
\pi\circ(a_{k}:k)
and work in the chart
in the chart
\phi
of
\cal S
mentioned in
Section

5.2
,
for which
F\circ\phi^{-1}
is holomorphic and
\phi\circ\psi
is an explicty map that is
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear on both sides of a line.
Checking
W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}
for such a map is easy.
It can thus be seen to be quasiconformal and the composition of a holomorphic bijection (or any quasiconformal map) with a quasiconformal map is still quasiconformal. This is not as simple near a vertex.

Alternatively, both near interior points of edges and near vertices, we can directly check that
f\in W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}
, as it is rather tractable in our case, and this is what we do in the rest of this section.

Near a point
z_{0}
on an edge, if
z_{0}
is not a vertex, we perform a change of variable
z
in the domain, more precisely a rotation, so that this edge is vertical, which we assume now: this is enough by
Corollary

49
.
Denote
z_{0}=x_{0}+iy_{0}
.
Let
\phi
be a test function whose support is contained in a square
S_{\varepsilon}
of equation
\max|{\operatorname{Re}\,}z-z_{0}|,|{\operatorname{Im}\,}z-z_{0}|<\varepsilon
for
\varepsilon
small enough. Then

\left\langle\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{y}},\phi\right\rangle=\left\langle f,-\frac{\partial{\phi}}{\partial{y}}\right\rangle=\int_{-\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon}\left(da\times-\int_{-\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon}db\times\Big(f(z)\frac{\partial{\phi}}{\partial{y}}(z)\Big)\right)

where in the inner integral:
z=(x_{0}+a)+i(y_{0}+b)
.

Claim: Let
e
be an edge of
Q_{j}
and
e^{*}
the edge without its endpoints. Then
f
is on
Q_{j}
the restriction of a smooth function defined on
Q_{j}\cup W
where
W
is a neighbourhood of
e^{*}
.
Indeed, recall that for every edge
e
of
P_{j}=a_{j}(Q_{j})
there is a chart
\psi:V\to{\mathbb{C}}
on a neighbourhood
V
of
\pi(e:j)
that satisfies
\forall z\in P_{j}
,
\psi(\pi(z:j))=z
.
Then the map
\psi^{-1}\circ a_{j}
is defined on
U=a_{j}^{-1}(\psi(V))
, its image is
V
, it is smooth and coincides with
\pi
on
Q_{j}
. Since
F
is holomorphic on
\cal S^{\prime}
, the composition
F\circ\psi^{-1}\circ a_{j}
is thus smooth and coincides with
f=F\circ\pi\circ a_{j}
on
U\cap Q_{j}
.

In particular we can integrate by parts without problem for
all

a\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
:

-\int_{-\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon}db\times\Big(f(z)\frac{\partial{\phi}}{\partial{y}}(z)\Big)=\int_{-\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon}db\times\Big(v(z)\phi(z)\Big)

where
v
is defined as follows: let
Q_{j}
be the polygon on the right of the vertical segment, and
Q_{k}
the one on the left. Let
f_{j}
and
f_{k}
be corresponding smooth extensions of
f
. If
a\geq 0
, let
v(z)=v_{j}(z)=\frac{\partial{f_{j}}}{\partial{y}}(z)
and if
a\leq 0
let
v(z)=v_{k}(z)=\frac{\partial{f_{k}}}{\partial{y}}(z)
. These two continuous maps coincide on the vertical line with the derivative of
f
along this line, hence
v
is continous.
The map
v
is a representative of the distribution derivative of
f
on
S_{\varepsilon}
, since by Fubini’s theorems we can finish the computation and get
\left\langle f,-\frac{\partial{\phi}}{\partial{y}}\right\rangle=\left\langle v,\phi\right\rangle
.
As a continuous map on
S_{\varepsilon}
,
v
is even better than locally
L^{2}
.

This is similar for
\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x}}
. Write

\left\langle\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x}},\phi\right\rangle=\left\langle f,-\frac{\partial{\phi}}{\partial{x}}\right\rangle=\int_{-\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon}\left(db\times-\int_{-\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon}da\times\Big(f(z)\frac{\partial{\phi}}{\partial{x}}(z)\Big)\right)

with
z=(x_{0}+a)+i(y_{0}+b)
and

-\int_{-\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon}da\times\Big(f(z)\frac{\partial{\phi}}{\partial{x}}(z)\Big)=-\int_{-\varepsilon}^{0}[\cdots]-\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}[\cdots].

Now

\int_{0}^{\pm\varepsilon}da\times\Big(f(z)\frac{\partial{\phi}}{\partial{x}}(z)\Big)=f(z_{0}+ib)\phi(z_{0}+ib)-\int_{0}^{\pm\varepsilon}da\times\Big(u(z)\phi(z)\Big)

where

u(z)=u_{j}(z)=\frac{\partial{f_{j}}}{\partial{x}}
if
a>0
and

u(z)=u_{k}(z)=\frac{\partial{f_{k}}}{\partial{x}}
if
a<0
.
This time
u_{j}
and
u_{k}
do not match anymore on the vertical axis so we cannot join them into a continuous function. Nevertheless, let
u=u_{j}
inside the polygons and
0
on the vertical edge: then
u
is measurable and bounded, hence locally
L^{2}
, and the formula
\left\langle f,-\frac{\partial{\phi}}{\partial{x}}\right\rangle=\left\langle u,\phi\right\rangle
holds by Fubini and cancellation of the term
f(z_{0}+ib)\phi(z_{0}+ib)
. This cancellation can be seen as a consequence of the continuity of
f
.

That
f
is also
W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}
at vertices
z_{0}
is proved similarly by decomposing the integral
\int f\frac{\partial{\phi}}{\partial{x_{i}}}
over the square neighbourhood
S_{\varepsilon}
of
z_{0}
with Fubini and cutting each horizontal or vertical segment on which the inner integral is taken into pieces cut by the edges (recall the edges are straight segments in the domain of
f
).
We can omit the vertical/horizontal segment going through
z_{0}
since its contribution to the integral is
0
.
On each piece an integration by parts can be taken and cancellations of boundary terms will occur thanks to the continuity of
f
.
We thus get a function
u_{i}
that is
0
on the edges and is on
\operatorname{Int}Q_{j}
the restriction of a function that is continuous on
Q_{j}-\{\text{vertices}\}
and such that the distribution derivative
\partial f/\partial x_{i}
is represented by
u_{i}
by Fubini’s theorems.
There remains to check that
u_{i}
is locally
L^{2}
at
z_{0}
and in fact to use Fubini above it was already necessary to check that it is locally
L^{1}
, as a condition for Fubini’s theorem is that the integrand has an absolute value of finite integral.
To check this, recall that as explained in
Section

2
, a local conformal coordinate is given by a composition of
a_{j}
with the complex logarithm
\log(a_{j}(z-z_{0}))
followed by well-chosen translations followed by multiplication by a complex constant
\alpha
independent of
j
followed by the exponential.
Another way to express it is branches of
z\mapsto c_{j}\times a_{j}(z-z_{0})^{\alpha_{j}}
where
c_{j}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
.
Moreover
\alpha_{j}=\frac{2\pi i}{i\theta+\log\lambda}
for some
\theta>0
and
\lambda\in(0,+\infty)
, in particular

{\operatorname{Re}\,}(\alpha_{j})>0.

The map
f
near
z_{0}
is the composition of such maps on finitely many sectors followed by holomorphic map
\psi
defined near
0
and independent of
j
(of course it depends on
z_{0}
).
Its derivative is bounded.
We have
d(z^{\alpha})=\alpha z^{\alpha}dz/z
and
|z^{\alpha}|\approx|z|^{{\operatorname{Re}\,}\alpha}
in the sense that their quotient remains bounded when
z
remains in the sector indexed by
j
.
So on each sector, using polar coordinates and
dx\wedge dy=rdr\wedge d\theta
, we have

\displaystyle\int\left|\frac{d(z^{\alpha})}{dz}\right|^{2}\approx\int_{0}^{R}r^{(2\,{\operatorname{Re}\,}\alpha-1)}dr,

which is convergent since
{\operatorname{Re}\,}\alpha>0
.

Consider also the Taylor expansion

\mu(c+z)=\mu_{0}+\mu_{x}x+\mu_{y}y+\mu_{xx}x^{2}+2\mu_{xy}xy+\mu_{yy}y^{2}+o(z^{2})

where
z=x+iy
and denote

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu_{xx}+\mu_{yy},

\displaystyle\rtimes

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\mu_{xy}.

Later in this proof we will use that the
o(z^{2})
is uniform, i.e. that

\mu(c+z)=\mu_{0}(c)+\mu_{x}(c)x+\mu_{y}(c)y+\mu_{xx}(c)x^{2}+2\mu_{xy}(c)xy+\mu_{yy}(c)y^{2}+|z|^{2}r(c,z)

where

as

and
