41
41

41

Since
\mu
has compact support and is
C^{2}
, there is a uniform modulus of continuity
\delta\mapsto M(\delta)
for its second order partial derivatives.
By two successive integrations, one finds that
|r(c,z)|\leq M(|z|)
.

Let
M_{i}
be the average of
\mu(z)
for
z\in C_{i}
.
Then

\displaystyle M_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu_{0}+\frac{\mu_{x}+\mu_{y}}{2}\varepsilon+\left(\frac{\Delta}{3}+\frac{\rtimes}{4}\right)\varepsilon^{2}+o(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle M_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu_{0}+\frac{-\mu_{x}+\mu_{y}}{2}\varepsilon+\left(\frac{\Delta}{3}-\frac{\rtimes}{4}\right)\varepsilon^{2}+o(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle M_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu_{0}+\frac{-\mu_{x}-\mu_{y}}{2}\varepsilon+\left(\frac{\Delta}{3}+\frac{\rtimes}{4}\right)\varepsilon^{2}+o(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle M_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu_{0}+\frac{\mu_{x}-\mu_{y}}{2}\varepsilon+\left(\frac{\Delta}{3}-\frac{\rtimes}{4}\right)\varepsilon^{2}+o(\varepsilon^{2})

where the
o(\varepsilon^{2})
are uniform as above.
Consider the
{\mathbb{R}}
-affine map

A_{j}(z)=z+M_{j}\overline{z},

which satisfies
\partial_{\bar{z}}A_{j}/\partial_{z}A_{j}=M_{j}
.
Let
\theta
be the sum of the angles at
0
of the four quadrilaterals
A_{j}(C_{j})
. As
\varepsilon\longrightarrow 0
, we have
\theta\longrightarrow 2\pi
.
Then the monodromy factor
\Lambda=\lambda e^{i\theta}
around
0
, which depends on
c
and
\varepsilon
, satisfies

\frac{1}{\Lambda}=\frac{A_{1}(i)}{A_{0}(i)}\times\frac{A_{2}(-1)}{A_{1}(-1)}\times\frac{A_{3}(-i)}{A_{2}(-i)}\times\frac{A_{0}(1)}{A_{3}(1)}

which, after simplification reads

\Lambda=\frac{1-M_{0}}{1+M_{0}}\times\frac{1+M_{1}}{1-M_{1}}\times\frac{1-M_{2}}{1+M_{2}}\times\frac{1+M_{3}}{1-M_{3}}

i.e.

\Lambda=\frac{L_{1}\times L_{3}}{L_{0}\times L_{2}}

with

L_{j}=\frac{1+M_{j}}{1-M_{j}}.

We have
\operatorname{res}=\frac{\log\lambda+i\theta}{2\pi}-1/

As
\varepsilon\longrightarrow 0
, the monodromy factor tends to
1
.
Since
\theta\longrightarrow 2\pi
, we have that
\log(\lambda+i\theta)-2\pi=\log_{p}\Lambda
where
\log_{p}
denotes the principal branch of the complex logarithm:

\operatorname{res}=\frac{\log_{p}\Lambda}{2\pi}

As soon as
\varepsilon
is small enough (independently of
c
).
After a moderately complicated calculation involving the expansions of the
M_{j}
, one finds that

(11)

\log_{p}\Lambda(c,\varepsilon)=-\left(\frac{2\rtimes}{1-\mu_{0}^{2}}+\frac{4\mu_{0}\mu_{x}\mu_{y}}{(1-\mu_{0}^{2})^{2}}\right)\varepsilon^{2}+o(\varepsilon^{2})

where the
o(\varepsilon^{2})
is uniform in the sense explained earlier.

The convergence of
(\log_{p}\Lambda)/\varepsilon^{2}
is thus uniform on
c\in{\mathbb{C}}
as
\varepsilon\longrightarrow 0
.
Note that for
\varepsilon=1/n
, the denominator
\varepsilon^{2}
is the area of each small square.

Let for
c\in{\mathbb{C}}

h(c)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\log_{p}\Lambda(c)}{\varepsilon^{2}}

and let
m=h\times\operatorname{Leb}
.

The rest of the proof is standard but we include it anyway.

Let
\cal C
denote the (finite) set of squares
S
of horizontal and vertical sides of length
1/n
, centred on the points
s
of coordinates
(i/n,j/n)
,
i,\,j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
\cal C^{\prime}
the (finite) subset of those for which
2S
has non-empty intersection with the support of
\mu
, where
2S
denotes the square of the same centre but double side length.
Note that
\cal C
and
\cal C^{\prime}
depend on
n
.
The square
2S
is formed of the four little squares that have
s
as a vertex and the condition ensures that every point in the support of
m_{n}
is the centre of some
S\in\cal C
.
In facy if
s\in\cal C-\cal C^{\prime}
then
m_{n}
and
m
are zero on
S
so in particular
\int_{S}m_{n}\tau=0=\int_{S}m\tau
.
Let
R>0
big enough so that
\overline{B}(0,R)
contains the support of
\mu
.
Note that if
S\in\cal C
then
S\subset\overline{B}(0,R+\sqrt{2}/n)\subset\overline{B}(0,R+\sqrt{2})
.

Let
\tau
be a continuous function on
{\mathbb{C}}
.
Let
\eta>0
. Then by uniform continuity of
h
(it is continuous with compact support) and of the restriction of
\tau
to
\overline{B}(0,R+\sqrt{2})
, for all
n
, for all square
S\in\cal C^{\prime}
, denoting
s
its centre, we have:

\sup_{z\in S}|h(z)-h(s)|<\eta,\ \ \sup_{z\in S}|\tau(z)-\tau(s)|<\eta,\ \ \left|\frac{\operatorname{res}(s)}{\operatorname{Leb}S}-h(s)\right|<\eta.

The third inequality is a consequence of the uniform convergence mentioned above.
For all
s\in\cal C
we have
\int_{S}m_{n}\tau=\operatorname{res}(s)\tau(s)
and thus for
S\in\cal C^{\prime}
we get

\left|\int_{S}m_{n}\tau-\int_{S}m\tau\right|\leq\int_{z\in S}\left|\frac{\operatorname{res}(s)}{\operatorname{Leb}S}\tau(s)-h(z)\tau(z)\right|\times\operatorname{Leb}

\leq\int_{z\in S}\left(\left|\frac{\operatorname{res}(s)}{\operatorname{Leb}S}\tau(s)-h(s)\tau(s)\right|+|h(s)\tau(s)-h(s)\tau(z)|+|h(s)\tau(z)-h(z)\tau(z)|\right)

\leq\int_{z\in S}\left(\eta\|\tau\|_{\infty}+\eta\|h\|_{\infty}+\eta\|\tau\|_{\infty}\right)\times\operatorname{Leb}\leq K\eta\operatorname{Leb}(S)

for some
K>0
independent of
n
and of
\eta
.

We saw that for
S\in\cal C-\cal C^{\prime}
then
m_{n}
and
m
are zero on
S
.
Hence
\left|\int_{\mathbb{C}}m_{n}\tau-\int_{\mathbb{C}}m\tau\right|
is bounded from above by the sum of
\left|\int_{S}m_{n}\tau-\int_{S}m\tau\right|
over the squares
S\in\cal C^{\prime}
.
Summing the upper bound of the previous paragraph, over this finite collection of squares, we get

\left|\int_{\mathbb{C}}m_{n}\tau-\int_{\mathbb{C}}m\tau\right|\leq K\eta\operatorname{Leb}W

where
W=\overline{B}(0,R+\sqrt{2})
.

The proof for the total mass is based on an almost identical computation. We take the constant
1
instead of
\tau
and use uniform continuity of
|h|
and uniform convergence of
|\operatorname{res}s|/\varepsilon^{2}
to
|h|
.
∎

We are also interested in the images (push-forward) of the measures
m_{n}
by the straightening maps
f_{n}
.

Corollary 52
.

The measure
(f_{n})_{*}m_{n}
weakly tends to
f_{*}m
in the same sense.

Proof.

This follows from
f_{n}
tending locally uniformly to
f
. Let
\tau
be continuous over
{\mathbb{C}}
. The proof is easy but we detail it here.

\int(f_{n})_{*}m_{n}\times\tau-\int f_{*}m\times\tau=\int m_{n}\times\tau\circ f_{n}-\int m\times\tau\circ f

=\int m_{n}\times(\tau\circ f_{n}-\tau\circ f)+\int(m_{n}-m)\times\tau\circ f.

The second term tends to
0
by a direct application of the second part of
.
The first term is bounded by

|m_{n}|\times\sup_{B(0,R)}|\tau\circ f_{n}-\tau\circ f|

where
R
is independent of
n
and is taken big enough so that all
m_{n}
and
m
have support in
\overline{B}(0,R)
.
We have seen that
|m_{n}|
is bounded.
By uniform continuity of
\tau
on compact sets, we have
\sup_{B(0,R)}|\tau\circ f_{n}-\tau\circ f|\longrightarrow 0
.
∎

Note
: In
Section

8
we show that if we average
\mu
through a (reasonable) function of
\mu
instead of directly taking
\mu_{n}=\int_{S}\mu/\int_{S}1
on each square
S
, we still get the same limit.

On the (lack of) invariance of the limit measure
m

We denote
m[f]
to emphasize the dependence of
m
on the
C^{2}
function
f
.

(1)

The limit
m
is invariant under a rescaling of the following form
g(x+iy)=ax+biy+c
with
a>0
,
b>0
and
c\in{\mathbb{C}}
, i.e.
m[g\circ f]=g_{*}m[f]
where
g_{*}
refers to the push forward of measures;

(2)

it is invariant under rotation by a fourth of a turn: for all linear map
g(Z)=iz+c
with
c\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
m[g\circ f]=g_{*}m[f]
;

(3)

it is
not
generally invariant under a rotation by an angle
\alpha
such that
\alpha\neq 0\bmod\pi/2
;

(4)

and it is
not
generally invariant under a non-conformal orientation-preserving
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear map.

These claims are easy to check from the formula given in
.

Another way to state them is that if instead of small squares directed by the main axes of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
we had chosen (identical) rectangles with the same directions, it would still give the same limit
m
. But if we had chosen other (identical) quadrilaterals or rectangles directed by different axes, we would obtain a different limit
m
for most choices of
\mu
, even though the corresponding maps
f_{n}
converge to the same solution
f
of
\mu
.

Hence, the weak limit
m
of the residues
m_{n}
attached to our approximation scheme for the solution of the Beltrami equation is less canonical than one would think from first examination, because not only it depends on
\mu
, but it also depends on the choice of a horizontal/vertical direction in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

Remark
.

We chose to cut the plane in small squares. If we had chosen for instance small hexagons, we would get a different formula for the limit
m
of
m_{n}
. The exploration of all the possibilities goes beyond the scope of this article.

7.4.
A limit for
\cal S_{n}

Do the similarity surfaces
\cal S_{n}
converge in some sense?

Consider the meromorphic Christoffel symbol
\zeta_{n}
on
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
associated to the construction. It is the symbol of a locally flat symmetric conformal connection with singularities at the poles of
\zeta_{n}
.

Consider also the push-forward by
f
of the measure
m
:
f_{*}m
.

It is quite easy to deduce from
Corollary

52
that
\zeta_{n}
has a weak limit:

Proposition 53
.

The sequence
\zeta_{n}
weakly tends on
{\mathbb{C}}
to the following convolution product:

\zeta_{n}\xrightharpoonup{\phantom{m}}\zeta:=(f_{*}m)*\frac{1}{z}

the weak limit is understood against continuous test functions, compactly supported in
{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

According to
Theorem

1
, denoting
\kappa(z)=1/z
and
\operatorname{neg}(z)=-z
we have

\zeta_{n}=((f_{n})_{*}m_{n})*\kappa.

Now

\int\tau\times(\zeta_{n}-\zeta)=\int\tau\times\big(((f_{n})_{*}m_{n}-f_{*}m)*\kappa\big)

=\int((f_{n})_{*}m_{n}-f_{*}m)(\tau*(\kappa\circ\operatorname{neg}))

The last identity comes from the Fubini-Tonelli theorem for the product measure
\operatorname{Leb}\times(|m_{n}|+|m|)
, remarking that
\kappa
is locally
L^{1}
and that both integration variables remain bounded because
\tau
,
m_{n}
and
m
have compact support.
The function
\tau*(\kappa\circ\operatorname{neg})
is continuous (because
\tau
is continuous and
\kappa
is locally
L^{1}
).
By
Corollary

52
the integral tends to
0
.
∎

Lemma 54
.

If
\mu
is a
C^{1}
Beltrami form on
{\mathbb{C}}
and has compact support, then its straightening is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism.

Proof.

The condition is too strong, but this statement is enough for our purposes.
One can check that the conditions of Theorem 7.2, page 235 of
[
LV73
]
hold, and its conclusion directly give that
f
is a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism.
42
42

42

According to [Bo], in
[
Vek53
]

one finds a proof of the
C^{1}
character of the solution if
\mu
is Hölder.

∎

In particular,
f
is at least
C^{1}
(actually, it is better
43
43

43

According to Theorem 15.6.2 of
[
AIM09
]
, since our
\mu
is
C^{2}
, the map
f
is
C^{2+\alpha}
for all
\alpha\in(0,1)
.
).
Recall that
m=h\operatorname{Leb}
for some continuous function
h
.
It follows that
f_{*}m=g\operatorname{Leb}
where
g
is a continuous function:
g(z)=h(f^{-1}(z))/\det Df(f^{-1}(z))
.
As a consequence, the convolution product
\zeta=(f_{*}m)*\frac{1}{z}
, defines a continuous function
\zeta
. We sum this up in the following statement.

Corollary 55
.

The function
f
is
C^{1}
and the function
\zeta
is continuous.

We now propose an interpretation of the limit
\zeta
with
Theorems

5
and
6
below.

Taking a limit of the parallel transport

We saw above that even though the singularities of
\zeta_{n}
form a more and more dense set, their decreasing residues have a sufficiently small influence so that the sequence
\zeta_{n}
weakly converges. In the theorem below, we prove that the influence is small enough to allow the convergence of the parallel transport, provided we make a small modification near the beginning and the end of the path.

We will prove in
Theorem

5
a result about parallel transport along sequences of paths that are
C^{1}
by parts. Note that parallel transport of a vector is well-defined for such paths when they avoid the singularities, and we have the formula

v(t)=v(0)\exp\left(\int_{u=0}^{t}\zeta_{n}(\gamma(u))\gamma^{\prime}(u)du\right).

In
Theorem

5
we will assume that
\gamma^{\prime}
stays away from
0
:
\inf|\gamma^{\prime}|>0
and we will use a sequence of paths
\gamma_{n}
tending to
\gamma
, to allow for more flexibility (for instance to dodge the singularities in the case the limit
\gamma
hits some singularities of
\zeta_{n}
for some
n
).
The formula above reads

v(t)=v(0)\exp(\tau)

with

\tau=\int_{0}^{t}\zeta_{n}(\gamma_{n}(u))\gamma_{n}^{\prime}(u)du.

The convergence of parallel transport can thus be stated purely in terms of this quantity.
If
\gamma_{n}
does not run through a singularity,
\tau
is a path-integral in the sense used in holomorphic functions theory:

\tau=\int\zeta_{n}(\gamma_{n})d\gamma_{n}.

Path integrals can also be defined as follows for non-holomorphic functions like
\zeta
and paths that are
C^{1}
by parts:

\int\zeta(\gamma)d\gamma=\int_{0}^{t}\zeta(\gamma(u))\gamma^{\prime}(u)du

but note that, unless
\zeta
is holomorphic, it does not depend only on the homotopy class of
\gamma
.
It would be natural to conjecture the convergence of the path integrals against
\zeta_{n}
to the one against
\zeta
.
However, there is a subtlety.
We have a problem when the path starts or ends too close to a singularity
s
: first note that if the path starts or ends on a singularity then integral is the sum of a converging term and of
\operatorname{res}(s)\log(\gamma-s)
, and the imaginary part of
\log(\gamma-s)
has a limit if
\gamma^{\prime}
does not vanish but not the real part.
If the path ends or starts close to a singularity for which
\operatorname{res}s\neq 0
, the integral will take big values, which may prevent convergence.
We will deal with this case, and also with the case where the ending or starting points are too close to a singularity, by ignoring the singularity, i.e. subtracting
\frac{\operatorname{res}s}{z-s}
from
\zeta_{n}
.
Before giving the criterion for “too close” above, let us gauge the influence of the closest singularity.
Assuming
n
large and
\gamma^{\prime}_{n}
bounded, and because
\mu
is
C^{2}
, we expect the singularities to sit at the vertices of a grid that is not too distorted and whose sides have lengths of order
1/n
(see
Lemma

58
).
Recall that the residues are a
\cal O(1/n^{2})
. Hence
\int\frac{\operatorname{res}s}{z-s}dz=-\operatorname{res}s\log(z-s)
is a
\cal O((\log n)/n^{2})
at the midpoints between two singularities
s=f(c)
and
s^{\prime}=f(c^{\prime})
corresponding to adjacent points
c
and
c^{\prime}
in the grid of small squares.
Now if we take a point
z
and let
s
be the closest singularity to
z
and call
r=|z-s|
, we get
-\operatorname{res}s\log(z-s)=\cal O((\log r)/n^{2})
.
If we let

r_{n}=\exp(-n),

we have that
r_{n}
is much smaller than the grid size, and that
|\log r_{n}|/n^{2}=1/n\longrightarrow 0
.
We thus make the following definition.

Definition 56
.

Let
\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}
, which depends on
\gamma_{n}
, be the rational map
\zeta_{n}
, which is expressed as the sum
\zeta_{n}(z)=\sum_{s}\frac{\operatorname{res}s}{z-s}
, from which we subtract all the terms for which
s
is at distance
<r_{n}
from
\gamma_{n}(0)
or
\gamma_{n}(1)
.

For
n
big enough, since
r_{n}
is much smaller than the distance between the residues by the forthcoming
Lemma

58
, at most two terms are subtracted above, whatever the path
\gamma_{n}
is.
Moreover, typically none will be subtracted and we will have
\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}=\zeta_{n}
in this case.

Theorem 5
.

The parallel transport has a limit in the following sense.
Consider a sequence of
C^{1}
by parts paths
\gamma_{n}:[0,1]\to U

that converges to a
C^{1}
path
\gamma
in the sense that both
\|\gamma_{n}-\gamma\|_{\infty}\longrightarrow 0
and
\|\gamma^{\prime}_{n}-\gamma^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\longrightarrow 0
as
n\to\infty
.
Assume that for all
n
,
\gamma_{n}
avoids the singularities of
\zeta_{n}
.
Assume that
\forall t
,
\gamma^{\prime}(t)\neq 0
.
Consider the meromorphic function
\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}
as above.
Then

\int\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}(\gamma_{n})d\gamma_{n}\longrightarrow\int\zeta(\gamma(t))\gamma^{\prime}(t)dt.

The proof of this theorem comes after preparatory statements.

Proposition 57
.

If we endow the set of homeomorphisms of the Riemann sphere
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
with the metric
d(f,g)=\max d(f(z),g(z))+\max d(f^{-1}(z),g^{-1}(z))
where
d
is the spherical metric, then the set of
K
-quasiconformal homeomorphisms of the Riemann sphere
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
fixing
0
,
1
and
\infty
is compact.

This follows from Theorem 2 in Chapter III of
[
Ahl66
]
(see
[
Ahl06
]
page 33), remarking that
f^{-1}
is also
K
-quasiconformal.

Let us come back to our situation, with a grid of step
1/n
and the quasiconformal maps
f_{n}
that tend to
f
.

Lemma 58
.

For all compact subset
K
of
{\mathbb{C}}
, there exists
C,C^{\prime}
such that for all
n
,
for any two grid corners
a
,
b
that sit in
K
, we have

C^{\prime}|b-a|\leq|f_{n}(a)-f_{n}(b)|\leq C|b-a|.

Proof.

Let us write

f_{n}=\hat{f}_{n}\circ f

with

\hat{f}_{n}=f_{n}\circ f^{-1}.

Like
f
and
f_{n}
, the map
\hat{f}_{n}
is normalized.
Since
\mu
is
C^{2}
, hence
C^{1}
, it follows that
f
is also a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism by
Lemma

54
.
Since it is holomorphic near infinity, and since
f
is a homeomorphism from
{\mathbb{C}}
to itself, it follows by classical arguments that
f^{\prime}(z)
has a limit
a\in{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
as
z\to\infty
. Hence
f
is actually globally bi-Lipschitz for the Euclidean metric on
{\mathbb{C}}
.
In particular there exists
\kappa>0
such that for any
n
and for any two distinct grid corners
u^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
, we have

|f(u^{\prime})-f(v^{\prime})|>\kappa/n.

The map
\hat{f}_{n}
straightens a Beltrami form

\hat{\mu}_{n}=f_{*}\mu_{n}

which satisfies
\sup_{\mathbb{C}}|\hat{\mu}_{n}|\leq M/n
for some
M>0
.
Indeed,
\mu_{n}
is on each little square the average of
\mu=f^{*}0
.
Since
\mu
is
C^{2}
with compact support, hence
C^{1}
with compact support, we have
\|\mu_{n}-\mu\|_{\infty}\leq\operatorname{cst}/n
.
The value of
|\hat{\mu}_{n}|(f(z))
is given by the smooth formula
\left|\frac{a-b}{1-a\overline{b}}\right|
that depends only on
a=\mu_{n}(z)
and
b=\mu(z)
and defined for
(a,b)\in{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
.
Since
|\mu_{n}|
and
|\mu|
are uniformly bounded away from
1
,
the variables
a,b
remain in a compact set and hence

\sup_{\mathbb{C}}|\hat{\mu}_{n}|\leq M/n

for some
M
, as claimed.

Let
\kappa>0
and consider two points
u
,
v\in f(K)
with

|u-v|>\operatorname{\kappa}/n

and denote
u_{n}=\hat{f}_{n}(u)
and
v_{n}=\hat{f}_{n}(v)
.
We claim that

\frac{1}{a_{n}}\leq\frac{|u_{n}-v_{n}|}{|u-v|}\leq a_{n}

where
a_{n}
depends on
\kappa
but is independent of
u
and
v
and
a_{n}\longrightarrow 1
as
n\to+\infty
. Once the claim is proved the lemma follows, using the bi-Lipschitz character of
f
.

To prove the claim, let us introduce a holomorphic motion parametrized by

\lambda\in B(0,R_{n}),\ R_{n}=1/\|\hat{\mu}_{n}\|_{\infty}\geq n/M,

the normalized straightening
\hat{f}_{n}(\lambda,z)
of
\lambda\hat{\mu}_{n}
.
For a fixed
z
,
\lambda\mapsto f_{n}(\lambda,z)
is holomorphic.
Let us restrict to
\lambda\in B(0,R_{n}/2)
, then
z\mapsto\hat{f}_{n}(\lambda,z)
is a normalized
3
-quasiconformal map.
In particular there exists
R>0
such that for any
z\in K
and
\lambda\in B(0,R_{n}/2)
,

\hat{f}_{n}(\lambda,z)\in B(0,R)
(see
Proposition

57
).
Consider the function
\Phi:\lambda\in B(0,R_{n}/2)\mapsto\hat{f}_{n}(\lambda,v)-\hat{f}_{n}(\lambda,u)
.
It takes its values in
B^{\prime}=B(0,2R)-\{0\}
.
We can use then the hyperbolic metric of
B^{\prime}
whose expression is

\frac{|dz|}{2|z|\log|2R/z|}

because a universal cover thereof is given by
{\operatorname{Re}\,}z<0
,
z\mapsto 2R\exp(z)
.
The distance in
B^{\prime}
between two concentric circles
C(0,r)
and
C(0,r^{\prime})
is

\frac{1}{2}\left|\log\frac{\log(2R/r^{\prime})}{\log(2R/r)}\right|.

Here and below, each occurrence of
\operatorname{cst}
refers to a (possibly) different constant, which is independent of
u
,
v
and
n
.
The hyperbolic distance in
B^{\prime}
between
\Phi(1)=v_{n}-u_{n}
and
\Phi(0)=v-u
is at most the hyperbolic distance from
0
to
1
in
B(0,R_{n}/2)
so it is at most
\operatorname{cst}/n
.
It follows that

e^{-\frac{\operatorname{cst}}{n}}\log|2R/\Phi(0)|\leq\log|2R/\Phi(1)|\leq e^{\frac{\operatorname{cst}}{n}}\log|2R/\Phi(0)|.

Hence

(e^{\frac{\operatorname{cst}}{n}}-1)\log|\Phi(0)/2R|\leq\log|\Phi(1)/\Phi(0)|\leq(e^{-\frac{\operatorname{cst}}{n}}-1)\log|\Phi(0)/2R|.

Hence

\frac{\operatorname{cst}}{n}\log\left|\frac{\Phi(0)}{2R}\right|\leq\log\left|\frac{\Phi(1)}{\Phi(0)}\right|\leq-\frac{\operatorname{cst}}{n}\log\left|\frac{\Phi(0)}{2R}\right|.

Now
|\Phi(0)|\geq\frac{\operatorname{cst}}{n}
hence

\left|\log\left|\frac{\Phi(1)}{\Phi(0)}\right|\right|\leq\operatorname{cst}\frac{\operatorname{cst}+\log n}{n}\leq\operatorname{cst}.

The claim follows.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

5
.

We have

\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}(z)=\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Sing}^{\prime}}\frac{\operatorname{res}s}{z-s}

where
\mathrm{Sing}^{\prime}
is the set of all actual (with non-zero residue) singularities of
\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}
(with
\infty
omitted), which is the same as the set
\mathrm{Sing}
of actual singularities of
\zeta_{n}
with
0
,
1
or
2
points removed and
\infty
omitted.
Let us write

I_{n}:=\int\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}(\gamma_{n})d\gamma_{n}=\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Sing}^{\prime}}\operatorname{res}(s)\int\frac{d\gamma_{n}}{\gamma_{n}-s}.

Then,

I_{n}=\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Sing}^{\prime}}\operatorname{res}(s)\Psi_{n}(s)=\langle\tilde{m}_{n}^{\prime},\Psi_{n}\rangle

where
\Psi_{n}(s)=\int\frac{d\gamma_{n}}{\gamma_{n}-s}
, and is
a determination of
\log\left(\frac{\gamma_{n}(1)-s}{\gamma_{n}(0)-s}\right)
and where
\tilde{m}^{\prime}_{n}
is the sum of Dirac masses at points
s\in\mathrm{Sing}^{\prime}
ponderated by
\operatorname{res}s
; note that
\tilde{m}^{\prime}_{n}
is also equal to
(f_{n})_{*}m_{n}
with at most two residudes removed.

We have for
n
big enough

|{\operatorname{Im}\,}\Psi_{n}|\leq C_{0}

where
C_{0}
is independent from
\varepsilon
and
n
.

Proof.

There exists a real
m>0
such that for all
n
,
\min|\gamma^{\prime}_{n}|\geq m
.
There exists
\eta>0
such that for all
n
big enough,
|t-s|\leq\eta

\implies

|\gamma^{\prime}_{n}(t)-\gamma^{\prime}_{n}(s)|\leq m/10
(by uniform convergence of
\gamma^{\prime}_{n}
to a continuous function).
For any
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
, on an interval
[t_{0},t_{1}]
of values of
t
of size
\eta
(or less), the argument of
\gamma^{\prime}_{n}(t)
deviates less than
\operatorname{asin}(1/10)
from its value at
t=t_{0}
and it follows that the variation of argument of
\gamma_{n}(t)-s
is at most
\pi+\operatorname{asin}(1/10)
on
[t_{0},t_{1}]
.
Slicing
[0,1]
into at most
\left\lceil\frac{1}{\eta}\right\rceil
intervals of length at most
\eta
, we get
|{\operatorname{Im}\,}\Psi_{n}|\leq\left\lceil\frac{1}{\eta}\right\rceil(\pi+\operatorname{asin}(1/10))
.
∎

Let
\varepsilon>0
and consider the set
G_{n}=V_{\varepsilon}(\gamma_{n})
defined as the
\varepsilon
-neighbourhood
\gamma_{n}
.
By
Proposition

57
, the sets
f_{n}^{-1}(G_{n})
are all contained in some common closed ball
K=\overline{B}(0,R)
.
By increasing
R
, we also assume that
K
contains for all
n
the corners of the litte squares on which the average of
\mu
is not
0
. In particular,
f_{n}(K)
contains
\operatorname{Sing}
and
G_{n}
.

Let us write

I_{n}=J_{n}+J^{\prime}_{n}

where

J^{\prime}_{n}=\sum_{s\in G_{n}\cap\mathrm{Sing}^{\prime}}\operatorname{res}(s)\Psi_{n}(s)

J_{n}=\sum_{s\in T_{n}}\operatorname{res}(s)\Psi_{n}(s)

where
T_{n}=\mathrm{Sing}^{\prime}-G_{n}
.

Note: In the rest of this proof, inequalities involving constants
C_{k}
for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
will appear. They will be valid for all
\varepsilon
and
n
subjected to the conditions
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{k}
and
n>n_{k}
, where
\varepsilon_{k}
and
n_{k}
are constants that will be implied.

Bound on
J^{\prime}_{n}
:

We have

\operatorname{Leb}G_{n}\leq C_{1}\varepsilon

for some
C_{1}
,
and for all
s\in\mathrm{Sing}
,

|\operatorname{res}s|\leq C_{2}/n^{2}

for some
C_{2}
.

Because of
Lemma

58
, the disks of radius
C_{3}/2n
around the actuals singularities are disjoint, because their preimage by
f_{n}
lie in
K
. So this is the case for the
s\in G_{n}\cap\mathrm{Sing}^{\prime}
. Moreover, as soon as
C_{3}/2n<\varepsilon
then these disks are contained in
G_{n}(2\varepsilon)
, so the sum of the areas of these disks is at most
2C_{1}\varepsilon
. It follows that

\#(G_{n}\cap\mathrm{Sing}^{\prime})\leq\frac{2C_{1}\varepsilon}{\pi(C_{3}/2n)^{2}}=C_{4}\varepsilon n^{2}

for some
C_{4}
.

Case 1:
\gamma_{n}(0)=\gamma_{n}(1)
. Then
\Psi_{n}
is purely imaginary and hence is bounded, according to one of the remarks above. Hence

|J^{\prime}_{n}|\leq\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Sing^{\prime}}\cap G_{n}}|\operatorname{res}(s)\Psi_{n}(s)|\leq\#(\mathrm{Sing^{\prime}}\cap G_{n})\times\max|\operatorname{res}s|\times\max|\Psi_{n}(s)|

\leq C_{4}\varepsilon n^{2}\times\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}}\times C_{0}=C_{4}C_{2}C_{0}\varepsilon.

Case 2:
\gamma_{n}(0)\neq\gamma_{n}(1)
.

We have

|\Psi_{n}(s)|<\log\frac{1}{|\gamma_{n}(0)-s|}+\log\frac{1}{|\gamma_{n}(1)-s|}+C_{5}

for some
C_{5}
.

Proof.

We have already seen that
|{\operatorname{Im}\,}\Psi_{n}(s)|\leq C_{0}
.
The real part is easier to deal with, since

{\operatorname{Re}\,}\Psi_{n}(s)=\log\frac{|\gamma_{n}(1)-s|}{|\gamma_{n}(0)-s|}=\log\frac{1}{|\gamma_{n}(0)-s|}-\log\frac{1}{|\gamma_{n}(1)-s|}.

For any
t
,
\left|\log\frac{1}{|\gamma_{n}(t)-s|}\right|\leq\log\frac{1}{|\gamma_{n}(t)-s|}+C
for
C=\max(0,2\log\operatorname{diam}B)
where
B
is a compact set containing all the curves
\gamma_{n}
and the singularities of non-vanishing residue of
\zeta_{n}
for all
n
. The constant
C
is independent of
\varepsilon
and
n
.
∎

For any singularity
s
at distance
\geq\varepsilon
from
\gamma_{n}(0)
we have

\log\frac{1}{|\gamma_{n}(0)-s|}\leq\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}.

For the singularities
s\in\mathrm{Sing}^{\prime}
at distance
<\varepsilon
from
\gamma_{n}(0)
we have
r_{n}\leq|s-\gamma_{n}(0)|<\varepsilon
with
r_{n}=\exp(-n)
.
Recall that the disks
B(s,C_{3}/2n)
are disjoint.
There is hence at most one
s_{0}\in\mathrm{Sing}^{\prime}
with
|s_{0}-\gamma_{n}(0)|<C_{3}/2n
and since
|s_{0}-\gamma_{n}(0)|>e^{-n}
we have

\log\frac{1}{|\gamma_{n}(0)-s_{0}|}\leq n.

For the other ones, let
k
be the smallest integer such that
2^{k}\frac{C_{3}}{2n}>\varepsilon
.
Recall that we assume that
n
is big enough so that
C_{3}/2n<\varepsilon
, hence
k>0
. We have

2^{k}\leq\frac{4n\varepsilon}{C_{3}}.

Let
j\in{\mathbb{N}}\cap[0,k)
. The number of
s\in\mathrm{Sing}^{\prime}
such that
|\gamma_{n}(0)-s|
belongs to
[2^{j}C_{3}/2n,2^{j+1}C_{3}/2n)
is at most
\pi\big((2^{j+1}+1)^{2}-(2^{j}-1)^{2}\big)=\pi(3\times 4^{j}+6\times 2^{j}\leq 9\pi\times 4^{j}
, because the union of the disjoint disks
B(s,C_{3}/2n)
is contained in the round annulus of
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that
(2^{j+1}+1)C_{3}/2n<|z-s|<(2^{j}-1)C_{3}/2n
.
For such an
s
, we have

\log\frac{1}{|\gamma_{n}(0)-s|}\leq\log\frac{2n}{2^{j}C_{3}}.

Hence

\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Sing^{\prime}}\cap B(\gamma_{n}(0),\varepsilon)}\log\frac{1}{|\gamma_{n}(0)-s|}\leq n+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}9\pi\times 4^{j}\times\log\frac{2n}{2^{j}C_{3}}

Now

\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}4^{j}\times\log\frac{2n}{2^{j}C_{3}}=4^{k}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\frac{1}{4^{k-j}}\log 2^{k-j}\frac{2n}{2^{k}C_{3}}=4^{k}\sum_{p=1}^{k}\frac{1}{4^{p}}\left(p\log 2+\log\frac{2n}{2^{k}C_{3}}\right)

\leq 4^{k}\sum_{p=1}^{k}\frac{1}{4^{p}}\left(p\log 2+\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\leq 4^{k}\left(\frac{4}{9}\log 2+\frac{1}{3}\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)

\leq\left(\frac{4n\varepsilon}{C_{3}}\right)^{2}\times\left(\frac{4}{9}\log 2+\frac{1}{3}\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)

Hence

\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Sing^{\prime}}\cap B(\gamma_{n}(0),\varepsilon)}\log\frac{1}{|\gamma_{n}(0)-s|}\leq n+C_{6}n^{2}\varepsilon^{2}+C_{7}n^{2}\varepsilon^{2}\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}.

Let us put it all together (assuming
\varepsilon\leq 1
): using
\max|\operatorname{res}s|\leq C_{2}/n^{2}
we have

|J^{\prime}_{n}|\leq\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}}\left(\sum\log\frac{1}{|\gamma_{n}(0)-s|}+\sum\log\frac{1}{|\gamma_{n}(1)-s|}+\sum C_{5}\right);

separating the sum of
\log\frac{1}{|\gamma_{n}(0)-s|}
according to whether or not
|\gamma_{n}(0)-s|\geq\varepsilon
, and then regrouping the terms and using that there are at most
C_{4}\varepsilon n^{2}
values of
s
as we saw earlier:

|J^{\prime}_{n}|\leq\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}}\left(C_{4}\varepsilon n^{2}\times\Big(2\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+C_{5}\Big)+n+C_{6}n^{2}\varepsilon^{2}+C_{7}n^{2}\varepsilon^{2}\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)

=C_{2}\times\left(C_{4}\varepsilon\times\Big(2\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+C_{5}\Big)+\frac{1}{n}+C_{6}\varepsilon^{2}+C_{7}\varepsilon^{2}\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right).

Hence

\limsup|J^{\prime}_{n}|\leq C_{8}\varepsilon\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}

for some
C_{8}
.
The important fact is that this bound tends to
0
as
\varepsilon\longrightarrow 0
.

Bound on
J_{n}
:

Similarly to the definition of
G_{n}
as the
\varepsilon
-neighbourhood of
\gamma_{n}
, we let
G
be the
\varepsilon
-neighbourhood of
\gamma
.
For
s\notin\gamma([0,1])
, let
\Psi(s)=\int\frac{d\gamma}{\gamma-s}
, which is also the limit of the functions
\Psi_{n}
.
Similarly as for
G_{n}
we have that
|{\operatorname{Im}\,}\Psi|\leq C_{0}
and

{\operatorname{Re}\,}\Psi(s)=\log\frac{|\gamma(1)-s|}{|\gamma(0)-s|}=\log\frac{1}{|\gamma(0)-s|}-\log\frac{1}{|\gamma(1)-s|}.

Note that
|\Psi(z)|
and
|\Psi_{n}(z)|
tend to
0
as
|z|\longrightarrow+\infty
.
There exists a sequence
\tilde{\Psi}_{n}
of continuous extensions of the restriction of
\Psi_{n}
to the closed set
{\mathbb{C}}-G_{n}
and a continuous extension
\tilde{\Psi}
of the restriction of
\Psi
to the closed set
{\mathbb{C}}-G
, such that moreover
\tilde{\Psi}_{n}
tends to
\tilde{\Psi}
uniformly on compact subsets of
{\mathbb{C}}
and such that
\|\tilde{\Psi}\|_{\infty}=\|\Psi|_{{\mathbb{C}}-G}\|_{\infty}
.
This follows from the Tietze extension theorem (Theorem 35.1 page 219 of
[
Mun00
]
). Apply it first to extend
\Psi|_{{\mathbb{C}}-G}
into
\tilde{\Psi}
.
Then apply it again to the metric space
X=(\{0\}\cup\big\{{1/n}\,\big|\,{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}}\big\})\times{\mathbb{C}}
, the continuous function that is the disjoint union of
\tilde{\Psi}
and the restrictions of the
\Psi_{n}
(the disjoint union of the domains of these maps is indeed a closed subset of
X
).

Now

J_{n}-\int\tilde{m}^{\prime}_{n}\tilde{\Psi}_{n}=\sum_{s\in G_{n}\cap\mathrm{Sing}^{\prime}}\operatorname{res}(s)\tilde{\Psi}_{n}(s)

Hence

\left|J_{n}-\int\tilde{m}^{\prime}_{n}\tilde{\Psi}_{n}\right|\leq\max_{s\in\mathrm{Sing}^{\prime}}|\operatorname{res}(s)|\times\#(G_{n}\cap\mathrm{Sing}^{\prime})\times\max_{G_{n}}\big|\tilde{\Psi}_{n}\big|

\leq\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}}\times C_{4}n^{2}\varepsilon^{2}\times\max_{n}\max_{G_{n}}|\tilde{\Psi}_{n}|\leq C_{7}\varepsilon^{2}

for some
C_{7}
.
Also,

\left|\int\tilde{m}^{\prime}_{n}\tilde{\Psi}_{n}-\int\tilde{m}^{\prime}_{n}\tilde{\Psi}\right|\leq\|\tilde{m}^{\prime}_{n}\|\|\tilde{\Psi}_{n}-\tilde{\Psi}\|_{\infty}

and as
n\longrightarrow+\infty
, the left factor stays bounded and the right one tends to
0
.
Let
m^{\prime}=f_{*}m
.
By
Corollary

52
(removing two singularities does not change the convergence, since the total mass removed is
\cal O(1/n^{2})
) we have

\int\tilde{m}^{\prime}_{n}\tilde{\Psi}\longrightarrow\int m^{\prime}\tilde{\Psi}

when
n\longrightarrow+\infty
.
Recall that
\|\tilde{\Psi}\|_{\infty}=\|\Psi|_{{\mathbb{C}}-G}\|_{\infty}
.
Since
\Psi
is a branch of
\log\left(\frac{\gamma(1)-s}{\gamma(0)-s}\right)
, and since on
{\mathbb{C}}-G
the distance to
\gamma
is at least
\varepsilon
, we get for all
s\in{\mathbb{C}}-G
:

|{\operatorname{Re}\,}\Psi(s)|\leq C_{8}+\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}

for some
C_{8}>0
. Since, as we saw,
|{\operatorname{Im}\,}\Psi|\leq C_{0}
we get

\|\tilde{\Psi}\|_{\infty}\leq C_{0}+C_{8}+\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}.

Now
m=h\operatorname{Leb}
where
h
is a continuous function so
m^{\prime}=f_{*}m=g\operatorname{Leb}
with
g(f(z))=h(z)/\det Df
is continuous too.
In particular
g
is bounded, say by
C_{9}
.
We have
\operatorname{Leb}G\leq C_{1}\varepsilon
, like for
G_{n}
.
It follows that

\left|\int_{G}m^{\prime}\tilde{\Psi}\right|\leq C_{9}\varepsilon\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}

for some
C_{9}
, and this quantity tends to
0
as
\varepsilon\longrightarrow 0
.
Concerning the original function
\Psi
, from the expression of
{\operatorname{Re}\,}\Psi
and the bound on
{\operatorname{Im}\,}\Psi
, we get

\left|\int_{G}m^{\prime}\Psi\right|\leq C_{10}\varepsilon+C_{9}\varepsilon^{2}\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}.

If we collect all the results, we know that for all
\eta>0
, by taking
\varepsilon
small enough, then for all
n
big enough, the quantity

I_{n}
to be evaluated, differs from
\int_{\mathbb{C}}m^{\prime}\Psi
by at most
\eta
, i.e.

\left|I_{n}-\int_{\mathbb{C}}m^{\prime}\Psi\right|<\eta.

This integral
\int_{\mathbb{C}}m^{\prime}\Psi
is equal to
\int\zeta(\gamma(t))\gamma^{\prime}(t)dt
:
indeed, recall that
\zeta=m^{\prime}*\frac{1}{z}
,
whence

\int\zeta(\gamma(t))\gamma^{\prime}(t)dt=\int_{t\in[0,1]}\left(\int_{s\in{\mathbb{C}}}\frac{1}{\gamma(t)-s}dm^{\prime}(s)\right)\gamma^{\prime}(t)dt

=\int_{s\in{\mathbb{C}}}\left(\int_{t\in[0,1]}\frac{d\gamma(t)}{\gamma(t)-s}\right)dm^{\prime}(s)=\int_{\mathbb{C}}m^{\prime}\Psi.

The permutation in the integral is justified because the integral of the absolute value

\int_{s\in{\mathbb{C}}}\frac{1}{|z-s|}dm^{\prime}(s)=\int_{s\in{\mathbb{C}}}\frac{g(s)}{|z-s|}d\operatorname{Leb}(s)

is convergent (
g
is bounded with compact support,
z\mapsto 1/|z|
is
L^{1}
on any compact set) and bounded for
z\in\gamma([0,1])
, so we are in the conditions of the Fubini Tonelli theorem.
∎

Remark
.

In the statement of
Theorem

5
, we decided to restrict to pathes avoiding the singularities and to remove the 0, 1 or 2 singularities that are too close to the extremities of the path.
We could have proceeded differently.
For instance we could have allowed for every paths by either:

-

removing the 0, 1 or 2 aforementioned singularities, and for every other singularity
s
crossed by
\gamma_{n}
, give a meaning to
\operatorname{res}(s)\int\frac{d\gamma_{n}}{\gamma_{n}-s}
if
\gamma_{n}^{\prime}
is continuous at all
t
for which
\gamma_{n}(t)=s
, or if the jump of
\arg\gamma_{n}^{\prime}
at
t
is smaller than, say,
\pi/2
;

-

chopping off
\zeta_{n}
close to some/all poles, for instance by setting
\zeta_{n}(z)=0
when
\exists s
singularity such that
|z-s|<r^{\prime}_{n}
, for an appropriate sequence
r^{\prime}_{n}
;

-

there are several choice in
r^{\prime}_{n}
and the way
\zeta_{n}
is chopped off; for instance one can arrange so that
\zeta_{n}
remains continuous;

-

one could instead subtract all the poles of
\zeta
that are at distance
<r^{\prime}_{n}
to
\gamma
, for an appropriate sequence
r^{\prime}_{n}
.

Other approaches are possible.

Interpreting the limit

Let us sum up what we have done up to now. Recall the similarity surfaces
\cal S_{n}
associated to our approximating Beltrami forms
\mu_{n}
, and recall their completions
\cal S^{\prime}_{n}
into a Riemann surface isomorphic to
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
.
To this global chart
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
we associated a singular connection, of symbol
\zeta_{n}
that is a meromorphic function
44
44

44

… A rational map in fact, since for each
n
there are only finitely many non-erasable poles.
all whose poles are all simple, but get more and more densely packed as
n
tends to infinity.
We proved that
\zeta_{n}
has a weak limit
45
45

45

In the previous section,
Theorem

5
, we improved the weak limit statement into a statement in terms of limit of associated parallel transport.
and that this weak limit is the function
\zeta
introduced in
Proposition

53
via a convolution product.

Theorem 6
.

Let
(e_{1},e_{2})
be the canonical basis of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Let
A
be the push-forward by
f
of the constant vector field
e_{1}
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, and
B
the push-forward of the constant vector field
e_{2}
.
Note that
A
and
B
commute.
Consider the conformal symmetric connection of symbol

\tilde{\zeta}(z)=-\frac{\partial_{A}B}{AB}

defined on
U
.
Then
\tilde{\zeta}
is equal to the symbol
\zeta
introduced in
Proposition

53
:

\tilde{\zeta}=\zeta.

The connection of symbol
\tilde{\zeta}
expresses as

\nabla_{X}Y=\partial_{X}Y-\partial_{B}A\frac{XY}{AB}.

Before we prove the theorem, let us introduce an easy lemma.

Lemma 59
.

Let
A
and
B
be two vector fields in an open subset
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\equiv{\mathbb{C}}
. Assume that they vanish nowhere.
Then there is a unique conformal symmetric connection
\nabla
such that the parallel transport under integral lines of
A
leaves
B
invariant.
The symbol of
\nabla
in the chart
U
is

-\frac{\partial_{A}B}{AB}.

Proof.

A conformal symmetric connection
\nabla
takes the form

\nabla_{X}Y=\partial_{X}Y+\zeta XY

where
\zeta
is a continuous function from
U
to
{\mathbb{C}}
.
The condition expresses as the pair the equation

\nabla_{A}B=0,

i.e.

\partial_{A}B+\zeta AB=0.

∎

Incidentally, if
A
and
B
commute, then

-\frac{\partial_{A}B}{AB}=-\frac{\partial_{B}A}{AB},

i.e. the unique conformal symmetric connection such that the parallel transport under integral lines of
A
leaves
B
invariant coincides with the unique conformal symmetric connection such that the parallel transport under integral lines of
B
leaves
A
invariant.

We will also need the following, harder to get, result.
Let, as earlier,

r_{n}=\exp(-n)

and recall that
f
is
C^{1}
(
Lemma

54
).

Proposition 60
.

\sup_{\operatorname{dist}(z,\frac{1}{n}{\mathbb{Z}})>r_{n}}\|D_{z}f_{n}-D_{z}f\|\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

where the norm is any operator norm on the set of
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear self-maps of
{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

In
Section

8
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

6
.

By
Lemma

59
, to prove the claim, it is enough to prove that for
\zeta
, the associated parallel transport along integral lines of
A
leave
B
invariant, where
A=f_{*}e_{1}
and
B=f_{*}e_{2}
.

We will use the fact that this parallel transport is the limit of the parallel transport associated to
\zeta_{n}
(
Theorem

5
).
Let
\gamma:[0,1]\to{\mathbb{C}}
defined by

\gamma(z)=f(x_{0}+(x_{1}-x_{0})t+iy_{0})

for some
\ell>0
and let for
n
big enough

\gamma_{n}(t)=f_{n}(x_{0}+(x_{1}-x_{0})t+iy_{n})

where
y_{n}
is chosen so that
y_{n}\longrightarrow y_{0}
and
d(y_{n},\frac{1}{n}{\mathbb{Z}})>r_{n}
: for instance
y_{n}=(0.5+\lfloor ny_{0}\rfloor)/n
.

The path
\gamma
is
C^{1}
and the path
\gamma_{1}
is
C^{1}
by parts.
By
Section

5.4
,
\gamma_{n}\longrightarrow\gamma
uniformly,
and by
Proposition

60

\gamma_{n}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\gamma^{\prime}
uniformly.

f_{n}

Figure 16.
Illustration of an argument in the proof of
Proposition

60
.

Now, recall that the parallel transport for
\zeta_{n}
is also the parallel transport associated to the similarity surface
\cal S_{n}
built from gluing together the images of the small squares by maps of the form
az+b\bar{z}
where
a
and
b
are any complex constants such that
b/a\in{\mathbb{D}}
is the value of
\mu_{n}
on this square. The image of such a square is a parallelogram
The horizontal line
x_{0}+(x_{1}-x_{0})t+iy_{n}
runs through a row of those squares. Consider the corresponding parallelograms.
We can place the initial parallelogram so that the image of the vertical edge of the square is a vertical edge with the same orientation and same size
1/n
.
Then its opposite side is parallel and has the same size.
If we scale rotate and place the other parallelograms in this row so as to match this side, all the images of the vertical sides remain vertical with the same length
1/n
. See
Figure

16
.
One deduces that if we define the vector
v_{n}
as the image by
f_{n}
of the vertical unit vector
e_{2}
attached to
x_{0}+iy_{n}
, then
the parallel transport by
\nabla_{\zeta_{n}}
of
v_{n}
along
\gamma_{n}
remains for all
t
the image
v_{n}(t)
by
f_{n}
of the vertical vector
e_{2}
attached to
x_{0}+(x_{1}-x_{0})t+iy_{n}
.
Now by
Proposition

60
and continuity of
Df
, for each fixed
t
, the vector
v_{n}(t)
tends as
n\longrightarrow+\infty
to the push-forward
v(t)
by
f
of
e_{2}
attached to
x_{0}+(x_{1}-x_{0})t+iy_{0}
. Note that
v(t)=B(\gamma(t))
.

We can now invoke
Theorem

5
: at the limit, the parallel transport along
\gamma
of
v(0)=B(\gamma(0))
is the vector
v(t)=B(\gamma(t))
.
∎

Unlike the case of
\tilde{\zeta}
, we have not been able to give an interesting expression of the curvature form
\omega=\bar{\partial}\tilde{\zeta}
in terms of
A
and

: a direct application of
\bar{\partial}
to the expression
(\partial_{B}A)/AB
seems to give rise to a complicated expression.

8.
On averaging the Beltrami form

In this article, up to now we used the average of the complex-valued function
\mu(z)
, assumed
L^{\infty}
in
or
C^{2}
in
, over the Lebesgue measure on squares. But the Beltrami differential is just one way to represent ellipse fields. We could have taken another quantity
\nu=\nu(\mu)
, like
(1+\mu)/(1-\mu)
, or even functions of
\mu
that are not holomorphic like
\frac{1+|\mu|}{1-|\mu|}e^{i\arg\mu}
.

So, what if we averaged
\nu
instead of
\mu
? In other words, given
n
, we take
\mu_{n}
on each small square
S
in the construction to be constant equal to
\nu^{-1}\left(\frac{\int\nu\circ\mu}{\int 1}\right)
, where the integrals are still against the Lebesgue measure on
S
.
Note that the quantity
\frac{\int\nu\circ\mu}{\int 1}
belongs to the convex hull of the image of
\nu
.

We assume that
\mu\mapsto\nu(\mu)
is a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism from the unit disk to a convex open subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and ask two questions:

(1)

Does the sequence
f_{n}
of normalized straightenings of
\mu_{n}
still converge to the straightening of
\mu
?

(2)

Assuming that
\nu
is
C^{2}
, in the setting of
, are the limit complex measure
m
, curvature form
\omega
and Christoffel symbol
\zeta
the same, and does the parallel transport still converge?

The answer to both questions is: yes.

For point (
1
), the proof of convergence (
Section

5.4
) relied on uniqueness of the straightening of
\mu
(
Theorem

3
) and on the sole facts that
\|\mu_{n}\|_{\infty}
is bounded away from
1
and that locally, the
L^{1}
norm of
\mu_{n}-\mu
tends to
0
(
Lemma

40
), which still hold: indeed
\nu\circ\mu_{n}
is the average of the bounded measurable function
\nu\circ\mu
on the little squares of the
n
-the generation; using the same proof as in
Lemma

40
we get that for all compact subset
S
of
{\mathbb{C}}
,
\|\nu\circ\mu_{n}-\nu\circ\mu\|_{L^{1}(S)}\longrightarrow 0
as
n\longrightarrow+\infty
.
The set
\nu\circ\mu({\mathbb{C}})
in
{\mathbb{C}}
is compactly contained in the image of
\nu
hence its convex
C
hull too. It follows that
\|\mu_{n}\|_{\infty}
is bounded away from
1
. It also follows that the map
\nu^{-1}
is uniformly Lipschitz on
C
, hence
\|\mu_{n}-\mu\|_{L^{1}(S)}=\|\nu^{-1}\circ\nu\circ\mu_{n}-\nu^{-1}\circ\nu\circ\mu\|_{L^{1}(S)}\leq C\|\nu\circ\mu_{n}-\nu\circ\mu\|_{L^{1}(S)}
for some constant
C>0
independent of
n
.

For point (
2
), recall that
\mu
is in this case also
C^{2}
, hence continuous.
So in four squares around a corner,
\mu
takes values that remain close to the value
\mu_{0}
of
\mu
at the corner.
Below, all the
o(\cdot)
are uniform in
|m|
small enough,
\mu_{0}
subject to
|\mu_{0}|<\kappa<1
and
z_{0}\in{\mathbb{C}}
.
Let

\nu(\mu_{0}+m)=\nu_{0}+Q_{1}(m)+Q_{2}(m)+o(|m|^{2})

be an expansion where
Q_{k}
is a degree-
k

{\mathbb{R}}
-homogeneous polynomial in
({\operatorname{Re}\,}m,{\operatorname{Im}\,}m)
taking values in
{\mathbb{C}}
seen as a dimension 2
{\mathbb{R}}
-vector space.
Let

\mu(z_{0}+z)=\mu_{0}+P_{1}(z)+P_{2}(z)+o(|z|^{2})

with a similar convention.
Note that
P_{1}
and
Q_{1}
are
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear endomorphisms of
{\mathbb{C}}
and that
Q_{1}
is invertible by hypothesis that
\nu
is a diffeomorphism.
Then

\nu^{-1}(\nu_{0}+v)=\mu_{0}+Q_{1}^{-1}(v)-Q_{1}^{-1}\circ Q_{2}\circ Q_{1}^{-1}(v)+o(|v|^{2}),

and

\nu\circ\mu(z_{0}+z)=\nu_{0}+Q_{1}\circ P_{1}(z)+Q_{1}\circ P_{2}(z)+Q_{2}\circ P_{1}(z)+o(|z|)^{2}.

Let
M_{j}
be the average of
\mu
on the squares
C_{j}
of side length
\varepsilon
appearing in the proof of
.
We have

M_{j}=\mu_{0}+P_{1}\big(\operatorname{\underset{C_{j}}{\operatorname{avg}}}z\big)+\operatorname{\underset{C_{j}}{\operatorname{avg}}}\big(P_{2}(z)\big)+o(\varepsilon^{2})

Denote
N_{j}
the average of
\nu\circ\mu
on
C_{j}
.
We get

N_{j}=\nu_{0}+Q_{1}\circ P_{1}\big(\operatorname{\underset{C_{j}}{\operatorname{avg}}}z\big)+Q_{1}\Big(\operatorname{\underset{C_{j}}{\operatorname{avg}}}P_{2}(z)\Big)+\operatorname{\underset{C_{j}}{\operatorname{avg}}}\big(Q_{2}\circ P_{1}(z)\big)+o(\varepsilon^{2})

Let
M_{j}^{\prime}=\nu^{-1}(N_{j})
.
Composing the previous formula with the expansion of
\nu^{-1}
and comparing to
M_{j}
we get

M^{\prime}_{j}=M_{j}+Q_{1}^{-1}\Big(\operatorname{\underset{C_{j}}{\operatorname{avg}}}\big(Q_{2}\circ P_{1}(z)\big)-Q_{2}\circ P_{1}\big(\operatorname{\underset{C_{j}}{\operatorname{avg}}}z\big)\Big)+o(\varepsilon^{2})

We have
Q_{2}\circ P_{1}(x+iy)=a_{xx}x^{2}+2a_{xy}xy+a_{yy}y^{2}
for some coefficients
a_{\ldots}\in{\mathbb{C}}={\mathbb{R}}+i{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then

\operatorname{\underset{C_{j}}{\operatorname{avg}}}\big(Q_{2}\circ P_{1}(z)\big)-Q_{2}\circ P_{1}\big(\operatorname{\underset{C_{j}}{\operatorname{avg}}}z\big)=(a_{xx}+a_{yy})\left(\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{4}\right)\varepsilon^{2}=B\varepsilon^{2}

where
B\in{\mathbb{C}}
depends on
z_{0}
.
So

M^{\prime}_{j}=M_{j}+Q_{1}^{-1}(B)\varepsilon^{2}+o(\varepsilon^{2}).

Note that the term
Q_{1}^{-1}(B)\varepsilon^{2}
depends on
z_{0}
but is independent of
j
.
Then the monodromy factor of the singularity for the new scheme is

\Lambda^{\prime}=\frac{L^{\prime}_{1}\times L^{\prime}_{3}}{L^{\prime}_{0}\times L^{\prime}_{2}}

where
L^{\prime}_{j}=(1+M^{\prime}_{j})(1-M^{\prime}_{j})
,
whereas for the original one it is

\Lambda=\frac{L_{1}\times L_{3}}{L_{0}\times L_{2}}

where
L_{j}=(1+M_{j})(1-M_{j})
.
Recall that
1+M_{j}
is close to
1+\mu_{0}
when
\varepsilon
is close to
0
.
A computation gives

\frac{L^{\prime}_{j}}{L_{j}}=1+Q_{1}^{-1}(B)\left(\frac{1}{1+\mu_{0}}+\frac{1}{1-\mu_{0}}\right)\varepsilon^{2}+o(\varepsilon^{2})=1+B^{\prime}\varepsilon^{2}+o(\varepsilon^{2})

Where
B^{\prime}
depends on
z_{0}
but not on
j
.
As a consequence,

\frac{\Lambda^{\prime}}{\Lambda}=1+o(\varepsilon^{2}).

Hence the limit of
\log_{p}\Lambda^{\prime}/\varepsilon^{2}
is the same as the limit of
\log_{p}\Lambda/\varepsilon^{2}
.
One can check that the convergence is still uniform.
The proof of
Proposition

53
adapts verbatim to the new situation so we still have

\zeta_{n}\xrightharpoonup{\phantom{m}}\zeta:=(f_{*}m)*\frac{1}{z}

for the new
\zeta_{n}
, where
\zeta
and
m
stay the same.
In
Lemma

58
one argument is that
\sup_{\mathbb{C}}|\hat{\mu}_{n}|\leq M/n
for some
M>0
.
It is based on the fact that
\|\mu_{n}-\mu\|_{\infty}=\cal O(1/n)
, which is still true here (
\nu\circ\mu
is
C^{2}
, hence
C^{1}
, with compact support, so its variation on a square of side
1/n
is
\cal O(1/n)
; hence its average will differ from the central value by at most
\cal O(1/n)
(in fact we have better than that); the preimage of this average by
\nu
also differs by the same order since everything takes place in a compact subset of the domain of
\nu^{-1}
which is at least
C^{1}
).
Several of the arguments of
Theorem

5
, are based on the fact that that the residues are
\cal O(1/n^{2})
, and this is still the case here. Also, we have seen that
f_{n}
still tends to
f
.
So Point (
2
) holds.

Non-holomorphy

On the other hand, in the case of holomorphic families of Beltrami forms, if
\mu\mapsto\nu(\mu)
is not assumed holomorphic, we lose the holomorphic dependence, with respect to the parameter, of
\mu_{n}
. For instance let
\nu(x+iy)=x+x^{3}+iy
and
\mu(x+iy)=tx
for
|x+iy|<0.1
where
t=u+iv
is a complex parameter in
{\mathbb{D}}
(choose any
C^{2}
extension for
|x+iy|>0.1
).
Then when
n>100
, near
0
we have on the square
{\operatorname{Re}\,}z\in[0,1/n]
,
{\operatorname{Im}\,}z\in[0,1/n]
that

\mu_{n}=P^{-1}(\frac{u}{2n}+\frac{u^{3}}{4n^{3}})+\frac{v}{2n}i

where
P(x)=x+x^{3}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. This quantity does not depend holomorphically on
u+iv
.

This has the consequence that we also loose holomorphic dependence in the parameter of the normalized straightening
f_{n}
of
\mu_{n}
. Indeed, if
f_{n}
depends holomorphically on
t
, then we prove below that
\mu_{n}=\bar{\partial}f_{n}/\partial f_{n}
also depends holomorphically on
t
, leading to a contradiction.

In fact we will prove a slightly more general lemma, probably already known:

Lemma 61
.

Let
t\in B(0,\varepsilon)\to f_{t}
be a family of normalized
K
-quasiconformal homemomorphisms of
{\mathbb{C}}
for some
K>1
.
Assume that for all
z
, the map
t\mapsto f_{t}(z)
is holomorphic.
Then the Beltrami differential
\mu_{t}
of
f_{t}
, as an element of the Banach space
\cal B=L^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}})
, depends analytically on
t
in the sense of
Proposition

47
.

Proof.

It is enough to prove holomorphic dependence near
t=0
.
We fix some ball
B
in
{\mathbb{C}}
that we will allow to grow near the end of the proof.
For all test function
\tau
we have
\langle f_{t},\tau\rangle
that depends holomorphically too (
f_{t}
satisfies local bounds that are uniform w.r.t.
t
varying in a compact set).
Applying this to a test function that is a partial derivative of another test function, we get that criterion (
5
) of
Proposition

46
is satisfied for the Banach space
L^{1}(B)
(a bound on
Df_{t}
in
L^{1}(B)
is needed: it follows for instancee from the
L^{2}
bound of
Lemma

43
).
It follows that the distribution derivatives of
f_{t}
, seen as
L^{1}
functions on
B
, are holomorphic w.r.t.
t
in the sense of criterion (
1
) of
Proposition

46
, and so are
\partial f_{t}
and
\bar{\partial}f_{t}
: there are
a_{n}
,
b_{n}
in
L^{1}(B)
and some
r>0
such that for all
t
with
|t|<r
,
\partial f_{t}=\sum a_{n}t^{n}
and
\bar{\partial}f_{t}=\sum b_{n}t^{n}
in the sense of distributions, with
\sum\left(\int_{B}|a_{n}|\right)r^{n}<+\infty
and
\sum\left(\int_{B}|b_{n}|\right)r^{n}<+\infty
.
Consider representatives
A_{n}
and
B_{n}
of
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
.
There is a subset
E
of
B
of Lebesgue measure
0
for which, for all
z\in B-E
the sums
\sum|A_{n}(z)|r^{n}
and
\sum|A_{n}(z)|r^{n}
converge.
For those
z
, the functions
t\mapsto A(z,t)=\sum A_{n}(z)t^{n}
and
B(z,t)=\sum B_{n}(z)r^{n}
are holomorphic.
We define
A(z,t)=0
and
B(z,t)=0
when
z\in E
.
The functions
A
and
B
are measurable.
The partial sums of the series
\sum a_{n}t^{n}
tend to
function
z\mapsto A(z,t)
in
L^{1}(B)
so the latter is a representative of
\sum a_{n}t^{n}
. A similar statement holds for
\sum b_{n}t^{n}
.
Then for all
t
,
z\mapsto A(z,t)
is a representative of
\partial f_{t}
and
z\mapsto B(z,t)
is a representative of
\bar{\partial}f_{t}
.
Moreover we know by properties of quasiconformal maps (see
) that for all
t
,
\partial f_{t}
is non-zero on the complement of a set (that depends a priori on
t
) of measure
0
.
So the set of
z
such that the function
t\mapsto A(z,t)
is identically equal to
0
can only have measure
0
.
So the function
B/A
is meromorphic in
t
for almost every
z
.
For all
t
with
|t|<r
, the function
z\mapsto B(z,t)/A(z,t)
is the quotient of representatives of respectively
\bar{\partial}f_{t}
and
\partial f_{t}
, so it is almost everywhere equal to
\mu_{t}
, which is bounded by some
\kappa<1
.
By measurability,
|B/A|>\kappa
defines a measurable set, hence
for almost every
z
, the inequality
|B/A|\leq\kappa
holds for almost every
t
. In particular the meromorphic function
t\mapsto B(z,t)/A(z,t)
has all its poles removable. Let us extend this function into a holomorphic function of
t\in B(0,r)
.
Then the extended function is measurable in
(z,t)
and can serve as the function
\mu_{t}(z)
in criterion (
3
) of
Proposition

47
.
∎

Appendix K Proof of
Proposition

60

Outside a uniform ball
\overline{B}(0,R^{\prime})
, the maps
f
and
f_{n}
are all holomorphic, injective, and tend to infinity at infinity.
In particular their derivatives
f_{n}^{\prime}(z)
and
f^{\prime}(z)
all have a limit as
z\to\infty
.
Moreover
f_{n}
tends to
f
uniformly on compact subsets of
{\mathbb{C}}
, according to
Section

5.4
. It follows, by properties of analytic maps, that
f_{n}^{\prime}
tends to
f_{n}
uniformly on
{\mathbb{C}}-\overline{B}(0,R^{\prime}+1)
. So the claim is proved near infinity and there remains to prove it on
\overline{B}(0,R^{\prime}+1)
.

Consider as in
Lemma

58

\widehat{f}_{n}=f_{n}\circ f^{-1}.

Since
f
is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism, it is enough to prove the following claim: for all
\varepsilon>0
, and all compact subset
K
of
{\mathbb{C}}
,

\sup_{\scriptstyle z\in K,\,\operatorname{dist}(z,\operatorname{Sing})>\varepsilon r_{n}}\|D_{z}\widehat{f}_{n}-\operatorname{Id}\|\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0,

where we recall that
\operatorname{Sing}
is the set of actual singularities of
\zeta_{n}
(with
\infty
omitted).
We have the following stronger version of
Lemma

58
:

Lemma 62
.

For all compact subset
K
of
{\mathbb{C}}
and any sequence
\varepsilon_{n}
satisfying

\log 1/\varepsilon_{n}=o(n)

there exists
b_{n}\longrightarrow 0
such that for all
n
big enough,
for any two points
u
,
v
in
K
, satisfying
|u-v|\geq\varepsilon_{n}
we have

\left|\frac{\widehat{f}_{n}(v)-\widehat{f}_{n}(u)}{v-u}-1\right|\leq b_{n}.

Proof.

Using the notation of
Lemma

58
consider again the function
\Phi:\lambda\in B(0,R_{n}/2)\mapsto\widehat{f}_{n}(\lambda,v)-\widehat{f}_{n}(\lambda,u)\in B^{\prime}=B(0,2R)-\{0\}
and recall that
R_{n}\geq n/M
.
We still have that the hyperbolic distance in
B(0,2R)-\{0\}
from
\Phi(0)=v-u
to
\Phi(1)=v_{n}-u_{n}
is at most the hyperbolic distance in
B(0,R_{n}/2)
from
0
to
1
, which is at most
\operatorname{cst}/n
.
Let
\Phi(0)=re^{i\theta}
and
\tau=\log(2R/r)>0
.
We now use the fact that the map
z\in{\mathbb{H}}_{+}\mapsto 2R\exp(i\theta-\tau z)\in B^{\prime}
preserves the respective infinitesimal hyperbolic metrics and sends
1
to
\Phi(0)
.
Consider the lift
\delta
of the path
t\in[0,1]\mapsto\Phi(t)
starting from
\delta(0)=1
.
We have

\Phi(1)/\Phi(0)=\exp(\tau\delta(0)-\tau\delta(1)).

The point
\delta(1)
is, in
{\mathbb{H}}_{+}
, at a hyperbolic distance from
\delta(0)=1
that is at most
\operatorname{cst}/n
.
So
|\delta(1)-\delta(0)|\leq\frac{\operatorname{cst}}{n}
for another constant, provided
n
is big enough (independently of
u
and
v
).
Multiplying by
\tau
(which depends on
u
and
v
), we get

\left|\log_{p}\frac{\Phi(1)}{\Phi(0)}\right|\leq|\tau\delta(1)-\tau\delta(0)|\leq\frac{\operatorname{cst}}{n}\tau.

Now

\frac{\tau}{n}=\frac{1}{n}\log\left|\frac{2R}{\Phi(0)}\right|\leq\frac{\operatorname{cst}+\log 1/\varepsilon_{n}}{n}\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

since
|\Phi(0)|\geq\varepsilon_{n}
and
\log 1/\varepsilon_{n}=o(n)
.
The claim follows.
∎

We assume that
n
is big enough so that
K
is contained in the array formed by the small squares, which we recall is the big square given by
|{\operatorname{Re}\,}z|\leq n
and
|{\operatorname{Im}\,}z|\leq n
.
To get some margin we in fact take
n
be big enough so that
K
is contained in
|{\operatorname{Re}\,}z|\leq n-1/n
and
|{\operatorname{Im}\,}z|\leq n-1/n
.

Consider now a set of four small squares around a square corner
c
. The Beltrami form
\mu_{n}
takes four values on these squares.
Consider the four quadrants defined by
\arg(z-c)
belonging to the intervals
(k\pi/2,(k+1)\pi/2)
,
k\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.
Consider the Beltrami form
\check{\mu}
that is constant on each of these quadrants and takes the same value
\check{\mu}_{k}
as the one of the four small squares with corner
c
that the quadrant contains (we omit
n
in these notations).
We know an explicit straightening of
\check{\mu}
, obtained as follows:
first apply

z\mapsto z-c,

then on each quadrant based on
0
, indexed by
k\in\{0,1,2,3\}
, consider the map

z\mapsto z+\check{\mu}_{k}\bar{z}.

Glue the four sectors thus obtained together along three of the four boundary pairs, using
{\mathbb{C}}
-affine maps.
For instance use the identity for
k=0
, then

z\mapsto\frac{i+\check{\mu}_{0}\bar{\imath}}{i+\check{\mu}_{1}\bar{\imath}}\,z

for
k=1
, then

z\mapsto\frac{-1-\check{\mu}_{1}}{-1-\check{\mu}_{2}}\cdot\frac{i+\check{\mu}_{0}\bar{\imath}}{i+\check{\mu}_{1}\bar{\imath}}\,z

for
k=2
and

z\mapsto\frac{-i-\check{\mu}_{2}\bar{\imath}}{-i-\check{\mu}_{3}\bar{\imath}}\cdot\frac{-1-\check{\mu}_{1}}{-1-\check{\mu}_{2}}\cdot\frac{i+\check{\mu}_{0}\bar{\imath}}{i+\check{\mu}_{1}\bar{\imath}}\,z

for
k=3
.
Note that each of these maps is close to the identity because the
\check{\mu}_{k}
are close to each other, as averages of the continuous function
\mu
on nearby small squares, and their modulus is never close to
1
since
\|\mu\|_{\infty}<1
.
We obtain a sector of angle close to
2\pi
(it may be bigger than
2\pi
). The sector is then closed appropriately as explained in
Section

2
by applying

z\mapsto z^{\alpha}=\exp(\alpha\log z)

for the branch of
\log z
whose imaginary part belongs to
[0,2\pi)
, where

\alpha=\frac{2\pi i}{2\pi i+\log\tau}

and

\tau=\frac{1+\check{\mu}_{3}}{1+\check{\mu}_{0}}\cdot\frac{-i-\check{\mu}_{2}\bar{\imath}}{-i-\check{\mu}_{3}\bar{\imath}}\cdot\frac{-1-\check{\mu}_{1}}{-1-\check{\mu}_{2}}\cdot\frac{i+\check{\mu}_{0}\bar{\imath}}{i+\check{\mu}_{1}\bar{\imath}}
, i.e.

\tau=\frac{1+\check{\mu}_{3}}{1-\check{\mu}_{3}}\cdot\frac{1-\check{\mu}_{2}}{1+\check{\mu}_{2}}\cdot\frac{1+\check{\mu}_{1}}{1-\check{\mu}_{1}}\cdot\frac{1-\check{\mu}_{0}}{1+\check{\mu}_{0}}

which is close to
1
,
and where the determination of
\log\tau
is the principal one.
In the proof of
we have in fact evaluated
\tau
(it corresponds to
\Lambda
in that proposition) and found that

\log\tau=\cal O(1/n^{2})
,
uniformly.
As a consequence,
\alpha
is close to
1
and

|\alpha-1|=\cal O(1/n^{2}),

uniformly.
Last we apply

z\mapsto az+b

where
a
and
b
will be chosen later.
Denote
\Phi_{n}
this composition of maps:

\Phi_{n}:F\to{\mathbb{C}}

where
F=\big\{{z\in{\mathbb{C}}}\,\big|\,{|{\operatorname{Re}\,}(z-c)<1/n,\ |{\operatorname{Im}\,}(z-c)|<1/n}\big\}
is the interior of the union of the four square.
The purpose of
\Phi_{n}
is to serve as a model of the map
f_{n}
near
c
.
Note that
\Phi_{n}
is quasiconformal, and is
{\mathbb{R}}
-differentiable inside each square.

Lemma 63
.

There exists
\eta_{n}\longrightarrow 0
such that for all
n
big enough, for all
c
as above, the differential of the corresponding
\Phi_{n}
on
\big\{{z\in F}\,\big|\,{|z-c|>r^{\prime}_{n}}\big\}
varies less than
\eta_{n}
, in the sense that for any two points
z
,
z^{\prime}
in this set, the corresponding differentials
L_{z}
and
L_{z^{\prime}}
satisfy
46
46

46

Where
\|\cdot\|
is
any operator norm on chosen in advance on the set of
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear endomorphisms of
{\mathbb{C}}
.

\|L_{z^{\prime}}\circ L_{z}^{-1}-\operatorname{Id}\|<\eta_{n}
.

Proof.

Of course the differential of
z\mapsto z-c
is the identity.
Each
z\mapsto z+\check{\mu}_{k}\bar{z}
is
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear and close to
z\mapsto z+\mu(c)\bar{z}
, because
|\check{\mu}_{k}-\mu(c)|<\operatorname{cst}/n
where
\operatorname{cst}
is independent of
c
and
n
.
The differential of
z\mapsto z^{\alpha}
is
\alpha z^{\alpha-1}dz
. Recall that
\alpha
is close to
1
and
z^{\alpha-1}=\exp((\alpha-1)\log z)
is close to
1
provided
(\alpha-1)\log|z|
is close to
0
. Since
(\alpha-1)=\cal O(1/n^{2})
uniformly, it is enough that
|z|>r_{n}=\exp(-n)
(we see that we could even have taken a much smaller
r_{n}
).
∎

Though we do not formally need it, it helps, for a clearer mental picture of the situation, to realize that, as we can see from the proof,
\Phi_{n}
is close to the similitude
z\mapsto a(z-c)+b
in some sense that we do not need to make explicit.

Figure 17.
This commutative diagram shows the objects involved in the end of the proof of
Proposition

60
. The model map
\Phi_{n}
is (voluntarily) only defined on the four small squares depicted in gray. Circled are the places where the derivative of
\check{f}_{n}
is not controlled, and the image of the places where the derivative of
f_{n}
and
\widehat{f}_{n}
is not controlled. These places are of size of order
r_{n}=\exp(-n)
, so in fact much smaller than the grid step
1/n
, so one should imagine the circles nearly invisible. Dashed is the image by
\Phi_{n}
of the gray square scaled about its centre by a factor of
0.75
.

It follows from
Lemma

63
that the differential of
\Phi_{n}^{-1}
also varies less than
\eta_{n}
on the image by
\Phi_{n}
of
\big\{{z\in F}\,\big|\,{|z-c|>r^{\prime}_{n}}\big\}
.
The image
\Phi_{n}(F)
of
\Phi_{n}
is close to be a parallelogram and the part that must be removed is contained in a very small disk near its centre, of size of order
r_{n}
times
\|L_{z}\|
, which is of the order of
|a|
where
a
is the constant yet to be chosen in the definition of
\Phi_{n}
, whereas the parallelogram has a diameter of order
|a|/n
, and bounded geometry (recall that
\|\mu\|_{\infty}<1
).
In the sequel we denote

F^{\prime}=\Phi_{n}(\big\{{z\in F}\,\big|\,{|z-c|>r_{n}}\big\}).

Let

\check{f}_{n}=f_{n}\circ\Phi_{n}^{-1}

so that

f_{n}=\check{f}_{n}\circ\Phi_{n}
.
Then
\check{f}_{n}
is an injective holomorphic map because it is a composition of quasiconformal maps that sends the circle ellipse field to itself.
Moreover,

\check{f}_{n}=\widehat{f}_{n}\circ(f\circ\Phi_{n}^{-1}).

In the definition of
\Phi_{n}
there was to choose constants
a
and
b
. We choose
b=f(c)
, so that
\Phi_{n}(c)=f(c)
. Consider the point
z^{*}=c+\frac{1+i}{2n}
, which is the centre of one of the four squares.
We choose
a
so that
\check{f}_{n}^{\prime}(\Phi_{n}(z^{*}))=1
.

Since
f
is
C^{1}
(actually
C^{2}
) and
F
has small diameter, the differential of
f
is nearly constant on
F
. It follows that the map
f\circ\Phi_{n}^{-1}
has a nearly constant differential on
F^{\prime}
.
In particular,
47
47

47

The set
F^{\prime}
is not convex but it is nearly a parallelogram with a removed hole that is nearly an ellipse.
pairs of points
u
,
v
in
F^{\prime}
have images
u^{\prime}
,
v^{\prime}
by
f\circ\Phi_{n}^{-1}
such that
(u^{\prime}-v^{\prime})/L(u-v)
is close to
1
, where
L
is some
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear map that depends on
n
and
c
but not on
u
and
v
.

We have also seen that
\widehat{f}_{n}
maps pairs of points
u,v\in K
at distance
>r_{n}
to pairs of points
u^{\prime}
,
v^{\prime}
whose associated vector is nearly the same:
(u^{\prime}-v^{\prime})/(u-v)
is close to
1
, uniformly.

It follows that pairs of points
u
,
v
in
F^{\prime}
with distance at least
|a|r_{n}/M^{\prime}
for some
M^{\prime}>0
independent of
n
and

, have images
u^{\prime}
,
v^{\prime}
by
\check{f}_{n}
such that
(u^{\prime}-v^{\prime})/L(u-v)
is close to
1
.
But recall that
\check{f}_{n}
is holomorphic and that its derivative at
\Phi_{n}(z^{*})
is equal to
1
. It follow by properties of univalent maps that
\check{f}_{n}
is close to the identity on the compact subset of
\Phi_{n}(F^{\prime\prime})
of
\Phi_{n}(F)
where
F^{\prime\prime}
is defined by the equations
|{\operatorname{Re}\,}z-c|<0.75/n
and

, and that

is close to

on that set.
It also follows that
L
is actually close to the identity.

Recall that
f\circ\Phi_{n}^{-1}
has a differential nearly constant and close to

on
F^{\prime}
. Hence
f\circ\Phi_{n}^{-1}
has actually a differential close to the identity on
F^{\prime}
.

Since,
\widehat{f}_{n}=\check{f}_{n}\circ(f\circ\Phi_{n}^{-1})^{-1}
we get
Proposition

60
by the chain rule.
